Reidemeister and movie moves for involutive links
Maciej Borodzik, Irving Dai, Abhishek Mallick, Matthew Stoffregen

Abstract
. An involutive link is a link which is invariant under the standard rotation by 180 degrees in S 3 S^{3} . We establish an equivariant analogue of the work of Carter and Saito aimed at studying equivariant cobordisms between involutive links. This gives a set of 39 39 equivariant movie moves that suffice to go between any two movie presentations of a pair of equivariantly isotopic cobordisms. Along the way, we give a singularity-theoretic proof of the equivariant Reidemeister theorem and study loops of equivariant Reidemeister moves. Our approach proceeds by analyzing codimension 2 2 singularities of equivariant maps from S 1 S^{1} to ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} , as well as utilizing embedded equivariant Morse theory.



[title=Index of Notation, columns=2]

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

Equivariant knots and links are well-studied
[
CK99
,
DN06
,
Mur71
,
Nai94
,
Sak86
]
and have appeared in a broad range of other topological contexts. Recent research in this area has utilized both classical techniques (see for example
[
BC25
,
DP23
,
DP24
,
Mer25
,
MP23
,
Zam25
]
) and gauge, symplectic and quantum invariants (see for example
[
BDMS25
,
DMS23
,
LS24
,
LW21
,
Mal24
,
San25
,
Wat17
]
).
Several of these papers have focused on questions involving equivariant surfaces and equivariant cobordisms.
Results in this direction have often been aimed at obstructing equivariant concordance or bounding quantities such as the equivariant slice genus from below.

In prior work
[
BDMS25
]
, the authors constructed a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant version of Khovanov homology for strongly invertible knots and showed that given an equivariant cobordism (with a specified equivariant movie decomposition), there exists a cobordism map between the equivariant invariants associated to its ends. This was used to provide equivariant slice genus bounds and establish non-existence results regarding equivariant surfaces. However, for more refined questions, additional functoriality results must be established. For example, to
distinguish
pairs of equivariant surfaces up to equivariant isotopy, we need to know that the homotopy type of our cobordism map is independent of all choices and preserved under equivariant isotopy. Such problems thus have both theoretical and practical interest.

A similar story has, of course, already played out in the non-equivariant setting. In
[
Kho00
]
, Khovanov showed that given a presentation of a surface cobordism, one can construct a corresponding cobordism map on Khovanov homology. This was subsequently shown to be well-defined and invariant under isotopies of link cobordisms in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]
: first by
Jacobsson
[
Jac04
]
(up to overall sign, also followed by
[
Kho02
,
BN05
]
), with improved statements in
[
Bla10
,
CMW09
]
and generalizations to
\mathfrak{sl}(N)

homologies in
[
ETW18
]
. Full functoriality in
S^{3}\times I
was established in
[
MWW22
]
and played a foundational role in Hayden and Sundberg’s work on exotic slice disks
[
HS21
]
and in defining skein
lasagna modules
[
MWW22
,
MWW23
]
; compare
[
Wed25
]
.
Establishing functoriality in this sense has thus been essential for applications of Khovanov homology to low-dimensional topology.

In the non-equivariant case, cobordism maps on Khovanov homology are constructed by first giving a presentation for the cobordism as a
movie
of Reidemeister moves and elementary cobordisms. We associate to each such piece a corresponding elementary cobordism map and define the overall cobordism map to be the composition of these. To establish well-definedness and/or isotopy invariance, one needs to have a comprehensive set of
movie moves
such that any two movies for the same isotopy class of cobordism are related by a sequence of these moves. This is a foundational result of Carter and Saito
[
CS93
]
, based on
[
Gil82
]
(see also
[
CRS97
,
Ros98
]
). One then checks that if two movies are related by a movie move, then their associated maps on Khovanov homology are unchanged (in this case, up to overall sign).

The aim of the present paper is to carry out the topological side of this program in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant setting. We concern ourselves with the general case of links which are invariant under the standard rotation by 180 degrees in
S^{3}
. Such links are called
involutive links
.
1
1

1

The notion of an involutive link encompasses several other studies of equivariant links in the literature. Many other papers place restrictions on how the involution interacts with various link components. (For example, in terms of preserving/reversing orientation, the number of fixed points on each component, and so on.) Our only restriction is that the involution on
S^{3}
is the standard rotation. In fact, it follows from the resolution of the Smith conjecture
[
Wal69
,
MB84
]
that any orientation-preserving involution on
S^{3}
with non-empty fixed-point set is conjugate to the standard rotation.
We first give a rigorous proof that any pair of diagrams for an involutive link are connected by a sequence of generalized equivariant Reidemeister moves, see Theorem
1.1
. This is comparable to the classical Reidemeister theorem in the non-equivariant category. Moreover, we show that an equivariant cobordism between two involutive links can be decomposed into an equivariant movie, see Theorem
1.2
. These claims were implicitly used in
[
BDMS25
]
to define equivariant Khovanov homology and construct equivariant cobordism maps.

The bulk of the paper is then concerned with comparing pairs of equivariant cobordisms. We give a list of equivariant elementary movie moves such that if two equivariant movies represent the same equivariant isotopy class of cobordism, then they are related by a sequence of these moves, see Theorem
1.4
. As a special case, we study loops of equivariant Reidemeister moves, see Theorem
1.3
. These results represent the first step towards proving full functoriality of the cobordism maps for equivariant Khovanov homology, see
[
BDMS25
]
.

Like
[
CS93
]
, our results rely heavily on the machinery of singularity theory. However, the details differ significantly. The approach of Carter and Saito, is via studying singularities of surfaces. We present a somewhat longer (but perhaps more intuitive) approach. For movies that do not change the isotopy type of the underlying link (such as for a sequence of Reidemeister moves), we use the classification of codimension 2 singularities of equivariant maps from the circle to the plane. In this approach, we follow
[
Arn94
,
Dav83
,
FK10
,
Wal08
]
, porting their results
to the equivariant setting via
[
Wal85
]
. For movies associated with a change of topology, we study equivariant embedded Morse theory,
alloying the results of
[
Was69
]
with
[
BP16
]
and using also
[
Aka07
,
BB25
]
for studying families of equivariant Morse functions.

1.2.
Main results

Our first result enumerates equivariant Reidemeister moves. View
S^{3}
as
\mathbb{R}^{3}
together with the point at
\infty
, and consider rotation about the
y
-axis. Our convention in this paper will be to fix the standard projection from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
onto the
xy
-plane. Under this projection, an involutive link (which we always assume to be in generic position) gives a
transvergent diagram
. Equivariantly isotopic link diagrams
are connected by a sequence of equivariant Reidemeister moves; see
[
LW21
,
San25
]
. We give a singularity theory based proof of
the following result.

Theorem 1.1

(see
[
LW21
,
San25
]
)
.

Let
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
be a pair of involutive links. An equivariant isotopy from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
induces, after perturbation, a sequence of the following moves:

•

Equivariant planar isotopies;

•

Off-axis Reidemeister moves (IR-1), (IR-2), (IR-3);

•

On-axis Reidemeister moves (R-1), (R-2);

•

On-axis mixed moves (M-1), (M-2), (M-3);

•

The non-local I-move of Figure
6.6
;

connecting their diagrams. See Figure
6.4
. If the isotopy is in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the I-move is not needed.

The next result deals with cobordisms. Fix the standard product involution on
S^{3}\times[0,1]
given by rotation about the
y
-axis in every
S^{3}
-slice.
The following result is a simplified version of Theorem
8.31
, proved in Section
8.4
.

Theorem 1.2

(see Theorem
8.31
)
.

Let
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
be a pair of involutive links. Suppose
\Sigma\subset S^{3}\times[0,1]
is an equivariant cobordism from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
such that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
on
\Sigma
has no isolated fixed points. Then
\Sigma
is equivariantly isotopic rel boundary to an equivariant cobordism
\Sigma_{0}
, where
\Sigma_{0}
is built by stacking elementary equivariant cobordisms of the forms:

•

Equivariant isotopies and equivariant Reidemeister moves as in Theorem
1.1
;

•

Births, saddles and deaths off-axis, see Figure
8.1
;

•

Births, saddles and deaths on-axis, see Figure
8.2
.

We refer to the last two items of the above list as
equivariant Morse moves
.
The assumption on the lack of isolated fixed points is satisfied, for instance, if the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action inverts the orientation of every connected component
of the cobordism. (As an example, this is the case for a connected cobordism between a pair of strongly invertible knots.)

Next, we discuss how two sequences of Reidemeister moves may be compared. In Section
7
, we give
18
loops of equivariant Reidemeister moves. An
elementary loop replacement
consists of replacing a contiguous fragment of one of these loops by its complementary fragment, going in the opposite direction. These loops are summarized in Table
2
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
be a pair of involutive links. Suppose we have two equivariant isotopies from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
and let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be corresponding sequences of equivariant Reidemeister moves afforded by Theorem
1.1
. If our equivariant isotopies can be connected by an equivariant isotopy-of-isotopies, then
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
can be transformed into each other by a sequence of the following:

•

Changing the order of Reidemeister moves performed at different places;

•

Inserting/deleting a pair of mutually inverse Reidemeister moves;

•

One of 18 elementary loop replacement moves from Table
2
.

Finally, we describe the elementary movie moves for equivariant cobordisms.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
be a pair of involutive links. Suppose
\Phi_{s}\colon\Sigma\to S^{3}\times[0,1]
,
s\in[0,1]
is a family of equivariant cobordisms from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
and let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be movies for
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
afforded by Theorem
1.2
. Then
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
can be transformed into each other by elementary movie moves associated with the following:

•

Commuting two moves occurring at different places, e.g. an equivariant Reidemeister move and an equivariant Morse move as in Subsection
9.2
;

•

A loop of Reidemeister moves of Theorem
1.3
;

•

A loop of Reidemeister moves around the point at infinity, see Subsection
9.3
;

•

A loop of equivariant Morse moves around the point at infinity as in Subsection
9.4
;

•

A loop of equivariant Morse moves around the point on the axis as in Subsection
9.5
;

•

A loop related to a singular equivariant Morse move as in Subsection
9.6
;

•

A loop related to an equivariant Morse move over another point in the diagram as in Subsection
9.7
;

•

A loop of equivariant Morse moves related to a failure of the Morse condition, see Subsection
9.8
.

Theorem
1.4
is proved as Theorem
9.9
in Section
9
, where technical details
regarding Theorem
1.4
are also explained. In particular,
we describe
in Subsection
9.10
the relation between loops of moves of Theorem
1.4
and elementary movie moves.

1.3.
Main methods

We now discuss some of the central methods used in the present work.

1.3.1.
Stratification induced by singularities

A large portion of this paper – especially our results concerning Reidemeister moves – is based on understanding the space
\mathcal{F}
of smooth equivariant maps from a disjoint union of circles
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The idea will be to stratify
\mathcal{F}
and investigate the behavior of families of maps as strata are crossed. As a starting point, if we require
that the image is a link diagram (no cusps, and no tangencies, no triple points), then we obtain the space
\mathcal{F}^{0}\subset\mathcal{F}
of
regular
maps, which
is open-dense. Thus, a generic map can be assumed to be regular.

However, a one-parameter family of maps will not usually stay inside
\mathcal{F}^{0}
, but will instead cross its complement

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}=\mathcal{F}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{0}.

We thus find a subset
\mathcal{F}^{1}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
whose complement in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
has codimension
2
. Any one-parameter family of maps can then be perturbed so that all intersections with
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}}
occur at points of
\mathcal{F}^{1}
. The space
\mathcal{F}^{1}
will be constructed as the union of strata
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
, each of which describes the failure of one of the regularity conditions, but which limits the failure to be as mild as possible. For instance, we might consider the space of maps with triple points, but these triple points are required to be ordinary, and no quadruple points are allowed.

Importantly, this will allow us to write down a model singularity from each
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
, called a
normal form
. In fact, in each case we will be able to write down a model one-dimensional deformation of such a singularity, called a
versal deformation
. These versal deformations will allow us to understand how our one-parameter family crosses
\mathcal{F}^{1}
. The qualitative change in behavior as each
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
is crossed gives rise to the equivariant Reidemeister moves.

A similar story holds for two-parameter families. While a one-parameter family will generically stay inside
\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}
, a two-parameter family of maps into
\mathcal{F}
will usually cross

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{F}\setminus(\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}).

We thus find a subset
\mathcal{F}^{2}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
whose complement in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
has codimension
3
. Any one-parameter family of maps can then be perturbed so that all intersections with
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}
occur at points of
\mathcal{F}^{2}
. As before,
\mathcal{F}^{2}
will be constructed as the union of strata
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{i}
, each describing a particular singularity which is (roughly speaking) the next level of severity as compared to the singularities in
\mathcal{F}^{1}
.

Once again, we write down a normal form and versal deformation for each
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{i}
. In this case, the versal deformation will be two-dimensional, as it describes how a generic two-dimensional family intersects the codimension
2
subset
\mathcal{F}^{2}
. We think of the parameter space of the versal deformation as a disk, where the center corresponds to a singularity in some
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{i}
. Generally, our deformation will continue to exhibit milder singularities along arcs radiating outwards from the center of the disk, corresponding to singularities of the various
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
. (The set of parameters along which a deformation is singular is called the
discriminant locus
; see Section
4.2
.) Going around the boundary of the disk gives a loop of equivariant Reidemeister moves. These are precisely the loops appearing in the statement of Theorem
1.3
.

1.3.2.
Equivariant Morse theory

The second tool that we use – especially in the context of analyzing equivariant cobordisms – is embedded equivariant Morse theory. We briefly discuss some of the more subtle points which arise in this context.

For the moment, let us consider the classical case of non-equivariant links and cobordisms. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]
be a cobordism and consider the height function
f\colon\Sigma\to[0,1]
given by projection onto the second factor of
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]
. If
\Sigma
is in general position,
then
f
is Morse. As it is well-known, a critical point of
f
induces a change of the topology of the link. It is usually accepted that an index zero critical point of
f
corresponds to adding a round circle to the diagram of the link, while an index one critical point results in performing a standard saddle move. Finally, an index two critical point of
f
destroys a circle.

However, it turns out that there is a subtlety in this discussion which arises especially in the study of families of cobordisms.
To explain this, let
z\in\Sigma
be a critical point of
f
. The embedded Morse Lemma (see e.g.
[
BP16
, Lemma 2.17]
) provides us with local coordinates
(u,v)
on
\Sigma
near
z
, such that
f
is quadratic in
(u,v)
and
z
is identified with
(0,0)
. Let
(x,y)
be coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The family of diagrams of links as
t
crosses the singular value is studied using the map
\pi\colon(u,v)\mapsto(x,y)
obtained by composing the
embedding
\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]

with the projection
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
If this
has non-vanishing derivative at
(0,0)
(that is, if it is a local diffeomorphism) and if the inverse image consists of the singular point only, then the discussion in the previous paragraph holds. That is, the change of a link diagram after crossing an index zero critical point is adding a round circle, and so on, see Definition
8.27
.

If we study a one-parameter family of embeddings of
\Sigma
into
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]
, however, then degenerate situations will usually occur at certain parameter values. That is, for certain embeddings in this family, we may have that
\det D\pi(0,0)=0
, or that
\pi^{-1}(0,0)
consists of more than just one point. These situations lead to movie moves involving Morse handles. The equivariant setting is even more challenging. While the degeneracy of
D\pi
can be handled in a similar manner as in the non-equivariant setting, the condition that
\pi
be one-to-one on its image near
z
is significantly more involved in the presence of the

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action; see Example
8.33
. In a one-parameter family, the equivariant Morse moves can happen on the diagram, leading to several types of movie moves.

1.4.
Structure of the article

For the first few sections, we set up the general language we use for our equivariant application. In Section
2
, we explain the classical theory of Reidemeister moves in the language of singularity theory. We introduce
jet spaces, state the Thom transversality theorem, and prove the usual Reidemeister theorem. We define regularity for diagrams and paths of diagrams. This section serves as an introduction to the techniques of the paper. In Section
3
, we start generalizing these notions to the equivariant setting. We recall an equivariant version of the jet transversality theorem
due to Wall
[
Wal85
]
and define regularity for involutive links. In Section
4
, we make a small detour: before we proceed to a classification
of codimension 1 and 2 singularities of equivariant maps, we need to recall several notions from singularity theory. We define RL equivalence and provide a definition of versality and infinitesimal versality.

We begin our work in earnest in Section
5
, where we provide normal forms and versal deformations for several classes of equivariant singularities. We introduce the notion of a strikethrough, which is when a relatively non-complicated singularity is met by an extra line passing through it. Many codimension 2 singularities arise as strikethroughs of codimension 1 singularities, so a general way of handling these cases makes the description more concise.
We also discuss equivariant cusps and tangencies. As we will see, these must be subdivided into several cases depending on their behavior with respect to the axis of symmetry.

We use our work in Section
5
to determine the codimension
1
and codimension
2
equivariant singularities and prove Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
. Theorem
1.1
is established in Section
6
, where we study one-parameter families of equivariant diagrams.
We first prove an equivariant Reidemeister theorem for diagrams in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and then discuss the question of diagrams in
S^{3}
, where the extra I-move
is needed. We introduce another non-local move, called an S-move, consisting of moving a double point over a point at infinity. Unlike the I-move, the S-move can be deduced from other Reidemeister moves. However, it allows for a more concise description of loops of Reidemeister moves
in later sections.

In Section
6
, we also discuss
the possibility of eliminating one of the two R-1 moves in the case of a strongly invertible knot, which is useful
for discussing pointed link diagrams. Theorem
1.3
is established in Section
7
, where we study codimension 2 singularities and their bifurcations. We classify all codimension 2 singularities and draw bifurcation diagrams for each.

In Section
8
, we study equivariant cobordisms. We develop the theory of equivariant embedded Morse functions. We prove density as
well as a variant of the embedded equivariant Morse lemma (Theorem
8.15
). We use this to prove Theorem
1.2
. Finally, in Section
9
, we study movie moves for equivariant cobordism and prove Theorem
1.4
.

As a last remark: in Section
4
we state the versality theorem (Theorem
4.16
) and note that the equivariant variant of the theorem has an analogous proof to the classical one. This relies on an equivariant version of Malgrange Preparation Theorem. The generalization is well-known to experts, but for completeness we include a quick argument in the appendix.

2.
Classical Reidemeister theory

In this section, we briefly review jet spaces and state several related transversality theorems. We then provide a warm-up for the rest of the paper by using jet spaces to prove the usual (non-equivariant) Reidemeister theorem. This will serve as a foundation for the introduction of equivariant jet spaces and involutive links in the sequel.

2.1.
Jet spaces

We begin by defining jet spaces and jet extensions; see
[
GG73
, Chapter 2]
and
[
Arn88
, Section 29]
. We start with the Euclidean case. Let
V
and
W
be two finite-dimensional vector spaces. Let
\mathcal{E}(V)
be the ring of
\mathbb{R}
-valued germs at
0\in V
and let
\mathfrak{m}(V)
be its maximal ideal; this consists of germs vanishing at
0
. Write
\mathcal{E}(V,W)
for the
\mathcal{E}(V)
-module of germs of smooth functions from
V
to
W
taking
0\in V
to
0\in W
. Then the classical space of
k
-jets taking
0
to
0
is defined to be

\mathfrak{J}^{k}(V,W)=\mathcal{E}(V,W)/\mathfrak{m}(V)^{k+1}\mathcal{E}(V,W).

Two germs in
\mathfrak{J}^{k}(V,W)
are the same if all of their partial derivatives up to order
k
coincide.

Now let
X
and
Y
be smooth manifolds and fix
x\in X
and
y\in Y
. Choose Euclidean neighborhoods
V
and
W
of
x
and
y
in which
x
and
y
are taken to the origin. The space of
k
-jets taking
x
to
y
is defined to be

\mathfrak{J}^{k}(x,y)=\mathfrak{J}^{k}(V,W).

We denote a typical element of
\mathfrak{J}^{k}(x,y)
by
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x)
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
k
-jet space

\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)
is the union of
\mathfrak{J}^{k}(x,y)
over all
(x,y)\in X\times Y
. This can canonically be given the structure of a smooth bundle over
X\times Y
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
. The
k
-jet extension
of
f
is the map

\mathfrak{j}^{k}f\colon X\to\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)

assigning to each
x\in X
the
k
-jet
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x)
obtained by restricting
f
to a germ from
x
to
f(x)
.

Roughly speaking,
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x)
records the partial derivatives of
f
at
x
up to order
k
. Clearly,
\mathfrak{J}^{0}(X,Y)=X\times Y
, while
\mathfrak{J}^{1}(X,Y)
is the bundle over
X\times Y
with fiber at
(x,y)
given by
\operatorname{Hom}(T_{x}X,T_{y}Y)
.
Note that we have two maps

\mathfrak{a}\colon\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)\to X\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{b}\colon\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)\to Y.

The first is given by sending a
k
-jet
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x)
to
x
and is referred to as the
source map
. The second is given by sending a
k
-jet
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x)
to
f(x)
and is referred to as the
target map
.

Example 2.3
.

Suppose
X=Y=\mathbb{R}
. Then
\mathfrak{J}^{1}(X,Y)=\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and the
1
-jet extension of a map
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the map
\mathfrak{j}^{1}f(x)=(x,f(x),f^{\prime}(x))
.

Jets often arise in connection with degeneracy conditions on maps. Our general strategy will be to express various degeneracy conditions in terms of hitting some subspace of an appropriately defined jet space. If these subspaces have sufficiently large codimension, one can argue that such degeneracies are avoided by a generic map.

Theorem 2.4

(Thom Transversality Theorem, see
[
GG73
, Theorem II.4.9]
)
.

Let
W\subset\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)
be a submanifold. Then the set of maps
f\colon X\to Y
such that
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f\colon X\to\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)

is transverse to
W
is residual in
C^{\infty}(X,Y)
.

In order to restrict the behavior of a function at multiple points simultaneously, we introduce the notion of a multijet space. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
X^{(s)}
be the set of ordered
s
-tuples
of pairwise distinct points in
X
:

X^{(s)}=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{s})\in X^{s}\colon x_{i}\neq x_{j}\textrm{ unless }i=j\}.

For each
k
, let
\mathfrak{a}^{s}
and
\mathfrak{b}^{s}
be the
s
-fold Cartesian powers

\mathfrak{a}^{s}\colon\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)^{s}\to X^{s}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{b}^{s}\colon\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)^{s}\to Y^{s}

of the source and target maps.

Definition 2.5
.

The
s
-fold multijet space

\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
is the preimage of
X^{(s)}
under
\mathfrak{a}^{s}
.

The
s
-fold multijet space consists of
s
-tuples of jets that live over
s
distinct points in
X
. Note that
\mathfrak{J}^{k}(X,Y)
is
\mathfrak{J}^{k,1}(X,Y)
. We likewise have the notion of a multijet extension.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
. The
s
-fold multijet extension
of
f
is the map

\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f\colon X^{(s)}\to\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)

that assigns to each
(x_{1},\dots,x_{s})\in X^{(s)}
the multijet

\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{s})=(\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{j}^{k}f(x_{s})).

The following result generalizes the Thom transversality theorem:

Theorem 2.7

(Multijet Transversality Theorem, see
[
GG73
, Theorem II.4.13]
)
.

Let
W\subset\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
be a submanifold. Then the set of maps
f\colon X\to Y

such that
\mathfrak{j}^{k}f\colon X^{(s)}\to\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
is transverse to
W
is residual in
C^{\infty}(X,Y)
.

In the above situation, we will sometimes say
f
is transverse to
W
to mean that the appropriate jet extension
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f
is transverse to
W
.
It is a standard procedure to translate this into a ready-to-use parameter counting argument. The idea is as follows. Suppose we have an
r
-dimensional family of maps from
X
to
Y
. For any map
f
in this family, the associated multijet extension
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f\colon X^{(s)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
has domain of dimension
sn
, where
n
is the dimension of
X
. As
f
varies, these multijet extensions thus sweep out a locus of dimension
sn+r
in
\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
. Generically, this will not intersect a submanifold of
\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
with codimension greater than
sn+r
. We make this precise below:

Theorem 2.8

(Parameter Counting)
.

Let
R
be an
r
-dimensional manifold, which we think of as a parameter space, and
\dim X=n
. Let
W\subset\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
be a submanifold of
codimension
w
. If
sn+r<w
, then there is a residual subset of
C^{\infty}(R\times X,Y)
such
that for any
F
in this subset and any
u\in R
, the jet extension

\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f_{u}
of
f_{u}=F(u,\cdot)\colon X\to Y
misses
W
.

Proof.

The result is classical, see e.g.
[
FK10
, Section 3]
, but we provide a quick proof which will generalize to the equivariant setting. The only subtlety in the above discussion is that (strictly speaking) we should phrase our argument in terms of the multijet transversality theorem for maps from
R\times X
to
Y
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be the preimage of the diagonal in
R^{s}
under

\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(R\times X,Y)\to(R\times X)^{s}\to R^{s},

where the first map is the source map and the second is projection. Let
\pi_{X}\colon R\times X\to X
be projection onto
X
. This induces a projection
\Pi_{X}\colon\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(R\times X,Y)\to\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
which is a locally trivial fibration. Set
W^{\prime}=\Pi_{X}^{-1}(W)\cap\Delta^{\prime}
. By the multijet transversality theorem, the set of maps
F\colon R\times X\to Y
which are transverse to
W^{\prime}
is residual.

Now,
\operatorname{codim}W
is
w
, and hence
\operatorname{codim}\Pi_{X}^{-1}(W)
is also
w
, since
\Pi_{X}
is a fibration. Likewise,
\operatorname{codim}\Delta^{\prime}

is
r(s-1)
. Thus
\operatorname{codim}W^{\prime}
is
r(s-1)+w
. On the other hand, the dimension of
R\times X
is
r+n
, and hence the dimension of
(R\times X)^{s}
is
s(r+n)
. Thus, if

s(r+n)<r(s-1)+w,

the mutlijet transversality theorem provides a residual subset of functions
F
in
C^{\infty}(R\times X,Y)
such that the multijet extension
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}F
misses
W^{\prime}
. This inequality is of course identical to the one in the hypothesis of the theorem. It remains to show that
F
missing
W^{\prime}
implies that
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f_{u}
misses
W
for all
u\in R
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f_{u}
hits
W
. This means that there exist points
x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\in X
such that

(\mathfrak{j}^{k}f_{u}(x_{1}),\mathfrak{j}^{k}f_{u}(x_{2}),\dots,\mathfrak{j}^{k}f_{u}(x_{s}))\in W.

Then

(\mathfrak{j}^{k}F(u,x_{1}),\mathfrak{j}^{k}F(u,x_{2}),\dots,\mathfrak{j}^{k}F(u,x_{s}))\in\Pi_{X}^{-1}(W)\subset\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(R\times X,Y).

However, the point
(\mathfrak{j}^{k}F(u,x_{1}),\mathfrak{j}^{k}F(u,x_{2}),\dots,\mathfrak{j}^{k}F(u,x_{s}))
also belongs to
\Delta^{\prime}
. Hence,
if
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}f_{u}
hits
W
, then
\mathfrak{j}^{k,s}F
hits
W^{\prime}
.
∎

Our usage of the parameter counting argument is so common that it will be useful to introduce some auxiliary notation:

Definition 2.9
.

Let
D
be a submanifold of
\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
. The
(jet space) codimension of
D
(sometimes called the
number of defining conditions
) is the usual codimension
\operatorname{codim}(D)
of
D
in
\mathfrak{J}^{k,s}(X,Y)
. We define the
(parametric) codimension of
D
to be

\operatorname{pcodim}(D)=\operatorname{codim}(D)-s(\dim X).

Note that
s(\dim X)
is the dimension of the domain of the relevant multijet extension. In this language, the parameter counting argument states that a generic
r
-parameter family of maps in
C^{\infty}(X,Y)
will miss
D
so long as
r<\operatorname{pcodim}(D)
.

Example 2.10
.

Consider the case of an
s
-tuple self-intersection singularity. The presence of an
s
-tuple self-intersection of
f
means that
\mathfrak{j}^{0,s}f
hits
D\subset\mathfrak{J}^{0,s}(X,Y)
, where
D
is the preimage of the diagonal in
Y^{s}
under the target map. This has
\operatorname{codim}D=(s-1)(\dim Y)
, so

\operatorname{pcodim}(D)=(s-1)(\dim Y)-s(\dim X).

For example, suppose
f
is a link diagram (as we discuss presently), so that
X
is a circle or disjoint union of circles and
Y
is
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then
\operatorname{pcodim}(D)=s-2
, so an
s
-tuple self-intersection does not occur in a generic
r
-parameter family of diagrams unless
r
is at least
s-2
.

2.2.
Regular diagrams

We now illustrate the theory by using jet spaces to establish standard facts regarding link diagrams and Reidemeister moves. We begin with the former.

Definition 2.11
.

Fix a disjoint union of circles
\mathcal{S}
. A
link
is an embedding

\widetilde{\phi}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Let
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the fixed projection from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
onto the
xy
-plane. We refer to

\phi\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\quad\text{given by}\quad\phi=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}

as the corresponding
link diagram
. In Subsection
6.6
, we will pass to links embedded in
S^{3}
by viewing
S^{3}
as the one-point compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Our convention will be that maps or objects in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
or
S^{3}
are decorated with a tilde, while maps or objects in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
are not. In general, a map
\phi\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
might have arbitrarily complicated singularities. Our goal will to show that we can always perturb
\phi
to have singularities which are particularly simple. The argument and notation here will be generalized to the equivariant setting in subsequent sections.

Definition 2.12
.

For a fixed
\mathcal{S}
, let
\mathcal{F}
be the space of all smooth maps from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The following describes a generic element
\phi\in\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{0}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{F}
consisting of maps whose only singularities are ordinary double points. Thus,

\phi
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{0}
if it has the following three properties:

(Reg-1)

\phi^{\prime}
is nonzero at every point of
\mathcal{S}
;

(Reg-2)

for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, if
\phi^{-1}(w)
consists of two points
t_{1},t_{2}
, then
\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})
,
\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})
are linearly independent;

(Reg-3)

for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the preimage
\phi^{-1}(w)
consists of at most two points.

Conditions
(Reg-1)
,
(Reg-2)
, and
(Reg-3)
can be phrased as:
\phi
has no cusps,

\phi
has no tangencies,
and
\phi
has no triple points. We refer to such a
\phi
as being
regular
.

This means there are three (non-exclusive) ways that
\phi
can fail to be regular. Hence the complement of
\mathcal{F}^{0}
decomposes into three strata:

Definition 2.14
.

For each
i
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}
be the subset of
\mathcal{F}
on which (Reg-
i
) is violated. Thus:

(
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{1}
)

(Reg-1)
is violated:
\phi
has a cusp; that is, there is at least one point where
\phi^{\prime}(t)=0
;

(
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{2}
)

(Reg-2)
is violated:
\phi
has a tangency; that is, there is at least one pair of points
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
such that
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})
and
\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})
,
\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})
are linearly dependent;

(
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{3}
)

(Reg-3)
is violated:
\phi
has a triple point; that is, there is at least one
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
for which
\phi^{-1}(w)
consists of three or more points.

Denote

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{1}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{2}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{3}=\mathcal{F}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{0}.

By examining these three strata, we obtain the following standard result:

Theorem 2.15

(see
[
Whi37
]
)
.

The subset
\mathcal{F}^{0}
of regular maps is open-dense in
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for each
i
, the set of maps missing each
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}
is open-dense. By definition,
\mathcal{F}^{0}
will then be the intersection of three such sets. We proceed via multijet transversality.

A map
\phi
lies in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{1}
if
\mathfrak{j}^{1}\phi
intersects the subspace of
\mathfrak{J}^{1}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined by
\phi^{\prime}(t)=0
. This is a codimension 2 subset of jet space (the vanishing of both components of the derivative) while the source is of dimension 1; Theorem
2.7
thus shows that the set of maps missing this subspace is residual. Because having
\phi^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
is clearly an open condition, in this case we have that the complement of
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{1}}
is open-dense.

A map
\phi
lies in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{2}
if
\mathfrak{j}^{1,2}\phi
intersects the subspace of
\mathfrak{J}^{1,2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined by the conditions
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})
and
\det(\phi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\phi^{\prime}(t_{2}))=0
. This is a codimension 3 subset of jet space (the equality
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})
gives two coordinate constraints, and the linear dependence gives a third) while the source is of dimension 2; Theorem
2.7
thus shows that the set of maps missing this subspace is residual. Once again we may directly check that the complement of
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{2}}
is open and thus that it is open-dense.

Finally, a map
\phi
lies in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{3}
if
\mathfrak{j}^{0,3}\phi
intersects the subspace of
\mathfrak{J}^{0,3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined by
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})=\phi(t_{3})
. This is a codimension 4 subset of jet space (there are four independent coordinate equalities) while the source is of dimension 3. Once again, the complement of
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{3}}
is open-dense.
∎

We quickly obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 2.16
.

Any link
\widetilde{\phi}
can be perturbed so that
\phi
is in
\mathcal{F}^{0}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
be a sequence converging to
\phi
in the
C^{\infty}
-topology and

\widetilde{\phi}_{n}=\widetilde{\phi}+\phi_{n}-\phi
. Here, we view the codomain of
\phi_{n}-\phi
as
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by setting the
z
-coordinate equal to zero. We have
\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{n}=\phi_{n}

and
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}\to\widetilde{\phi}
in
C^{\infty}
. In particular, the convergence is also in the
C^{1}
-norm.
It follows from stability of embeddings that
\widetilde{\phi}_{n}
is an
embedding isotopic to
\widetilde{\phi}
.
∎

2.3.
Reidemeister moves

We complete our discussion of classical knot theory by sketching a proof of the Reidemeister theorem using jet spaces. For this, we will need to understand one-parameter families of maps in
\mathcal{F}
. While a generic element of
\mathcal{F}
lies in
\mathcal{F}^{0}
, a one-parameter family of maps in
\mathcal{F}
will usually cross the strata of Definition
2.14
. We thus begin by identifying a subset
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
of each stratum consisting of the mildest possible singularities within that stratum. Our treatment is brief, since we give a detailed discussion of the equivariant case in subsequent sections.

Definition 2.17
.

For each
i
, define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}
as follows.

(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1})

\phi
has a cusp of the lowest possible order; i.e., a
(2,3)
-cusp (see Definition
5.13
). This means that at the cusp point,
\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)
and
\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
are linearly independent.

(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{2})

\phi
has a tangency of the lowest possible order; i.e., a
1
-fold tangency (see Definition
5.28
). This means that at the point of tangency, we can find coordinates around
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
, and
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})
such that the two branches of
\phi
are given by

\phi(t)=(t,0)\quad\text{and}\quad\phi(s)=(s,h(s)),

for some
h
with
h(0)=h^{\prime}(0)=0
and
h^{\prime\prime}(0)\neq 0
.

(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{3})

\phi
has an ordinary triple point. This means that
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})=\phi(t_{3})
for some
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
, and
t_{3}
, but
\{\phi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\phi^{\prime}(t_{2}),\phi^{\prime}(t_{3})\}
are pairwise linearly independent.

In each case, we furthermore require
\phi
has no coincidences (see Section
5.1
) or strikethroughs (see Section
5.2
). The former means that each
\phi
has exactly one singularity; for example, we do not allow the simultaneous appearance of two cusps or the appearance of a cusp and a tangency. The latter means that we do not allow the participation of any other branch of
\phi
in one of the singularities defined above; for example, we do not allow a triple point to become a quadruple point. Denote

\mathcal{F}^{1}=\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}_{2}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}_{3}.

We claim that a generic one-parameter family of maps may cross
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}}
, but only does so in a controlled manner, by staying within
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
. For instance, when
i=1
, Definition
2.17
says that a one-parameter family may develop a cusp, but this cusp will be of the lowest possible order. Or, when
i=3
, Definition
2.17
says that such a family may develop a triple point, but this triple point will occur between three mutually transverse branches.

Definition 2.18
.

A path
\phi_{s}
for
s\in[0,1]
in
\mathcal{F}
is called
regular
if:

(PR-1)

\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
are regular maps;

(PR-2)

\phi_{s}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}
for all
s
;

(PR-3)

there are finitely many values
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}
such that
\phi_{s_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}^{1}
, while for other
values
\phi_{s}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
;

(PR-4)

regarded as a path in
\mathcal{F}
,
\phi_{s}
crosses
\mathcal{F}^{1}
transversely.

To show that a generic path stays within
\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}
, we consider its complement:

Definition 2.19
.

For each
i
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{i}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
. Denote

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}^{2}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}^{2}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{3}^{2}=\mathcal{F}\setminus(\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}).

As in the proof of Theorem
2.15
, we proceed by showing:

Theorem 2.20

(see
[
Arn94
]
)
.

Let
\phi_{s}
be any path in
\mathcal{F}
with
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
. Then there exists an arbitrarily close path
\phi_{s}^{\prime}
,
equal to
\phi_{s}
at the ends, which is regular.

Sketch of proof.

We prove
(PR-2)
by showing that each
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{2}
is the union of strata with
\operatorname{pcodim}
at least
2
and is thus missed by a generic path. Our discussion is brief, as we describe the codimension
2
strata in greater detail in Section
7
.

There are two ways for
\phi
to be in
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{2}}
. The first (and less interesting) way is for
\phi
to develop a coincidence or strikethough. A coincidence means
\phi
violates two or more of the conditions
(\textrm{Reg}
-
i)
at the same time, or the same condition twice. For instance,
\phi
could have two cusps, which would correspond to the jet extension hitting a codimension 4 subspace of
\mathfrak{J}^{1,2}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
(two equations for each cusp). As the domain in this case has dimension 2, such a subspace has
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
. Other coincidences of singularities are dealt with similarly. In the case of a strikethrough, for each new branch we add at least two equations (one equation for each coordinate of the branch) and increase the dimension of the domain by one. Hence all coincidences or strikethroughs have
\operatorname{pcodim}
at least
2
.

The other way to be in
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{2}}
is to fail one of the specific conditions laid out in Definition
2.17
.

•

For
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}^{2}}
,
\phi
must have a higher-order cusp. That is, at the cusp point we have that
\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)
and
\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
are linearly dependent.

•

For
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}^{2}}
,
\phi
must have a degenerate or higher-order tangency. That is, at the point of tangency, we either have that one of the branches is actually a cusp, or the two branches are tangent to higher order.

•

For
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{3}^{2}}
,
\phi
must have a degenerate triple point. That is, at the triple point, we either have that one (or more) of the branches is actually a cusp, or two (or more) branches are tangent.

In Section
6
, we verify that the above subsets are unions of strata with
\operatorname{pcodim}
at least
2
. For example,
\phi
has a higher-order cusp if and only if its jet extension hits the subspace of
\mathfrak{J}^{3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
cut out by three equations:
\phi^{\prime}(t)=0
, together with the linear dependence of
\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)
and
\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)
. Since the domain has dimension
1
, this subspace has
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
. It follows that none of these singularities occur in a generic 1-parameter family of maps. This proves that a residual set of maps from
[0,1]
to
\mathcal{F}

stays in
\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}
, establishing item
(PR-2)
.

The other conditions are straightforward. Item
(PR-1)
is contained in the assumptions of the theorem. We thus deal with
(PR-3)
and
(PR-4)
. By the transversality theorem, a residual set of maps
from can be made transverse
to the strata defining
\mathcal{F}^{1}
. For such a map
\phi\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{F}
, the number of parameter values
s
for which
\phi(s)\in\mathcal{F}^{1}

is finite, and the intersection is transverse.
∎

Cusp:
Tangency:
Triple point:

Figure 2.1.
Perestroikas of critical points.

Theorem
2.20
implies the classical Reidemeister theorem. The essential idea is as follows: for each
i
, the singularities described by
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
are sufficiently constrained so that we have a local model for any
\phi
lying in
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
. Such a model is called a
normal form
for the corresponding singularity; see Section
4.1
. In fact, we moreover have a local model for any one-parameter family of maps transversely crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
. This uses the notion of a
versal deformation
; see Section
4.2
. Versal deformations allow us to construct qualitative before-and-after pictures for the maps in such a family.

For instance, up to a choice of coordinates, a
(2,3)
-cusp is locally given by
\phi(t)=(t^{2},t^{3})
. A model one-parameter family of maps passing through this cusp is given by
\phi_{\varepsilon}(t)=(t^{2},t^{3}+\varepsilon t)
. This family is universal in an appropriate sense discussed in Section
4.2
, and hence describes the behavior of a general one-parameter family transversely crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1}
. The before-and-after pictures of this family for
\varepsilon<0
and
\varepsilon>0
represent the usual R1 Reidemeister move.

A formal summary of this argument is given below:

Corollary 2.21
.

Any two diagrams of the same knot can be connected by a sequence of isotopies and finitely many Reidemeister moves.

Sketch of proof.

A path
\widetilde{\phi}_{s}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
s\in[0,1]
induces a path
\phi_{s}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

via
\phi_{s}=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
. We perturb
\phi_{s}
to a regular path
\phi_{s,n}

in such a way that
\phi_{s,n}
agrees with
\phi_{s}
for
s=0,1
.
The perturbation
is small in
C^{\infty}
topology, in particular, we may assume that
||\phi_{s,n}-\phi_{s}||_{C^{1}}\leq\frac{1}{n}
for all
s
.
By
(PR-3)
,
\phi_{s,n}
intersects

\mathcal{F}^{1}
at finitely many points. We study how
\phi_{s,n}
changes when
s
crosses such a parameter.

Suppose for
s_{0}\in[0,1]
,
\phi_{s_{0},n}\in\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,3
, and suppose
\varepsilon>0
is such that
for
s\in[s_{0}-\varepsilon,s_{0}+\varepsilon]\setminus\{s_{0}\}
,
\phi_{s,n}\notin\mathcal{F}^{1}
. By
(PR-4)
,
the maps
\phi_{s_{0}-\varepsilon,n}
and
\phi_{s_{0}+\varepsilon,n}
belong to different connected components of
\mathcal{F}^{0}
. It
follows (see
[
Arn94
]
) that the change
\phi_{s_{0}-\varepsilon,n}(\mathcal{S})
and
\phi_{s_{0}+\varepsilon,n}(\mathcal{S})

is given by a
perestroika
; see Figure
2.1
.

As
\phi_{s,n}
converges to
\phi_{s}
, the sequence
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,n}:=\widetilde{\phi}_{s}+\phi_{s,n}-\phi_{s}
converges
to
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,n}
, where it is enough to use convergence in
C^{1}
-norm. By stability of embeddings, for sufficiently large
n
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,n}
is isotopic to
\widetilde{\phi}
.
Also
\pi(\widetilde{\phi}_{s,n})=\phi_{s,n}
. A perestroika at the level of
\phi_{s,n}
induces a Reidemeister move at the level
of
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,n}
.
As displayed in Figure
2.1
, a Reidemeister move R1 occurs at each cusp, a Reidemeister move R2 occurs at each tangency,
and a Reidemeister move R3 happens at each triple point.
∎

3.
Equivariant jet spaces

We now begin our discussion of involutive links. As in Section
2
, we start by introducing equivariant jet spaces. We then establish the genericity of regular diagrams in the equivariant setting; this closely follows the argument of Section
2
. The proof of the equivariant Reidemeister theorem, however, will require significantly more investment, so we delay it to future sections. Nevertheless, our discussion here will be foundational to the rest of the paper.

3.1.
General setup

We begin with a general discussion of equivariant jet spaces following
[
Wal85
]
. Although we only deal with the group
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we give Wall’s construction for
G
a compact Lie group in order to illustrate the subtleties that arise in the definition.

We start with the Euclidean case. Let
V
and
W
be two finite-dimensional vector spaces acted on by
G
; we assume this action to be orthogonal. Let
\mathcal{E}^{G}(V)\subset\mathcal{E}(V)
be the ring of
G
-invariant germs at
0\in V
and
\mathfrak{m}^{G}(V)=\mathfrak{m}(V)\cap\mathcal{E}^{G}(V)
be its maximal ideal. Let
\mathcal{E}^{G}(V,W)\subset\mathcal{E}(V,W)
be the
\mathcal{E}^{G}(V)
-module of
G
-equivariant germs taking
0\in V
to
0\in W
. The space of
G
-equivariant
k
-jets taking
0
to
0
is then defined to be

\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k}(V,W)=\mathcal{E}^{G}(V,W)/\mathfrak{m}^{G}(V)^{k+1}\mathcal{E}^{G}(V,W).

Now suppose
G
acts smoothly on two manifolds
X
and
Y
. We first note that if
f
is a
G
-equivariant map, then the behavior of
f
in a neighborhood of
x
determines the behavior of
f
near the entire orbit of
x
. Thus, one might expect the equivariant jet space to be a bundle over
(X/G)\times Y
. Indeed, if the action of
G
is free, then smooth
G
-equivariant maps from
X
to
Y
are in bijection with smooth maps from
X/G
to
Y
, and the study of equivariant jets can be reduced to the study of non-equivariant jets on the quotient. However, in general the equivariant jet space at
x
should depend on the isotropy group of
x
. This is because the isotropy group preserves a neighborhood of
x
and imposes a nontrivial restriction on germs at
x
when requiring
G
-equivariance.

Let
x\in X
thus have isotropy group
H\subset G
. Fix a slice
S
of the
G
-action at
x
; this is a locally
H
-invariant submanifold which is transverse to the
G
-orbit of
x
at
x
. Fix a Euclidean neighborhood
V
of
x
in
S
which is preserved by
H
and on which
H
acts orthogonally. Note that a
G
-equivariant map from
X
to
Y
necessarily takes
x
into the
H
-fixed point set
Y^{H}
of
Y
. Thus, let
y\in Y^{H}
and let
W
be a Euclidean neighborhood of
y
in
Y
on which
H
acts orthogonally. The space of equivariant
k
-jets taking
x
to
y
is then defined to be

(3.1)

\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k}(x,y)=\mathfrak{J}_{H}^{k}(V,W).

Note that the right-hand side consists of
H
-equivariant, rather than
G
-equivariant, germs.

Now consider a point
g\cdot x
in the orbit of
x
. This has isotropy group
gHg^{-1}\subset G
. Clearly,
g\cdot S
is a slice through
g\cdot x
, and
g\cdot V
and
g\cdot W
are
gHg^{-1}
-invariant neighborhoods of
g\cdot x
and
g\cdot y
, respectively. We thus have an obvious isomorphism

(3.2)

\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k}(x,y)\cong\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k}(g\cdot x,g\cdot y)

obtained by sending a germ
f
from
x
to
y
to the germ
gfg^{-1}
from
g\cdot x
to
g\cdot y
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
[x]=G\cdot x
be a
G
-orbit in
X
. An
equivariant
k
-jet at
[x]
(or
along
[x]
) consists of a
k
-jet at each point of
[x]
, such that each jet is equivariant in the sense of (
3.1
) and the different
k
-jets are taken to each other by the isomorphism (
3.2
).

Roughly speaking, the reader should think that there are two steps to ensuring that a
k
-jet is
G
-equivariant. The first is that the germ itself should be equivariant under the local action of the isotropy group at that point. The second is that a germ at
x
gives a class of germs defined along the orbit
[x]
, each of which is equivariant in the previous sense. Note that because of the first condition, equivariant germs at points with non-isomorphic isotropy groups are qualitatively different and should not be regarded as elements of the same jet space. To this end, we introduce the following partition of
X
:

Definition 3.4
.

The
orbit type
of
x\in X
is determined by the isotropy group
H
of
x
and the representation
\rho
of
H
on the tangent space
T_{x}S
. Two pairs
(H_{1},\rho_{1})
and
(H_{2},\rho_{2})
are equivalent if there exists some
g\in G
such that
gH_{1}g^{-1}=H_{2}
and the representations
h\mapsto\rho(h)
and
h\mapsto\rho_{2}(ghg^{-1})
of
H_{1}
are equivalent; that is,
\rho_{1}(h)=\rho_{2}(ghg^{-1})
for all
h\in H_{1}
.

Let
\lambda
now be a fixed orbit type and
X_{\lambda}
be the subset of points in
X
with orbit type
\lambda
. Clearly,
X_{\lambda}
is composed of the union of
G
-orbits. Write
(X/G)_{\lambda}
for the image of
X_{\lambda}
in
X/G
. This is of course the same as the set of orbits in
X_{\lambda}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\lambda
be a fixed orbit type. The
equivariant
k
-jet space

\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\lambda}(X,Y)
is the collection of all equivariant
k
-jets over all
[x]\in(X/G)_{\lambda}
. This can canonically be given the structure of a smooth manifold; see
[
Wal85
]
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\lambda
be a fixed orbit type and
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be equivariant. The
k
-jet extension
of
f
is the map

\mathfrak{j}^{k,\lambda}_{G}f\colon(X/G)_{\lambda}\to\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\lambda}(X,Y)

that assigns to each
[x]\in(X/G)_{\lambda}
the equivariant
k
-jet
\mathfrak{j}^{k,\lambda}_{G}f([x])
along
[x]
obtained by restricting
f
to a germ at each point of
[x]
.

The important takeaway is that there are many different equivariant jet spaces, each corresponding to a fixed orbit type and involving the points in
X
of that orbit type. We will sometimes assemble all of these jet spaces into a single jet space by taking their disjoint union; we denote this by
\mathfrak{J}^{k}_{G}(X,Y)
. However, as discussed in
[
Wal85
]
there does not seem to be a satisfactory way to topologize this union to make it into a single bundle over
X
or
X/G
. Nevertheless, if
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a
G
-equivariant map, we obtain a
k
-jet extension

\mathfrak{j}^{k}_{G}f\colon X\to\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k}(X,Y)

which assigns to each
x\in X
the equivariant
k
-jet
\mathfrak{j}^{k,\lambda}_{G}f(x)=\mathfrak{j}^{k,\lambda}_{G}f([x])\in\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\lambda}(X,Y)
, where
\lambda
is the orbit type of
x
and
[x]
is the equivalence class of
x
in
(X/G)_{\lambda}
.

We now turn to equivariant multijets. For each orbit type
\lambda
, we have a source map

\mathfrak{a}^{\lambda}\colon\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\lambda}(X,Y)\rightarrow(X/G)_{\lambda}.

More generally, let
\Lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{s})
be a tuple of orbit types, not necessarily all distinct. We then have the multijet source map

\mathfrak{a}^{\Lambda}\colon\mathfrak{J}^{k,\lambda_{1}}_{G}(X,Y)\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{J}^{k,\lambda_{s}}_{G}(X,Y)\rightarrow(X/G)_{\lambda_{1}}\times\cdots\times(X/G)_{\lambda_{s}}.

We introduce the notation

X^{(\Lambda)}\subset(X/G)_{\lambda_{1}}\times\cdots\times(X/G)_{\lambda_{s}}

to be the subsets of
distinct
tuples, in analogy with
X^{(s)}
from Section
2.1
. This condition is, of course, only meaningful if two of the
\lambda_{i}
coincide.

Definition 3.7
.

Fix a tuple
\Lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{s})
of orbit types. The
\Lambda
-fold multijet space
is the preimage

\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\Lambda}(X,Y)=(\mathfrak{a}^{\Lambda})^{-1}(X^{(\Lambda)}).

Definition 3.8
.

Fix a tuple
\Lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{s})
of orbit types and let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be equivariant. The
\Lambda
-fold multijet extension
of
f
is the map

\mathfrak{j}_{G}^{k,\Lambda}f\colon X^{(\Lambda)}\to\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\Lambda}(X,Y)

that assigns to each
([x_{1}],\dots,[x_{s}])\in X^{(\Lambda)}
the multijet

\mathfrak{j}_{G}^{k,\Lambda}f([x_{1}],\dots,[x_{s}])=(\mathfrak{j}^{k,\lambda_{1}}_{G}f([x_{1}]),\dots,\mathfrak{j}^{k,\lambda_{s}}_{G}f([x_{s}])).

Cleary, an equivariant multijet is analogous to the notion of a non-equivariant multijet, except that we speak of
s
distinct orbits rather than
s
distinct points, and we must specify the types of these orbits. We are now ready to state the multijet transversality theorem in the equivariant setting:

Theorem 3.9

(see
[
Wal85
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Fix a tuple
\Lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{s})
of orbit types.
Let
\smash{W\subset\mathfrak{J}_{G}^{k,\Lambda}(X,Y)}
be a submanifold. Then the set of equivariant maps
f\colon X\to Y

such that
\smash{\mathfrak{j}_{G}^{k,\Lambda}f}
is transverse to
W
is residual.

3.2.
The case
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}

We now apply our discussion of equivariant jets to the case of involutive links. Unless otherwise specified, henceforth we use
\mathcal{S}
to denote a disjoint union of circles, but now equipped with a smooth involution
\sigma
with isolated fixed points. (That is, we do not allow the degenerate case where
\sigma
acts as the identity on an entire component.) Write
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
for the fixed-point set of
\sigma
and
\mathcal{S}^{-}
for the subset of
\mathcal{S}
on which
\sigma
acts freely.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\widetilde{\tau}(x,y,z)=(-x,y,-z)
be the involution on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by rotation about the
y
-axis. We denote the fixed-point axis of
\widetilde{\tau}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Likewise, let
\tau(x,y)=(-x,y)
be the corresponding involution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We denote the fixed-point axis of
\tau
by
\mathcal{L}=\pi(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a disjoint union of circles equipped with an involution
\sigma
. A link
\widetilde{\phi}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
involutive
if

\widetilde{\phi}\circ\sigma=\widetilde{\tau}\circ\widetilde{\phi}

and the image of
\phi
intersects
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
at a finite number of points.
2
2

2

It is easily checked that this means each component of
\phi(\mathcal{S})
must intersect
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
in either zero or two points.
The resulting link diagram
\phi=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}
is equivariant in the following sense:

\phi\circ\sigma=\tau\circ\phi.

Note that the axis of symmetry is visible in the plane of the page. We call such a diagram a
transvergent diagram
, in contrast to an
intravergent diagram
, for which the axis of symmetry intersects the plane of projection at a single point.

We now describe the equivariant jet space
\mathfrak{J}^{k}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which will be fundamental to the rest of the paper. In this context, there are two orbit types, corresponding to points with trivial isotropy group and points that are fixed by
G
. We denote these orbit types by
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}
, respectively. By definition, the equivariant jet space corresponding to
\lambda_{0}
is simply the non-equivariant jet space over
\mathcal{S}^{-}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which is diffeomorphic to a collection of open intervals:

\mathfrak{J}^{k,\lambda_{0}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})=\mathfrak{J}^{k}(\mathcal{S}^{-}/\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Elements of
\mathfrak{J}^{k,\lambda_{0}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
are pairs of germs of the form
(f,\tau f\sigma)
, where
f
is defined on a small subinterval of
\mathcal{S}^{-}
.

To understand the equivariant jet space corresponding to
\lambda_{1}
, we consider
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant germs from points in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
to points in
(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\mathcal{L}
. Thus, let
x\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
. Choose a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant neighborhood
V
of
x
with parameter
t
such that
x
is taken to
0
and
\sigma
acts by negation. The set of real
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant germs at
x
then consists of power series in
t^{2}
. The corresponding maximal ideal is given by
(t^{2})
, and hence

\mathfrak{m}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V)^{k+1}=(t^{2k+2}).

Now let
y
be a point in
\mathcal{L}
and consider
\mathcal{E}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V,W)
, where
W=\mathbb{R}^{2}
is equipped with the involution
\tau
. We treat this as a global equivariant coordinate chart and relax the condition that
y
be identified with the origin. In local coordinates, such a germ is given by
(x(t),y(t))
, where
x(t)
and
y(t)
are power series in
t
. Imposing
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariance means that
x(-t)=-x(t)
and
y(-t)=y(t)
; this occurs when
x(t)
consists of odd-exponent terms and
y(t)
consists of even-exponent terms. Quotienting out by
\mathfrak{m}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(V)^{k+1}
, we thus have that

\mathfrak{J}^{k,\lambda_{1}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(x,y)=\{(\alpha_{1}t+\alpha_{3}t^{3}+\cdots+\alpha_{2k+1}t^{2k+1},\beta_{0}+\beta_{2}t^{2}+\cdots+\beta_{2k}t^{2k})\}.

Note that this means for points in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant
k
-jet space records derivatives of germs up to order
2k+1
rather than
k
, although equivariance causes half of these to be zero. This convention is discussed in
[
Wal85
]
.

We now consider equivariant multijets. As discussed in Section
3.1
, we obtain an equivariant multijet space for every choice of tuple
\Lambda=(\lambda_{0},\cdots,\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{1})=(m\lambda_{0},n\lambda_{1})
. An element of this multijet space lives over
m
distinct equivalence classes in
\mathcal{S}^{-}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
n
distinct points in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

Definition 3.12
.

We write

\mathfrak{J}^{k,(m,n)}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})=\mathfrak{J}^{k,(m\lambda_{0},n\lambda_{1})}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2}).

When our multijet space only involves orbits of type
\lambda_{0}
, we frequently use a shorter notation

\mathfrak{J}^{k,m}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2}):=\mathfrak{J}^{k,(m,0)}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Example 3.13
.

Consider the subset of equivariant multijet space that corresponds to a triple point off of the axis of symmetry. Phrased in terms of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant maps from
\mathcal{S}^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this is given by the condition

\exists t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}\text{ pairwise distinct, such that }\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})=\phi(t_{3})\notin\mathcal{L}.

Note that the above occurs if and only if
\sigma(t_{1})
,
\sigma(t_{2})
, and
\sigma(t_{3})
form a triple point on the other side of the axis of symmetry. However, as these conditions are not independent, we still think of the singularity as involving three (rather than six) points in the domain. It is straightforward to rephrase the above condition in terms of jets over three distinct classes in
\mathcal{S}^{-}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This takes place in

\mathfrak{J}^{0,(3,0)}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})=\mathfrak{J}^{0,3}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Example 3.14
.

Consider the subset of equivariant multijet space that corresponds to having a double point on the axis of symmetry. Phrased in terms of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant maps from
\mathcal{S}^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this is given by the condition

\exists t_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{-},t_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\text{ such that }\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2}).

Note that the above occurs if and only if
\phi(\sigma(t_{2}))
also coincides with
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})
. Once again, however, we think of the singularity as involving two (rather than three) points in the domain. It is straightforward to rephrase the above condition in terms of a jet over a class in
\mathcal{S}^{-}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and a jet over a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-fixed point. This takes place in

\mathfrak{J}^{0,(1,1)}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2}).

We now have the analogue of the parameter counting theorem, which we state for the case of equivariant maps from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This follows immediately from the same argument as before, after observing that the dimension of the domain of the relevant jet extension is equal to the number of factors of orbit type
\lambda_{0}
.

Lemma 3.15

(Equivariant Parameter Counting)
.

Let
R
be an
r
-dimensional manifold, which we think of as a parameter space specifying a family of equivariant link diagrams. Let
W\subset\mathfrak{J}^{k,(m,n)}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a submanifold of
codimension
w
. If
r+m<w
, then there is a residual subset of equivariant maps from
R\times\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such
that for any
F
in that subset and any
u\in R
, the equivariant jet extension

\smash{\mathfrak{j}^{k,(m,n)}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}f_{u}}
of
f_{u}=F(u,\cdot)\colon X\to Y
misses
W
.

Sketch of proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Theorem
2.8
. The difference is that
in Theorem
2.8
, we consider maps from
R\times X
to
Y
. Here, we consider maps
from
R\times(\mathcal{S}^{-})^{m}\times(\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})^{n}
. The choice of the source is dictated by the parameters
m,n
depending on
the orbit type
W
belongs to. The dimension of the source is
r+m
. The rest of the proof uses the same argument.
∎

Once again, we can phrase this in terms of the codimension of a set of equivariant maps:

Definition 3.16
.

Let
D
be a submanifold of
\smash{\mathfrak{J}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{k,(m,n)}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.
The
(jet space) codimension of
D
(sometimes called the
number of defining conditions
) is the usual codimension
\operatorname{codim}(D)
of
D
. We define the
(parametric) codimension of
D
to be

\operatorname{pcodim}(D)=\operatorname{codim}(D)-m.

Note that
m
is the dimension of the domain of the relevant multijet extension. In this language, the parameter counting argument states that a generic
r
-parameter family of equivariant link diagrams will miss
D
so long as
r<\operatorname{pcodim}(D)
.

Remark 3.17
.

The parametric codimension in the complex setting was studied e.g. in
[
BZa08
]
. In the real case, many calculations can be repeated.

3.3.
Equivariant regular diagrams

We close this section by establishing the genericity of equivariant regular diagrams. For this, we generalize the argument of Section
2
. As henceforth all of our objects will be equivariant, we re-use the same notation
\mathcal{F}
(and
\mathcal{F}^{0}
, and so on) as in the non-equivariant caase.

Definition 3.18
.

For a fixed
\mathcal{S}
, let
\mathcal{F}
be the space of all equivariant smooth maps from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that take distinct points in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
to distinct points in
\mathcal{L}
.
3
3

3

It is clear that an actual link diagram will have this property, but here we are simply studying the space of equivariant maps
\phi
from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The reason that this condition is separated from Definition
3.19
below is that it does not lead to an equivariant Reidemeister move; rather, it should be thought of as a necessary condition for
\phi
to lift to an actual involutive link.

The following is the analogue of Definition
2.13
:

Definition 3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{0}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{F}
consisting of equivariant maps satisfying the following conditions:

(ER-1)

\phi^{\prime}
is nonzero at every point of
\mathcal{S}^{-}
;

(ER-2)

for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{L}
, if
\phi^{-1}(w)
consists of two points
t_{1},t_{2}
, then

\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})
,
\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})
are linearly independent;

(ER-3)

for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{L}
, the preimage
\phi^{-1}(w)
consists of at most two points;

(ER-4)

if
t\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
is mapped to
\mathcal{L}
, then
\phi^{\prime}(t)
is not tangent to
\mathcal{L}
;

(ER-5)

if
t\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
is mapped to
\mathcal{L}
, then
\phi^{\prime}(t)
is not perpendicular to
\mathcal{L}
;

(ER-6)

if
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
and
\phi(t_{1}),\phi(t_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
, then
\phi(t_{1})\neq\phi(t_{2})
unless
t_{1},t_{2}

are in the same orbit of
\sigma
;

(ER-7)

\phi^{\prime}
is nonzero at every point of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
;

(ER-8)

\phi(\mathcal{S}^{-})
and
\phi(\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
are disjoint.

We refer to such a
\phi\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
as being
regular
.

Remark 3.20
.

Several of these items are quite similar: for example,
(ER-1)
and
(ER-7)
can obviously be combined into the condition that
\phi^{\prime}
is nonzero at every point of
\mathcal{S}
. We separate these because their violation leads to different Reidemeister moves in the equivariant setting.

The following theorem relates
\mathcal{F}^{0}
above with
\mathcal{F}^{0}
from Definition
2.13
, and justifies the usage of the term regularity for both:

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
\phi\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
be equivariant. Then
\phi
is in
\mathcal{F}^{0}
(as in Definition
3.19
) if and only if it is in
\mathcal{F}^{0}
(as in Definition
2.13
) as a non-equivariant map.

Proof.

Assume
\phi
satisfies Definition
3.19
and consider Definition
2.13
. Clearly,
(Reg-1)
is implied by
(ER-1)
and
(ER-7)
. Next, consider
(Reg-2)
, which means that there are no tangencies at double points. If
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{L}
, this is guaranteed by

(ER-2)
. If
w\in\mathcal{L}
, then the corresponding points
t_{1},t_{2}
in
\mathcal{S}
are either both in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, or one is in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and one is in
\mathcal{S}^{-}
, or both are in
\mathcal{S}^{-}
. The first is disallowed by Definition
3.18
and the second cannot happen by
(ER-8)
. In the third case, we must have
t_{1}=\sigma(t_{2})
by
(ER-6)
; it is then easily seen that
\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})
and
\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})
are linearly independent by the equivariance of
\phi
combined with
(ER-4)
and
(ER-5)
. Finally, consider
(Reg-3)
, which means that there are no triple points. If
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{L}
, this is guaranteed by
(ER-3)
. If
w\in\mathcal{L}
, we consider the possibilities for
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
. If all three points lie in
\mathcal{S}^{-}
, we obtain a contradiction with
(ER-6)
since each orbit has size two. Otherwise, exactly one point is in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
by Definition
3.18
. But this contradicts
(ER-8)
.

Conversely, assume
\phi
satisfies Definition
2.13
and consider Definition
3.19
. Clearly,
(ER-1)
and
(ER-7)
are implied by
(Reg-1)
. Next,
(ER-2)
is obviously implied by
(Reg-2)
. Considering
(ER-4)
and
(ER-5)
, we see that these are implied by
(Reg-2)
as well. Indeed, if
t\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
is mapped to
\mathcal{L}
, then
\sigma(t)
is also mapped to
\mathcal{L}
; hence if
(ER-4)
or
(ER-5)
were violated, then the equivariance of
\phi
would create a double point tangency. Likewise,
(Reg-2)
implies Definition
3.18
, since if
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
were both mapped to the same point of
\mathcal{L}
, it is easily checked that
\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})
and
\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})
would both be perpendicular to
\mathcal{L}
and thus create a double point tangency. Finally,
(Reg-3)
obviously implies
(ER-3)
. Considering
(ER-6)
and
(ER-8)
, we see that these are implied by
(Reg-3)
as well. Indeed, if
(ER-6)
was violated, we would obtain a quadruple point with preimage consisting of a pair of two-element orbits of
\sigma
; if
(ER-8)
were violated, we would obtain a triple point with preimage consisting of a two-element orbit of
\sigma
and a point in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.
∎

We close this section by establishing the analogue of Theorem
2.15
. As in Section
2
, this will be established by investigating the codimension of the strata corresponding to violations of
(ER-1)
through
(ER-8)
.

Definition 3.22
.

For each
i
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}
be the subset of
\mathcal{F}
on which (ER-
i
) is violated. These are described explicitly in Table

. Denote

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{8}=\mathcal{F}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{0}.

Table 1.
The strata
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}}
. We refer to
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{6}}
and
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{8}}
as double points, even though taking into account all symmetric branches makes them into quadruple points and triple points, respectively.

Theorem 3.23
.

The subset
\mathcal{F}^{0}
of regular maps is open-dense in
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that a generic map misses
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 8
.
This is done through a case-by-case analysis. In the last two columns of Table
1
, we give the relevant multijet spaces
and the (jet space) codimension of the appropriate submanifold. Note that for the first six entries, the conditions do not involve points of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, so by the discussion in Section
3.1
, we may phrase our analysis in terms of non-equivariant jet spaces.

We thus only comment on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{7}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{8}
. For
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{7}
, the relevant condition is codimension 1 since in the equivariant jet space, one component of the derivative already automatically vanishes at points of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
. The source is of dimension 0, so the stratum is missed by a generic map. For
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{8}
, we relevant condition is codimension 2. The source is of dimension 1, since
t_{1}
varies in a space of dimension 1 while
t_{2}
varies in a space of dimension 0, so the stratum is missed by a generic map.
∎

Theorem
2.20
and Corollary
2.21
can be promoted to the equivariant setting. We will do this in
Subsection
6.4
, when we use the machinery of versality and normal forms to provide more rigorous proofs than those
in the non-equivariant setting.

4.
Deformations of singularities

In this section, we introduce the language needed to study deformations of singularities. Our main goal will be to introduce the notion of a versal deformation. Roughly speaking, this is a deformation of a singularity which induces any other deformation, up to reparameterization. We use versal deformations to classify families of singularities in the next section.

4.1.
RL equivalence

We begin with a general discussion of equivalence of germs, starting with the non-equivariant case. For
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{S}
, let
\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
be the space of multigerms of smooth functions from
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Clearly,

\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})=\mathcal{O}({t_{1}})\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{O}({t_{n}}),

where each
\mathcal{O}({t_{i}})
is the germ of smooth functions into
\mathbb{R}^{2}
at
t_{i}
. We refer to the restriction of
\phi\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
to a neighborhood of
t_{i}
as the
branch
of
\phi
through
t_{i}
.

Definition 4.1

(see
[
AGZV12
]
)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
and
g\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,t_{n}^{\prime})
. We say that
f
and
g
are
right-left (RL) equivalent
if there exist open neighborhoods
U
and
U^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}
and
V
and
V^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, together with germs of diffeomorphisms
h_{R}
and
h_{L}
, making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Here,
U
and
U^{\prime}
are open neighborhoods of
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\}
and
\{t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}\}
, respectively, while
V
and
V^{\prime}
are open neighborhoods of
\{f(t_{1}),\dots,f(t_{n})\}
and
\{g(t^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,g(t^{\prime}_{n})\}
, respectively. The map
h_{R}\colon U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
is the germ of a diffeomorphism sending
t_{i}
to
t^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,n
, while the map
h_{L}\colon V\rightarrow V^{\prime}
is the germ of a diffeomorphism sending
f(t_{i})
to
g(t^{\prime}_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. It is clear that RL equivalence is an equivalence relation.

Now consider the equivariant case. For a subset of points
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
which is setwise preserved by
\sigma
, we have the space of equivariant multigerms, which we denote by

\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=(\mathcal{O}({t_{1}})\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{O}({t_{n}}))^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

We usually group
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
in such a way so that
\sigma t_{i}=t_{i+1}
for
i=1,3,\dots,2\ell-1
and
\sigma t_{i}=t_{i}
for
i=2\ell+1,\dots,n
. The equivariant multigerm space then decomposes as

\mathcal{O}(t_{1},t_{2})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{O}(t_{{2\ell-1}},t_{{2\ell}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\mathcal{O}(t_{{2\ell+1}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{O}(t_{n})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

Since the involution
\sigma
on
\mathcal{S}
interchanges neighborhoods of
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
, it is clear that

\mathcal{O}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cong\mathcal{O}(t_{{2i-1}})

by taking
f\in\mathcal{O}({t_{2i-1}})
to
(f,\tau f\sigma)
. We refer to the restriction of
\phi
to a neighborhood of a two-element orbit
\{t_{i},\sigma t_{i}\}
as a
symmetric pair of branches
, while the restriction of
\phi
to a neighborhood of a one-element orbit
t_{i}=\sigma t_{i}
is called a
fixed-point branch
. We usually abuse terminology and still refer to a symmetric pair of branches as a single branch. When the context is clear, we will correspondingly write
f
in place of
(f,\tau f\sigma)
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
g\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,t_{n}^{\prime})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
. We say that
f
and
g
are
right-left (RL) equivalent
if there exist data as in Definition
4.1
which are equivariant. That is, the neighborhoods
U
and
U^{\prime}
are required to be preserved by
\sigma
, the neighborhoods
V
and
V^{\prime}
are required to be preserved by
\tau
, and
h_{R}
and
h_{L}
are required to be equivariant germs of diffeomorphisms.

Let
D
be a subset of some non-equivariant or equivariant multijet space. Up to RL equivalence, we would like to explicitly describe the space of germs appearing in
D
. By this, we mean the following: recall that a multijet is an equivalence class of germs over some
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
. Our aim is to give a model family of germs such that any germ in any equivalence class from
D
is RL equivalent to some germ in our family.

Definition 4.3
.

Fix
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{S}
and let
\Lambda
be a smooth manifold, which we think of as a parameter space. A
family
of germs at
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
is a smooth map
\Phi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
. The definition extends to the equivariant case by replacing
\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
with
\smash{\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}}
. We denote
\phi_{\lambda}=\Phi(\lambda)
.

Definition 4.4
.

Suppose
D
is a subset of some non-equivariant or equivariant multijet space. A
normal form
for
D
is a family of germs
(\Phi,\Lambda)
such that any germ in any equivalence class from
D
is RL equivalent to
\Phi(\lambda)
for a finite (non-empty) set of
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

The point of Definition
4.4
is the following. Let
\phi
be a map from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose jet extension hits
D
. Each such intersection corresponds to a multijet over a particular set of points
\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}\}
. The assertion of Definition
4.4
is that up to local reparameterization of the domain and codomain, the behavior of
\phi
at
\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}\}
is determined modulo some set of parameters from the parameter space
\Lambda
. An especially salient case will be when there are no parameters; e.g.,
\Lambda
consists of a single point. In this case, we refer to
D
as being
simple
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
D
be a subset of some non-equivariant or equivariant multijet space. We say that
D
is
simple
if it has a normal form with
\Lambda
a single point. Equivalently, this means that whenever
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{O}(t_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,t_{n}^{\prime})
are two multigerms whose jet extensions lie in
D
, we have that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are RL equivalent.

The reader should think of a simple subset of jet space as consisting of (the jet extensions of) precisely one RL orbit. The archetypal example is the following.

Example 4.6
.

Consider the subset
