higher-order cusps. This means that if the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
involves some differentials vanishing, then they vanish only to the required order and not a higher order.

•

higher-order tangencies. This means that if the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
involves two branches that intersect, then they intersect
with the prescribed tangency and not to a tangency of higher order.

In Lemmas
5.3
and
5.7
, we already proved that a coincidence or a strikethrough of a codimension 1 singularity
has codimension at least
2
.

6.1.
Off-axis singularities

Off-axis singularities involve points on
\mathcal{S}^{-}
that are mapped to
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{L}
.
Each of these thus actually constitutes a symmetric orbit of singularities and leads to a symmetric orbit of Reidemeister moves. These result in symmetrized versions of the usual three Reidemeister moves.

6.1.1.
Off-axis cusp

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1}
to be the space of off-axis
(2,3)
-cusps with no coincidences or strikethroughs. Then
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1}}
consists
of strata describing a coincidence, a strikethrough, or a higher-order cusp. By Lemma
5.20
, an off-axis cusp is a codimension 1 if and only if it is a
(2,3)
-cusp, so this is a union of strata of codimension
2
. By Lemma
5.21
, a one-parameter versal deformation of the off-axis
(2,3)
-cusp is given by
(t^{2},t^{3}+\lambda t)
.
For
\lambda>0
, this describes a smooth curve, while for
\lambda<0
there is a self-intersection at
t=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda}
. The bifurcation
diagram is given in Figure
6.1
.

Figure 6.1.
Top: bifurcation of an outer cusp. The middle picture shows the cusp singularity. Middle: bifurcation diagrams
of an outer tangency. Bottom: bifurcation diagrams of an outer triple point.

6.1.2.
Off-axis tangency

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{2}
to be the space of
1
-fold tangencies with no coincidences or strikethroughs. Then
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{2}}
consists
of strata describing a coincidence, a strikethrough, or a higher-order tangency. By Lemma
5.33
, an outer tangency is codimension 1 if and only if it is a
1
-fold tangency, so this is a union of strata of
codimension
2
. By Lemma
5.35
, our tangency has normal form given by the two branches
(t,0)
and
(s,s^{2})
. A one-parameter versal deformation is given by perturbing the second branch to
(s,s^{2}+\lambda)
. For
\lambda<0
the branches intersect twice, while for
\lambda>0
, they
do not intersect. The bifurcation
diagram is given in Figure
6.1
.

6.1.3.
Off-axis triple points

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{3}
to be the space of ordinary triple points. Note that this comes from an ordinary strikethrough of the ordinary double point and hence has codimension
1
. Any extra tangency between branches leads to a higher codimension phenomenon, as well as any strikethroughs or coincidences. Thus the complement
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{3}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{3}}
consists of strata of codimension at least
2
. A versal deformation for the ordinary triple point was given in Example
4.14
. The bifurcation diagram is given in Figure
6.1
.

6.2.
On-axis singularities.

On-axis singularities involve domain points on
\mathcal{S}^{-}
but image points lying on the axis of symmetry. In an on-axis singularity we have two symmetric branches of our map that intersect at a point on
\mathcal{L}
.

6.2.1.
On-axis line tangency

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{4}
to be the space of
1
-fold line tangencies with no coincidences or strikethroughs. Then
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{4}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{4}}
consists
of strata describing a coincidence, a strikethrough, or a higher-order line tangency. By Lemma
5.33
, a line tangency is codimension
1
if and only if it is order
1
, so this is a union of strata of codimension
2
. By Lemma
5.36
, a
1
-fold line tangency has normal form given by
(t^{2},t)
. A one-parameter versal deformation is given by
(t^{2}+\lambda,t)
. The bifurcation diagram is given in Figure
6.2
.

Figure 6.2.
Top: Bifurcation of central tangency. Middle: Bifurcation of perpendicularity tangency.
Bottom: Bifurcaton of central double point.

.

6.2.2.
On-axis perpendicular tangency

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{5}
to be the space of
4
-fold perpendicular tangencies with no coincidences or strikethroughs. Then
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{5}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{5}}
consists
of strata describing a coincidence, a strikethrough, or a higher-order perpendicular tangency. By Lemma
5.33
, a perpendicular tangency is codimension
1
if and only if it is order
4
as a tangency, so this is a union of strata of codimension
2
. By Lemma
5.37
, a
4
-fold perpendicular tangency has normal form given by the two symmetric branches
(t,t^{3})
and
(-s,s^{3})
. A versal deformation is given by simultaneously perturbing
(t,t^{3}+\lambda t)
and
(-s,s^{3}+\lambda s)
. The bifurcation diagram is given in Figure
6.2
.

6.2.3.
On-axis double point

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{6}
to be the space of on-axis double points with no coincidences or strikethroughs, satisfying the additional condition that there are no tangencies between the four branches and that none of them are parallel or perpendicular to
\mathcal{L}
. More precisely, recall that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{6}}
consists of germs such that there exist
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
not in the same orbit of
\sigma
with
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
. To define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{6}
, we additionally require that any two vectors among
\{\phi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\phi^{\prime}(t_{2}),\tau\phi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\tau\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})\}
are linearly independent and that none of them are horizontal or vertical. Negating this condition adds an extra equation, so it is clear that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{6}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{6}}
consists of strata of codimension 2. A versal deformation of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{6}
is given by shifting the branches through
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
to the right according to
\phi(t)\mapsto\phi(t)+(\lambda,0)
, and shifting their symmetric branches to the left. The bifurcation diagram is somewhat complex,
because it involves four branches. See Figure
6.2
.

6.3.
Fixed-point singularities

Like on-axis singularities, fixed-point singularities involve image points on the axis of symmetry, but (unlike on-axis singularities) involve domain points from
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

6.3.1.
Fixed-point cusp

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{7}
to be the space of fixed-point
(2,3)
-cusps with no coincidences or strikethroughs. Then
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{7}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{7}}
consists
of strata describing a coincidence, a strikethrough, or a higher-order fixed-point cusp. By Lemma
5.20
, a fixed-point cusp is codimension
1
if and only if it is a
(2,3)
-cusp, so this is a union of strata of codimension
2
. By Lemma
5.25
, a versal deformation of the
(2,3)
-fixed point cusp is given by
(t^{3}+\lambda t,t^{2})
. The bifurcation diagram is given in Figure
6.3
.

Figure 6.3.
Top: Bifurcation of a fixed point
(2,3)
cusp. Bottom: Bifurcation of a fixed double point.

6.3.2.
Fixed double point

Define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}
to be the space of fixed double points with no coincidences or strikethroughs, satisfying the additional condition that none of the branches to the double point are tangent to each other. More precisely, recall that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{8}}
consists of germs such that for some
t_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
and
t_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, we have
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})
. To define
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}
, we require that none of
\{\phi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\tau\phi^{\prime}(t_{1}),\phi^{\prime}(t_{2})\}
are parallel to each other (and that all of them are nonzero). Negating this condition adds an extra equation, so it is clear that
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{8}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}}
consists of strata of codimension 2. A versal deformation of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}
is given by shifting the branch through
t_{1}
(and thus the symmetric branch through
\sigma t_{1}
) up according to
\phi(t)+(0,\lambda)
. The bifurcation diagram is given in Figure
6.3
.

6.4.
Regular paths and equivariant Reidemeister moves in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Having described the strata
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}
, we now define regularity of paths. Following Section
2.3
, let

\mathcal{F}^{1}=\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}.

Definition 6.1
.

A path
\phi_{s},s\in[0,1]
in
\mathcal{F}
is called
regular
if:

(S-1)

\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
are regular maps;

(S-2)

\phi_{s}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}
for all
s
;

(S-3)

there are finitely many values
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}
such that
\phi_{s_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}^{1}
; for other
values
\phi_{s}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
;

(S-4)

regarded as a path in
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\phi_{s}
crosses
\mathcal{F}^{1}
transversely.

As in Section
2.3
, we set
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{i}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{i}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
for each
i
and denote

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{8}=\mathcal{F}\setminus(\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}).

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\phi_{s}
,
s\in[0,1]
, be any path in
\mathcal{F}
with
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
.
Then there exists an arbitrarily close path
\phi^{\prime}_{s}
,
equal to
\phi_{s}
at the ends, which is regular.

Proof.

By construction,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
is the union of codimension
2
strata. Using the Equivariant Transversality Theorem
3.9
applied as in Lemma
3.15
, there is a path
\phi^{\prime}_{s}
, equal to
\phi_{s}
on the boundary (so that
(S-1)
holds),
as close to
\phi_{s}
as we please, missing the codimension
2
strata (so
(S-2)
holds) and intersecting
\mathcal{F}^{1}
transversely. The
latter amounts to saying that
(S-4)
holds; this implies
(S-3)
.
∎

Now suppose
\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
is a path of equivariant maps from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
such that
\phi_{s}=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
is regular. Then
\phi_{s}
crosses
\mathcal{F}^{1}
at finitely many parameters
s
. Lifting these crossings to
\smash{\widetilde{\phi}_{s}}
gives the following behaviors:

Definition 6.3
.

The following list of moves
(IR-1)
–
(M-3)
constitutes the set of
equivariant Reidemeister moves
.

(IR-1)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{1}
(off-axis cusp)
leads to the (IR-1) move.

(IR-2)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{2}
(off-axis tangency) leads to the (IR-2) move.

(IR-3)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{3}
(off-axis triple point) leads to the (IR-3) move.

(R-1)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{7}
(fixed-point cusp) leads to the (R-1) move.

(R-2)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{4}
(on-axis line tangency) leads to the (R-2) move.

(M-1)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{8}
(fixed double point) leads to the (M-1) move.

(M-2)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{5}
(on-axis perpendicular tangency) leads to the (M-2) move.

(M-3)

Crossing
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{6}
(on-axis double point) leads to the (M-3) move.

See Figure
6.4
. We remark that these figures should be considered as patterns, not as an exhaustive list of examples, in the sense that the signs of crossings may be changed (as long as the change is consistent). For future reference, we also consider the moves where the tunnels and bridges are not specified. A move among
(IR-1)
–
(M-3)
considered as a move on a diagram in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where the crossings are not specified, is called a
planar equivariant Reidemeister move
.

IR-1 move:
IR-2 move:
IR-3 move:
R-1 move:
R-2 move:
M-1 move:
M-2 move:
M-3 move:

Figure 6.4.
The equivariant Reidemeister moves.

This quickly gives the equivariant Reidemeister theorem.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
be involutive links whose projections to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
give transvergent diagrams
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. If
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are isotopic as involutive links, then
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
can be connected by a sequence of equivariant Reidemeister moves as in Definition
6.3
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
be an equivariant isotopy from
L_{1}
to
L_{2}
and set
\phi_{s}=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
. Theorem
6.2
allows us to perturb
\phi_{s}
to a path
\phi^{\prime}_{s}
that is regular. We can lift this perturbation to an isotopy of equivariant links by setting

\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}}_{s}=\widetilde{\phi}_{s}+\phi^{\prime}_{s}-\phi_{s}

where
\phi^{\prime}_{s}-\phi_{s}
is considered as a map into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This is an equivariant isotopy, provided that the perturbation is sufficiently small. Each time
\phi^{\prime}_{s}
crosses a stratum
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{i}
yields a corresponding Reidemeister move, as described in Definition
6.3
.
∎

6.5.
Undoing the
(R-1)
move

Two transvergent diagrams of the same strongly invertible knot in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
are related by a sequence of equivariant Reidemeister moves.
These moves include (R-1)-moves performed at the two fixed points of the knot. We
assume that these points are
(0,1)
and
(0,2)
. Although it will not play a large role in the present paper, it is sometimes helpful to know that we can choose one of these two points and perform
equivariant Reidemeister moves only at the second.

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose
D
and
D^{\prime}
are two diagrams of the same strongly invertible knot
K
such that
D
and
D^{\prime}
differ by a single
(R-1)
move
performed at the point
(0,1)
. Then, there is a sequence of Reidemeister moves connecting
D
and
D^{\prime}
which may contain the
(R-1)
move
at
(0,2)
, but does not involve any
(R-1)
move at
(0,1)
.

Proof.

The rough idea is that any time the
(R-1)
move would be performed at
(0,1)
, we simply rotate the rest of the diagram instead, keeping the diagram around
(0,1)
fixed. We include the details here for the reader who wishes to see the argument written out explicitly. Let
\smash{\widetilde{\phi}}\colon S^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a parameterization of
K
. Then
\smash{\phi=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}}
is a regular diagram parameterizing
D
and satisfies the conditions listed in Theorem
3.21
. In particular,
\phi
satisfies item
(ER-7)
, which states that
\phi^{\prime}
is nonzero at every point in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

Choose cylindrical coordinates
(r,z,\theta)
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Fix any
r_{1},r_{2}>0
with
r_{1}<r_{2}
. Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be the concentric cylinders centered at
(0,0,1)
given by

\{r\leq r_{i}\}
and
|z_{i}-1|\leq r_{i}
for
i=1,2
. We assume
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are sufficiently small so that they intersect
K
in small arc. Let
\kappa
be any smooth function which is zero inside
C_{1}
and
\pi
outside of
C_{2}
. For
s\in[0,1]
, let

\widetilde{\phi}_{s}\colon S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\quad\text{by}\quad\widetilde{\phi}_{s}(u)=R_{s\kappa(\widetilde{\phi}(u))}\circ\widetilde{\phi}(u),

where
R_{w}
is the rotation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
about the
z
-axis by angle
w
. Clearly,
\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
consists of taking a knot and rotating all of it, except for the part contained in
C_{1}
. The endpoint of this rotation gives the same diagram as if we had performed an
(R-1)
move at
(0,1)
.

Perturb the projection
\phi_{s}
of this equivariant isotopy to be regular. Note, that
\phi_{s}
already satisfies
(ER-7)
at
(0,1)
for all
s
; since
(ER-7)
is an open condition, our perturbation has this property also. Now, the
(R-1)
move at
(0,1)
arises precisely from
violations of
(ER-7)
at
(0,1)
. We have thus constructed a regular path consists of equivariant Reidemeister moves without the
(R-1)
move at
(0,1)
, as desired.
∎

It is not difficult to show that this procedure does, in fact, create an
(R-1)
move at
(0,2)
. In fact, it is easily seen that the parity of the number of
(R-1)
moves needed to connect to equivariant diagrams is invariant.

6.6.
Equivariant Reidemeister moves in
S^{3}

In the non-equivariant setting, if
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
are two links in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that are isotopic as links in
S^{3}
, then they are isotopic as links in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This is evident from the usual dimension counting argument. Indeed, since
\mathcal{S}
is one-dimensional and hitting
\infty
is a codimension 3 condition, a generic one-parameter family of maps from
\mathcal{S}
into
S^{3}
can be assumed to avoid
\infty
. Unfortunately, this does not hold in the equivariant setting, leading to a difference between equivariant Reidemeister moves in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
S^{3}
.

To investigate this, we must understand the possible singularities of a diagram at
\infty
. Let
\iota\colon S^{3}\to S^{3}
be an inversion of the sphere that commutes with the symmetry action and moves
\infty
to
0
. Write
\pi^{\iota}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
for the composition
\pi\circ\iota
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{3}=S^{3}\setminus\{0\}
. We then set

\phi^{\iota}=\pi^{\iota}\circ\widetilde{\phi}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

for any map
\widetilde{\phi}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow S^{3}
. This gives a diagram of
\widetilde{\phi}
around
\infty
. We abuse of notation slightly since
\phi^{\iota}
is not defined on
\smash{\widetilde{\phi}^{-1}(0)}
, but this will
not pose any problems.

Definition 6.6
.

Singularities of a map
\widetilde{\phi}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow S^{3}
at
\infty
are defined in terms of the behavior of
\phi^{\iota}
near the origin.

•

An equivariant map
\widetilde{\phi}
is regular at infinity if
\phi^{\iota}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
misses the origin.

•

A family of equivariant maps
\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
is regular at infinity if the map
\phi^{\iota}_{s}\colon\mathcal{S}\times I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
is transverse to the origin. In particular,
\phi^{\iota}_{s}
hits the origin
only for finitely many parameters
s
. At such parameters, we require that the diagram
\phi^{\iota}_{s}
is regular at the origin in the sense of Definition
3.19
. For example,
\phi^{\iota}_{s}
can have at most a double point at the origin, and so on.

Theorem 6.7
.

Regular maps and paths are residual in the space of equivariant maps from
\mathcal{S}
to
S^{3}
.

Proof.

The space of all equivariant maps is the disjoint union
\mathcal{I}^{0}\cup\mathcal{I}^{1}\cup\mathcal{I}^{2}\cup\mathcal{I}^{3}
, where:

•

\mathcal{I}^{0}
consists of maps such that
\phi^{\iota}
misses the origin.

•

\mathcal{I}^{1}
consists of maps such that
\phi^{\iota}
hits the origin and the diagram is regular at the origin in the sense of Definition
3.19
.

•

\mathcal{I}^{2}
consists of all remaining maps.

We claim that
\mathcal{I}^{1}\cup\mathcal{I}^{2}\cup\mathcal{I}^{3}
has
\operatorname{pcodim}=1
; and that
\mathcal{I}^{2}
has
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
.

Consider the first claim. The union
\mathcal{I}^{1}\cup\mathcal{I}^{2}
consists of maps where
\phi^{\iota}
hits the origin. This can happen two ways: either
\phi^{\iota}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
hits the origin, which requires two conditions and has domain of dimension
1
; or
\phi^{\iota}(\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
hits the origin, which requires one condition and has domain of dimension
0
. Hence in both cases the situation is of codimension 1. Imposing the failure of regularity increases this codimension by
1
, showing the second claim; imposing the failure of a condition from
\mathcal{F}^{1}
increases the codimension further by
1
. This gives the third claim. The theorem then follows from the Equivariant Parameter Counting Lemma
3.15
.
∎

A path of equivariant maps
\widetilde{\phi}_{s}
from
\mathcal{S}
to
S^{3}
may thus be assumed to lie in
\mathcal{I}^{0}\cup\mathcal{I}^{1}
and cross
\mathcal{I}^{1}
transversely at a finite number of parameters. As before, we attempt to understand the possible before-and-after pictures associated to these crossings. By construction, at each such parameter
s_{i}
, the diagram
\phi^{\iota}_{s_{i}}
hits the origin at some
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
. There are two possibilities:

•

If
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, then a fixed-point branch of
\phi_{s_{i}}
hits
\infty
. As
s
varies, this fixed point sweeps over
\infty
as indicated in Figure
6.5
.

•

If
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
, then there are two branches of
\phi_{s_{i}}
that hit
\infty
, corresponding to a double point over
\infty
. As
s
varies, the pair of arcs forming this double point sweep over
\infty
as indicated in Figure
6.5
.

This gives rise to the following moves at
\infty
:

Definition 6.8
.

We define the
I-move
to be the move which takes a strand of the knot diagram and passes it through
\infty
in an equivariant manner. See Figure
6.6
.

Definition 6.9
.

We define the
S-move
to be the move which takes a double point in the diagram and passes it through
\infty
in an equivariant manner. See Figure
6.6
.

Figure 6.5.
Crossing the point at infinity. Top: the fixed branch crosses the infinity point. Bottom: a pair of branches crossing
the infinity point.

Figure 6.6.
Left: The I-move. The shaded part is the remaining diagram, which is not changed by the move. Right: the S-move.

It is not hard to check that the I-move cannot be replicated by the composition of any other equivariant Reidemeister moves, since the I-move interchanges the order of the fixed points on the axis of symmetry. In contrast, any two diagrams related by the S-move are related by a different sequence of equivariant Reidemeister moves. To see this, consider Figure
6.6
. Take the top strand of the double point and move it over the rest of the diagram, while simultaneously moving the bottom strand of the double point under the rest of the diagram. This gives an equivariant isotopy in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that replicates the effect of the S-move. Perturbing this isotopy so that it is regular gives the desired sequence of equivariant Reidemeister moves. Nevertheless, the S-move will be useful when discussing loops of Reidemeister moves in Section
9
.

We thus finally obtain a proof of Theorem
1.3
:

See
1.3

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
6.4
, together with the above discussion of the I-move and S-move.
∎

Towards further applications in Section
9.3
, we analyze codimension 2 singularities at infinity. We consider two-parameter families of equivariant embeddings

\widetilde{\phi}_{s,s^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{S}\to S^{3}
. The map
\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
is the projection, and
\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}
is the projection
at infinity. The fact that
\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
and
\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}
are defined only on a subset of
\mathcal{S}
(e.g.
\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
is not
defined at
t\in\mathcal{S}
for which
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,s^{\prime}}(t)\in S^{3}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{3}
), will not pose significant problems.

These will essentially turn out to be the codimension 1 on-axis singularities of
\phi^{\iota}
taking place at the point
(0,0)\in\mathcal{L}
.

Definition 6.10
.

For a two-parameter family
\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
,
(s,s^{\prime})\in[0,1]^{2}
its infinity locus

\Sigma=\{(s,s^{\prime})\in[0,1]^{2}\colon\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}\textrm{ hits }(0,0)\in\mathcal{L}\}

is decomposed as a union of
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\Sigma^{-}
corresponding to whether
{\phi^{\iota}}^{-1}_{s,s^{\prime}}(0,0)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

or
\mathcal{S}^{-}
(the spaces
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\Sigma^{-}
may intersect).

We say that the family
is
regular at infinity
if the infinity locus
has the following properties.

(
\Sigma
-1)

\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is a smooth closed
1
-manifold and
\Sigma^{-}
is an immersed
1
-manifold with boundary with possibly ordinary
double points as self-intersections;

(
\Sigma
-2)

the boundary of
\Sigma^{-}
belongs to
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
;

(
\Sigma
-3)

the intersection of
\Sigma^{-}
and
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is transverse;

(
\Sigma
-4)

for
(s,s^{\prime})\in\Sigma
the diagram of
\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}
at
(0,0)
is either regular, or has a codimension 1 singularity.

(
\Sigma
-5)

The function
(s,s^{\prime})\mapsto s^{\prime}
restricts to a Morse function on
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and on
\Sigma^{-}
..

Lemma 6.11
.

Any two-parameter family
\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
can be
C^{\infty}
-perturbed to a family that is regular at infinity.

Proof.

The condition that
\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}
has
a singularity at
(0,0)
of codimension 2 or more is of codimension at least
3
in
\mathcal{F}
: in fact, to the standard dimension count behind
the decomposition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{F}^{1}
, we add one for an extra condition that the singularity occurs at
(0,0)
. This shows

(

Σ

-3)
.

Next, consider the space
\widetilde{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{S}\times[0,1]^{2}
given by
(t,s,s^{\prime})
such that
\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}(t)=0
.
This space can be regarded as the preimage
\Phi^{-1}(0,0)
under the map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{S}\times[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\Phi(t,s,s^{\prime})=\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}(t)
. Take
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{-}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and let
\Phi^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\Phi
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
(it is well-defined,
because
\Phi
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant with respect to the
t
variable).
Also, let
\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
be the restriction of
\Phi
to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
. On perturbing
\Phi
, we may assume that
(0,0)
is a regular
value of both
\Phi^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
. Write
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}
as preimages. These are clearly smooth 1-dimensional manifolds, and
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
has no boundary. Let
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
be the projection of
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
to
[0,1]^{2}
under the projection map
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\times[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]^{2}
, likewise
we let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the projection of
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}
to
[0,1]^{2}
. By equivariance of
\Phi
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
agrees with
\Sigma^{-}
.

For a dense subset of functions
\Phi
, the projections
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\Sigma^{-}
are regular in the sense of Definition
2.13
. That is,
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\Sigma^{-}
have at most double points as singularities. Moreover, if the projection is regular,
the only singularities of the intersection of
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\Sigma^{-}
are transverse double points proving
(

Σ

-3)
.

We claim that
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
has no double points. Suppose it has at
(s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0})
. As
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is smooth, a double point corresponds
to the pair
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
such that
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
and
\phi^{\iota}_{s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}}(t_{i})=(0,0)
. But then,
\widetilde{\phi}_{s_{0},s^{\prime}_{0}}
hits the infinity point at two values,
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
. This leads to a contradiction, proving
(

Σ

-1)
.

Next, suppose that
(s_{n},s_{n}^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{-}
converges to
(s_{0},s_{0}^{\prime})
that is not in
\Sigma^{-}
. By construction, there exists
a sequence
t_{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
such that
\Phi(t_{n},s_{n},s_{n}^{\prime})=(0,0)
. On passing to a subsequence, we assume that
t_{n}
converges to
t_{0}
.
Clearly
\Phi(t_{0},s_{0},s_{0}^{\prime})=(0,0)
.
If
t_{0}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
, then
(s_{0},s_{0}^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{-}
, contradiction. Hence,
t_{0}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, so
(s_{0},s_{0}^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, proving
(

Σ

-2)
.

Finally, we prove
(

Σ

-5)
. We show more: on perturbing
\Phi
, we may assume that the functions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
,
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\times[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
given by

(t,s,s^{\prime})\mapsto s^{\prime}
restrict to Morse functions on
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, respectively. This proves
(

Σ

-5)
,
and also gives insight on what is the Morse condition on the set of double points of
\Sigma^{-}
, namely,
the function restricts to a Morse function
on each branch through a double points. Also, we may assume that there are no local minima or maxima at these double points.
∎

Suppose now
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,s^{\prime}}
is regular at infinity. Fix
s^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. As
s
goes from
0
to
1
, the path

\gamma_{s}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]^{2}
,
\gamma_{s}=(s,s^{\prime})
can intersect
\Sigma^{-}\cup\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
. Each intersection correspond either to an
S
-move
or an
I
-move, or to a Reidemeister move at infinity. There is a partial translation of the intersection type and the type of bifurcation
that occurs.

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects
\Sigma^{-}
transversely at a point disjoint from the closure of
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
,
then this corresponds to either to an S-move along the path
s\mapsto\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
;

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
transversely at a point disjoint from
\Sigma^{-}
,
then this corresponds either to an I-move along the path
s\mapsto\phi_{s,s^{\prime}}
, or to an M-2 move (for finitely many values of
s^{\prime}
);

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects a smooth point of
\Sigma^{-}
non-transversally, then this can be either a cancellation of a pair of S-moves, or a tangency at infinity (R-2 move);

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects a smooth point of
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
non-transversally, then this is a cancellation of a pair of I-moves;

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects a double point of
\Sigma^{-}
, then
\phi^{\iota}_{s,s^{\prime}}
has two pairs of branches intersection
(0,0)
.
This leads to the M-3 move at infinity;

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects a point in
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
intersected with a point in the relative interior of
\Sigma^{-}
, then
this is a move involving one fixed branch and a pair of branches interchanged by
\tau
, but the double points do not disappear.
This is the M-1 move;

•

If
\gamma_{s}
intersects a point of the relative boundary of
\Sigma^{-}
(this point is necessarily on
\Sigma^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, then
this corresponds to a Reidemeister move where a double point disappears. This is the R-1 move.

7.
Two-parameter deformations of equivariant link diagrams

We now lay the groundwork for the proof of Theorem
1.3
by studying two-parameter deformations of equivariant link diagrams. We have seen in Section
6
that equivariant Reidemeister moves arise from studying one-parameter families of maps which cross
\mathcal{F}^{1}
. The movie moves of Theorem
1.3
will analogously arise from understanding two-parameter families of maps. Explicitly, a two-parameter family of maps will not stay within
\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}
, but will intersect the codimension
2
subset

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\mathcal{F}\setminus(\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}).

We thus begin by identifying a subset
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{2}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}
whose complement in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
has codimension
3
. This will consist of the mildest possible singularities within
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}
. Any two-parameter family of maps can then be perturbed such that all intersections with
\smash{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}
occur at points of
\mathcal{F}^{2}
.

Once again, each of these intersections corresponds to a codimension
2
singularity which is sufficiently constrained so that we can write down a normal form and versal deformation. This provides a local model for how our two-parameter family crosses
\mathcal{F}^{2}
. As described in Example
4.19
, such a versal deformation is parameterized over the disk, with the center of the disk giving the codimension
2
singularity. The discriminant locus of the deformation will consist of arcs radiating out from the center of the disk, along which our family has codimension
1
singularities. Traveling around the boundary of the disk gives a cyclic sequence of Reidemeister moves, with each move coming from crossing one of the arcs in the discriminant locus. These are precisely the sequences from Theorem
1.3
.

The general strategy will be to show that any codimension
2
singularity lies in one of
18
individual codimension
2
strata
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{i}
. (There is also an additional stratum
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{0}
corresponding to coincidence of codimension
1
singularities.) We then simply define

\mathcal{F}^{2}=\mathcal{F}^{2}_{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{2}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{F}^{2}_{18}.

These are enumerated with the help of Section
5
, which we also use to provide normal forms and versal deformations. The bulk of this section will thus consist of understanding the two-parameter bifurcation diagram in each case.

7.1.
Towards a classification of codimension 2 singularities

To begin with, we categorize potential codimension 2 singularities into several groups:

•

Off-axis singularities, see Section
7.2
;

•

On-axis or fixed-point cusps, see Section
7.3
;

•

On-axis or fixed-point strikethroughs, see Section
7.4
;

•

On-axis or fixed-point tangencies, see Section
7.5
;

•

Coincidences of two codimension 1 singularities.

Coincidences of singularities are easily understood and we treat them directly in the proof of Theorem
7.7
below. We treat off-axis cusps, strikethroughs, and tangencies together in Section
7.2
, since this case is the same as in the non-equivariant setting, and is thus straightforward.

The following theorem asserts that all codimension
2
singularities are of one of the forms discussed in Section
5
, except for one special case. This occurs when a fixed-point branch passes through an on-axis line tangency. We have not covered this possibility in Section
5.2
, as we prefer not to give the general theory of strikethroughs by fixed-point branches. We subsume this case in Section
7.5
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Each singularity with
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
that is not a coincidence is either:

(S-1)

an off-axis singularity;

(S-2)

an on-axis or fixed-point cusp;

(S-3)

an on-axis or fixed-point (ordinary) strikethrough;

(S-4)

an on-axis or fixed-point tangency;

or the special case:

(S-5)

a fixed-point branch of
\phi
intersecting an on-axis line tangency of order
1
.

Proof.

If the singularity occurs at two distinct points in the codomain, then it is a coincidence. Thus, suppose we have a codimension
2
singularity occurring at
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If
w\notin\mathcal{L}
, then the singularity is off-axis and we are in the situation
(S-1)
. Thus we assume
w\in\mathcal{L}
. Let the corresponding domain points be
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
and their orbits. We proceed by analyzing the possible values of
n
, together with what possibilities there are for the
t_{i}
(i.e., whether they lie in
\mathcal{S}^{-}
or
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
). As usual, we speak of
\phi
as having
n
branches, even though if
t_{i}\neq\sigma t_{i}
the branch through
t_{i}
generates a symmetric copy.

Firstly, note that at most one of the
t_{i}
lies in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, since different points of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
are mapped to different points of
\mathcal{L}
. Let
f
be the number of
t_{i}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, so that either
f=0
or
f=1
. The coincidence
\phi(t_{1})=\phi(t_{2})=\cdots=\phi(t_{n})
constitutes
2n-2
equations. We require that these lie on
\mathcal{L}
; this gives one extra equation if
f=0
and is automatic if
f=1
. The dimension of the domain is given by
n-f
. Hence the parametric codimension, even before imposing extra conditions like tangency or the singularity of branches, is given by
(2n-2)+(1-f)-(n-f)=n-1
. Thus
n
is at most
3
.

If
n=3
, then to have
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
there must be no additional conditions. Since at least one branch is through a point of
\mathcal{S}^{-}
, our singularity is a strikethrough of an on-axis or fixed-point singularity with two branches and
\operatorname{pcodim}=1
. This means our overall singularity is a strikethrough of an on-axis double point or a fixed double point. In either case, we are in the setting of
(S-3)
.

If
n=2
, then to have
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
we must have exactly one additional condition. One possibility is that this condition is between the two branches; i.e., the two branches are tangent. This leads to an on-axis or fixed-point tangency, and we are in case
(S-4)
. The other possibility is that the additional condition induces a singularity within a single branch (or between a branch and its symmetric copy). If the nonsingular branch is through a point of
\mathcal{S}^{-}
, then our singularity is a strikethrough of an on-axis or fixed-point singularity with one branch and
\operatorname{pcodim}=1
. This means our overall singularity is a strikethrough of an on-axis line tangency, on-axis perpendicular tangency, or fixed-point cusp. In each case we are in the setting of
(S-3)
. It is also possible that the nonsingular branch is a fixed-point branch. In this case, our singularity must consist of a fixed-point branch intersecting an on-axis tangency. If this is a line tangency, then we have the special case
(S-5)
; if it is a perpendicular tangency, then in fact the branches form a fixed-point tangency and we are in the case of
(S-4)
.

Finally, if
n=1
, then to have
\operatorname{pcodim}=2
we need exactly two additional conditions. If the branch is through a point of
\mathcal{S}^{-}
, then we have a higher-order on-axis cusp or higher-order on-axis tangency; these are cases
(S-2)
and
(S-4)
. If the branch is through a point of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, then we have a higher-order fixed-point cusp; this is case
(S-2)
.
∎

7.2.
Off-axis singularities

The enumeration of codimension
2
off-axis singularities is essentially the same as in the non-equivariant case. It follows from our discussion in Section
5
(see also
[
Dav83
,
Wal08
]
) that these consist of:

•

off-axis
(2,5)
-cusp, called the
A_{4}
singularity;

•

off-axis
2
-fold tangency, called the
A_{5}
singularity;

•

ordinary strikethrough of an off-axis
(2,3)
-cusp;

•

ordinary strikethrough of an off-axis
1
-fold tangency;

•

ordinary strikethrough of an off-axis ordinary triple point (resulting in an off-axis quadruple point), called the
X_{9}
singularity.

We display these in Figure
7.1
. The bifurcation diagrams are described in
[
FK10
]
, but for completeness we recall these here. Throughout, we alter various conventions throughout Section
5
to more closely match
[
FK10
]
.

Figure 7.1.
Off-axis singularities. From left-to-right: a
(2,5)
-cusp,
2
-fold tangency, strikethrough of a
(2,3)
-cusp, strikethrough of a
1
-fold tangency, and quadruple point.

7.2.1.
(2,5)
-cusp

By Lemma
5.21
, a
(2,5)
-cusp has normal form
(x=t^{5},y=t^{2})
and versal deformation
(x=t^{5}+\lambda_{1}t^{3}+\lambda_{2}t,y=t^{2})
. We explain the changes in the topology of the curves in this family by first finding the discriminant set. This procedure will be used for the remaining 17 cases of codimension 2 singularities.

We check for which values of
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
this map has a codimension
1
singularity. In this case, the only possibilities are an off-axis cusp, off-axis tangency, or off-axis triple point. For cusps: a
(2,3)
-cusp occurs along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
.
4
4

4

If in addition
\lambda_{1}=0
, this further degenerates to the
(2,5)
-cusp. Throughout this section, we abuse language slightly and implicitly assume
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are not simultaneously zero.
For tangencies: we start by understanding when our deformation has a self-intersection. Suppose
x(t)=x(t^{\prime})
and
y(t)=y(t^{\prime})
. The second equality forces
t=-t^{\prime}
; the first equality then gives
x(t)=0
. A
1
-fold tangency will occur for values of
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
at which
x(t)
has a double real root, which is equivalent to
t^{4}+\lambda_{1}t^{2}+\lambda_{2}
having a double real root. This happens along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}^{2}=4\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
. For triple points: we see that there are none, since
y(t)=y(t^{\prime})=y(t^{\prime\prime})
implies that two out the three parameters
t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
are equal.

The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.2
, see also
[
FK10
, Figure 8v]
.
5
5

5

There is a slight formal mistake in the figure in
[
FK10
]
due to the omission of the condition
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
accompanying the relation
\lambda_{1}^{2}=4\lambda_{2}
.
The parameters
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
vary over the disk bounded by the blue circle, which we take to be
\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=\varepsilon
for some small
\varepsilon
. We indicate the discriminant set (i.e., the set of parameters for which a singularity occurs) in red. This consists of
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
and
\{\lambda_{1}^{2}=4\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
. Note that the discriminant set intersects the boundary of the disk in a finite number of points and divides it into a collection of arcs. At each such point, we draw a singular diagram (circled in red), while for each arc, we draw a regular diagram (circled in blue) for a representative point within that arc.

As we travel around the central circle, we go from regular diagram to regular diagram by passing through the indicated singular diagrams. Two regular diagrams on either side of a singular diagram are related by the equivariant Reidemeister move associated to that singular diagram. Crossing the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
corresponds to an
(IR-1)
move, while crossing the locus
\{\lambda_{1}^{2}=4\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
corresponds to an
(IR-2)
move.

Figure 7.2.
Off-axis
(2,5)
-cusp.

7.2.2.
2
-fold tangency

By Lemma
5.35
, a
2
-fold tangency has normal form with two branches
y=0
and
(x=t,y=t^{3})
and versal deformation which perturbs the second branch to
(x=t,y=t^{3}+\lambda_{1}t+\lambda_{2})
.

There are no cusps or triple points regardless of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
.
A
1
-fold tangency will occur if
y(t)
has a double root, which occurs along the locus
\{4\lambda_{1}^{3}=-27\lambda_{2}^{2}\}
and leads to an
(IR-2)
move.
The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.3
, see also
[
FK10
, Figure 8iv]
.

Figure 7.3.
Off-axis
2
-fold tangency.

7.2.3.
Strikethrough of a
(2,3)
-cusp

This has normal form with two branches
y=ax
and
(x=t^{3},y=t^{2})
for some
a\neq 0
. We can rescale our coordinates so that
a=1
. By Theorem
5.11
, a versal deformation is given by
y=x+\lambda_{1}
and
(x=t^{3}-\lambda_{2}t,y=t^{2})
.

For
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, the cusp persists, leading to an
(IR-1)
move. For
\lambda_{2}<0
, the second branch is injective, while for
\lambda_{2}\geq 0
, there is a double point for
t=\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{2}}
at
(0,\lambda_{2})
. Along
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}\geq 0\}
, the strikethrough line passes through this double point, leading to a triple point and hence an
(IR-3)
move. Finally, as computed in
[
FK10
, Lemma 4.4]
, along
\{3\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=0\}
we have a tangency of the strikethrough line to the cusp branch, leading to an
(IR-2)
move. The diagram is given in Figure
7.4
, see also
[
FK10
, Figure 8iii]
.

Figure 7.4.
Strikethrough of an off-axis
(2,3)
-cusp.

7.2.4.
Strikethrough of a
1
-fold tangency

This has normal form with three branches
y=0
,
(x=t,y=t^{2})
, and
y=ax
for some
a\neq 0
. We can rescale our coordinates so that
a=1
. By Theorem
5.11
, a versal deformation is given by sending the second and third branches to
(x=t,y=t^{2}+\lambda_{1})
and
y=x+\lambda_{2}
.

There are no cusps. Along
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
the tangency between the first and second branches persists, leading to an
(IR-2)
move. The double points between the first and second branches occur at
(\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}},0)
; a triple point occurs when the strikethrough line passes through one of these. This occurs along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
, leading to an
(IR-3)
move. The diagram is given in Figure
7.5
, see also
[
FK10
, Figure 8ii]
.

Figure 7.5.
Strikethrough of an off-axis
1
-fold tangency.

7.2.5.
Strikethrough of a triple point

This case is not quite covered by Theorem
5.11
since the ordinary triple point is not an unbalanced homogenous singularity in the sense of Definition
5.10
. We have a normal form given by
x=0
,
y=0
,
y=x
, and
y=ax
, where
a\neq 0
or
1
; compare
[
Dav83
,
FK10
]
. However, this time the parameter
a
cannot be removed by a local coordinate change. It is not difficult to check that a versal deformation of the singularity is given by fixing the first two branches and deforming the last two to
y=x+\lambda_{1}
and
y=ax+\lambda_{2}
. However, this set of parameters is really 3-dimensional, because of the failure of simplicity: strictly speaking, the parameters are
(a,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

We can bypass this difficulty by freezing the parameter
a
. That is, if we have any two-dimensional deformation of the four-tuple point, then by versality it is induced
from the above deformation. Thus, there exist functions
\lambda_{1}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
,
\lambda_{2}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
,
a(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})

such that the deformation is given by
y=x+\lambda_{1}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
and
y=a(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})x+\lambda_{2}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
, where

\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}
are the deformation parameters. We assume without loss of generality that the four-tuple point occurs when
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=0
and nowhere else;
that is
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
vanish only at
(0,0)
. We can then modify the deformation in such a way that
a
is constant near
(0,0)
.

Although in principle the behavior of the deformation may depend on the constant value of
a
, in this case it is not difficult to see that different values of
a
result in essentially the same picture. We display the case of
a=-1
. There are no cusps or tangencies, because all the branches are straight lines.
It is straightforward to check that triple points occur along the loci
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
,
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
,
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\}
, and
\{\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}\}
, leading to
(IR-3)
moves.
The diagram is given in Figure
7.6
, see also
[
FK10
, Figure 8i]
.

Figure 7.6.
Off-axis quadruple point.

7.3.
On-axis and fixed-point cusps

By Lemma
5.20
, the on-axis and fixed-point cusps with codimension
2
are:

•

on-axis oblique
(2,3)
-cusp;

•

fixed-point
(2,5)
-cusp;

•

fixed-point
(3,4)
-cusp.

We display these in Figure
7.7
.

Figure 7.7.
On-axis and fixed-point cusps. From left-to-right:
an oblique
(2,3)
-cusp, fixed-point
(2,5)
-cusp, and fixed-point
(3,4)
-cusp.

7.3.1.
Oblique
(2,3)
-cusp

By Lemma
5.22
, an oblique
(2,3)
-cusp has normal form given by
(x=t^{2},y=t^{3}+t^{2})
and versal deformation

x(t)=t^{2}+\lambda_{1},\ y(t)=t^{3}+t^{2}+\lambda_{2}t+\lambda_{1}.

Note that the deformation of the symmetric branch is given by

x(s)=-s^{2}-\lambda_{1},\ y(s)=s^{3}+s^{2}+\lambda_{2}s+\lambda_{1}.

Along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, the cusp persists as a pair of off-axis cusps, leading to an
(IR-1)
move. As in Section
7.2.1
, to determine when other singularities occur, we start by understanding when multiple points occur in our deformation. Now, however, we must consider both when the cusp branch intersects itself, and when it intersects its symmetric copy. This is due to the fact that our singularity is no longer far away from
\mathcal{L}
.

To solve for a self-intersection, set
x(t)=x(t^{\prime})
and
y(t)=y(t^{\prime})
with
t\neq t^{\prime}
; this results in
t=\pm t^{\prime}
from the first equation and
t^{3}+\lambda_{2}t=0
from the second. Whenever
\lambda_{2}\leq 0
, we thus have a single self-intersection

(7.2)

t=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{2}}\ \text{at }(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}).

If this self-intersection is on
\mathcal{L}
, then we obtain an on-axis double point; this occurs along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}\leq 0\}
and results in an
(M-3)
move. Moreover, (
7.2
) may contribute to a triple point if the
s
-branch passes through the point of self-intersection. This occurs if
s^{2}=\lambda_{2}-2\lambda_{1}
and
s^{2}(s+1)+\lambda_{2}s+\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
, which implies

(2\lambda_{2}-2\lambda_{1})(s+1)=0.

There are two ways this can be satisfied. The first is when
s=-1
, in which case substituting back gives
1=\lambda_{2}-2\lambda_{1}
. This does not occur for small values of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
. The second is when
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
, but this has already been covered by the previous discussion.

Now consider when the two symmetric branches intersect each other. Setting
x(t)=x(s)
and
y(t)=y(s)
, this occurs when

(7.3)

t^{2}+s^{2}=-2\lambda_{1},\ (t-s)(t^{2}+ts+s^{2}+t+s+\lambda_{2})=0.

One obvious solution to the second equation occurs when
t=s
, in which case we have two points of intersection

t=s=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}}\ \text{at }(0,\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}}(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}))

so long as
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
. These occur on
\mathcal{L}
and may contribute to the formation of a codimension
1
singularity in the following manner: if the two points of intersection collide, then we obtain a tangency between the two branches. This occurs if either
\lambda_{1}=0
or
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
. The latter case has already been considered. The former induces a line tangency along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
, leading to an
(R-2)
move.

The other way to solve (
7.3
) is for the second factor of the second equation to vanish. Setting
p=t+s
,
q=ts
turns this into

(7.4)

p^{2}-2q=-2\lambda_{1},\ p^{2}-q+p=-\lambda_{2}.

Solving the second equation for
q
in terms of
p
and the substituting this into the first gives

(7.5)

q=p^{2}+p+\lambda_{2},\ p^{2}+2p+2(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})=0.

From the second equation, we see that for small
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
, we always have two distinct solutions for
p
. However, each solution in
p
and
q
generally corresponds to two solutions in
t
and
s
, as interchanging the values of
t
and
s
does not change
p
or
q
. Hence a tangency will arise whenever a solution to (
7.4
) in
p
and
q
corresponds to a solution with
t=s
, as this reflects a pair of solutions degenerating into a single solution. This will happen when
p^{2}=4q
. Plugging
p^{2}=4q
back into (
7.4
) gives
q=-\lambda_{1}
,
p=3\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
, and plugging these back into
p^{2}=4q
gives
(3\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}=-4\lambda_{1}
. It is then straightforward to check that we have an on-axis perpendicular tangency along
\{\lambda_{2}=3\lambda_{1}\pm 2\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
, leading to a
(M-2)
move.

The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.8
. As the curve
\lambda_{2}=3\lambda_{1}\pm 2\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}}
is very close
to the vertical line, for the reader’s convenience we zoom in on a portion of the diagram in Figure
7.9
.

Figure 7.8.
Oblique (2,3)-cusp.

Figure 7.9.
Close-up picture for
\lambda_{1}\sim 0
and
\lambda_{2}\sim-0.23
.

7.3.2.
Fixed-point
(2,5)
-cusp

By Lemma
5.25
, a fixed-point
(2,5)
-cusp has normal form
(x=t^{5},y=t^{2})
and versal deformation
(x=t^{5}+\lambda_{1}t^{3}+\lambda_{2},y=t^{2})
. The analysis is the same as for the off-axis
(2,5)
-cusp, except that the
(IR-1)
and
(IR-2)
moves are replaced with the
(R-1)
and
(R-2)
moves, respectively. The diagram is given in Figure
7.10
.

Figure 7.10.
Fixed-point
(2,5)
-cusp.

7.3.3.
Fixed-point
(3,4)
-cusp

By Lemma
5.26
, a fixed-point
(3,4)
-cusp has normal form
(x=t^{3},y=t^{4})
and versal deformation
(x=t^{3}+\lambda_{1}t,y=t^{4}+\lambda_{2}t^{2})
.

Setting the derivative equal to zero, we see that there are two possibilities for cusps. First, if
\lambda_{1}=0
, then there is a cusp when
t=0
, at the origin. Hence along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
, we have an on-axis
(2,3)
-cusp, leading to an
(R-1)
move. Second, along the locus
\{2\lambda_{1}=3\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
, we have a pair of off-axis cusps when
t=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}/3}=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{2}/2}
, leading to an
(IR-1)
move.

To determine the occurrence of other singularities, we first understand how our branch intersects itself. Setting
p=t+t^{\prime}
,
q=tt^{\prime}
gives the identities

t^{2}-{t^{\prime}}^{2}=(t-t^{\prime})p,\ t^{3}-{t^{\prime}}^{3}=(t-t^{\prime})(p^{2}-q),\ t^{4}-{t^{\prime}}^{4}=(t-t^{\prime})(p^{3}-2pq).

Substituting these into
x(t)=x(t^{\prime})
and
y(t)=y(t^{\prime})
yields

(7.6)

p^{2}-q+\lambda_{1}=0,\ p^{3}-2pq+\lambda_{2}p=0.

Factor the second equation into
p(p^{2}-2q+\lambda_{2})=0
and substitute
p^{2}=q-\lambda_{1}
into this to get
p(-q-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})=0
. We obtain two classes of solutions corresponding the two factors of this expression vanishing. Combining these with the first equation of (
7.6
) gives

p=0\quad\text{and}\quad q=\lambda_{1}

for the first, and

q=-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad p^{2}=\lambda_{2}-2\lambda_{1}

for the second. In the latter case, we also require
\lambda_{2}-2\lambda_{1}\geq 0
.

These self-intersections can contribute to the formation of a codimension
1
singularity in several different ways. First, if a solution to (
7.6
) in
p
and
q
corresponds to a solution where
t
or
t^{\prime}
is
0
, then we have a fixed double point. For the first class of solution, this occurs when
\lambda_{1}=0
, which is subsumed by our casework for cusps. For the second, this occurs when
-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=0
. Hence we obtain a fixed double point along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}\leq 0\}
, leading to an
(M-1)
move. Second, if a solution to (
7.6
) in
p
and
q
additionally satisfies
p^{2}=4q
, then it leads to a tangency, as explained in Section
7.3.1
. Combining
p^{2}=4q
with the first class of solution to (
7.6
) gives
\lambda_{1}=0
, while combining
p^{2}=4q
with the second gives
2\lambda_{1}=3\lambda_{2}
. Both of these are subsumed by our casework for cusps.

However, there is one more possibility for a tangency. This occurs when the two classes of solutions in
p
and
q
collide; i.e., when
\lambda_{2}-2\lambda_{1}=0
. Note that in this case we require
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
, so that
p=t+t^{\prime}=0
and
q=tt^{\prime}=\lambda_{1}
have solutions in
t
,
t^{\prime}
. It is then straightforward to check that we have an on-axis perpendicular tangency along the locus
\{2\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
, leading to an
(M-2)
move. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.11
.

Figure 7.11.
Fixed-point
(3,4)
-cusp.

7.4.
On-axis and fixed-point strikethroughs

By Section
5.2
, the on-axis and fixed-point strikethroughs with codimension
2
are:

•

strikethrough of an on-axis
1
-fold line tangency;

•

strikethrough of an on-axis
2
-fold perpendicular tangency;

•

strikethrough of an on-axis double point;

•

strikethrough of a fixed-point
(2,3)
-cusp;

•

strikethrough of a fixed double point.

These are shown in Figure
7.12
. To provide a deformation for a strikethrough singularity, we move the strikethrough line and separately deform the codimension 1 singularity. For unbalanced homogenous singularities, Theorem
5.11
shows that the resulting deformation is versal. For the two non-simple singularities (i.e., the strikethrough of an on-axis double point and strikethrough of a fixed double point), we use the procedure of freezing the slope of the strikethrough line, as described in Section
7.2.5
.

Figure 7.12.
On-axis and fixed-point strikethroughs. From left-to-right: strikethrough of a line tangency, strikethrough of a perpendicular tangency, strikethrough of an on-axis double point, strikethrough of a fixed-point cusp, and strikethrough of a fixed double point.

7.4.1.
Strikethrough of a
1
-fold line tangency

This has normal form
(x=t^{2},y=t)
and
y=ax
for some
a\neq 0
. Up to reparameterizing, we can assume
a=1
. A versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\{x=t^{2}+\lambda_{1},y=t\},\ E_{2}=\{y=x-\lambda_{2}\},

with symmetric branches

E_{3}=\tau(E_{1})=\{x=-t^{2}-\lambda_{1},y=t\},\ E_{4}=\tau(E_{2})=\{y=-x-\lambda_{2}\}.

For small values of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, the only tangency is between
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
. This is a line tangency, which persists along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
. If
\lambda_{1}>0
, then
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
are disjoint, leading to a regular diagram. For
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
, we see that
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
intersect
\mathcal{L}
together at
(0,\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}})
. The strikethrough line
E_{2}
passes through
(0,\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}})
when
\lambda_{2}=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}}
. Thus, along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}^{2}\}
we have an on-axis double point. The
diagram is drawn in Figure
7.13
.

Figure 7.13.
Strikethrough of a
1
-fold line tangency.

7.4.2.
Strikethrough of a
2
-fold perpendicular tangency

This has normal form
(x=t,y=t^{3})
and
y=ax
for some
a\neq 0
. Up to reparameterizing, we can assume
a=1
. A versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\{x=t,y=t^{3}+\lambda_{1}t\},\ E_{2}=\{y=x+\lambda_{2}\},

with symmetric branches

E_{3}=\tau(E_{1})=\{x=-t,y=t^{3}+\lambda_{1}t\},\ E_{4}=\tau(E_{2})=\{y=-x+\lambda_{2}\}.

For small values of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, the only tangency is between
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
. This is a perpendicular tangency, which persists along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
.
Next, along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, all four curves pass through the origin and we have an on-axis double point. This is the only singularity which involves the intersection of
E_{2}
with
E_{4}
. To search for triple points, we thus look for double points involving
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
. These intersect at the origin, as well as
(\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}},0)
, provided
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
. The line
E_{2}
passes through the latter if
\lambda_{2}=\mp\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}}
. Along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}^{2}\}
we thus obtain an off-axis triple point. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.14
.

Figure 7.14.
Strikethrough of a
2
-fold perpendicular tangency.

7.4.3.
Strikethrough of an on-axis double point

We assume the two branches involved in the double point are
y=a_{1}x
and
y=a_{2}x
, and the strikethrough line is
y=a_{3}x
.
By rescaling
x
and
y
, we can make
a_{1},a_{2}
arbitrary, as long as
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
,
but we cannot adjust
a_{3}
. For explicitness, we suppose
a_{1}=2.5
,
a_{2}=1
. The versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\{y=2.5x+\lambda_{1}\},\ E_{2}=\{y=x\},\ E_{3}=\{y=a_{3}x+\lambda_{2}\},

with symmetric branches

E_{4}=\tau(E_{1})=\{y=-2.5x+\lambda_{1}\},\ E_{5}=\tau(E_{2})=\{y=-x\},\ E_{6}=\tau(E_{3})=\{y=-a_{3}x+\lambda_{2}\}.

The deformation parameters are
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, and
a_{3}
. However, as in Section
7.2.5
, we can freeze the
a_{3}
variable, leading to
a_{3}
being constant. For explicitness, we take
a_{3}=0.4
, so that the slopes of the three lines are approximately
\pm 22.5^{\circ}
,
\pm 45^{\circ}
, and
\pm 67.5^{\circ}
. No matter what the parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are, each pair of lines intersects transversely.

The lines
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
intersect
\mathcal{L}
at the points
(0,\lambda_{1})
,
(0,0)
,

(0,\lambda_{2})
. Thus, along any of the loci
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
,
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, and
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\}
, we have an on-axis double point, where two out
of these three points coincide. This leads to an
(M-3)
move. Off-axis triple points occur when
E_{i}\cap E_{j}\cap E_{k}
is nonempty for
i\in\{1,4\},j\in\{2,5\}
, and
k\in\{3,6\}
. This leads to 8 cases, but by symmetry we may reduce to
4
. The conditions are easy to compute explicitly:
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}\neq\emptyset
along
\{\lambda_{2}=-\frac{2}{5}\lambda_{1}\}
;
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{6}\neq\emptyset
along
\{\lambda_{2}=-\frac{14}{15}\lambda_{1}\}
;
E_{1}\cap E_{5}\cap E_{3}\neq\emptyset
along
\{\lambda_{2}=\frac{2}{5}\lambda_{1}\}
; and
E_{1}\cap E_{5}\cap E_{6}\neq\emptyset
along
\{\lambda_{2}=\frac{6}{35}\lambda_{1}\}
. These give rise to
(IR-3)
moves. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.15
.

Figure 7.15.
Strikethrough of a central double point.

7.4.4.
Strikethrough of a fixed-point
(2,3)
-cusp

This has normal form
(x=t^{3},y=t^{2})
and
y=ax
for some
a\neq 0
. Up to reparameterizing, we can assume
a=1
. A versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\tau(E_{1})=\{x=t^{3}-\lambda_{2}t,\ y=t^{2}\},\ E_{2}=\{y=x+\lambda_{1}\},\ E_{3}=\tau(E_{2})=\{y=-x+\lambda_{1}\}.

Along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, the fixed-point cusp persists and results in an
(R-1)
move. The fixed-point branch
E_{1}
always intersects
\mathcal{L}
at
(0,0)
; if
E_{2}
passes through this, then we obtain a fixed double point. This occurs along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
, leading to an
(M-1)
move. So long as
\lambda_{2}\geq 0
, the fixed-point brach also intersects
\mathcal{L}
at
(0,\lambda_{2})
; if
E_{2}
passes through this, we have an on-axis double point. This occurs along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}\geq 0\}
, leading to an
(M-3)
move.

As for tangencies,
E_{2}
is transverse to
E_{3}
, so we consider tangencies between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. For this, we find intersections between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
and solve for a double root. Substituting the defining equations for
E_{1}
into that of
E_{2}
, we must find when
t^{3}-t^{2}-\lambda_{2}t+\lambda_{1}=0
has a double solution. Taking the derivative, this consists of the set of
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
such that
3t^{2}-2t-\lambda_{2}=0
and
t^{3}-t^{2}-\lambda_{2}t+\lambda_{1}=0
have a simultaneous solution. We obtain the parameterized curve

C=\{\lambda_{2}=3s^{2}-2s,\lambda_{1}=2s^{3}-s^{2}\}.

Along
C
, there is an off-axis tangency, leading to an
(IR-2)
move.

The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.16
. For small values of
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
, the curve
C
is very close to the
\lambda_{2}
-axis; the full curve
C
in fact loops back around and intersects the
\lambda_{1}
axis again, but we consider only the portion very close to the origin. We thus zoom in on the two singularities corresponding to the intersection of
C
with
\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=\varepsilon
. These are shown in Figures
7.17
and
7.18
.

Figure 7.16.
Strikethrough of a fixed-point
(2,3)
-cusp.

Figure 7.17.
Close-up picture for
\lambda_{1}\sim 0
and
\lambda_{2}\sim 0.2
.

Figure 7.18.
Close-up picture for
\lambda_{1}\sim 0
and
\lambda_{2}\sim-0.2
.

7.4.5.
Strikethrough of a fixed double point

We assume the branches involved in the fixed double point are
y=0
and
y=a_{1}x
, along with its symmetric copy
y=-a_{1}x
. Let the strikethrough line be
y=a_{2}x
.
By rescaling
x
and
y
, we can choose
a_{1}
, as long as
a_{1}\neq 0
and
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
, but we cannot fix
a_{2}
. For explicitness, we suppose
a_{1}=1/2
. A versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\tau(E_{1})=\{y=0\},\ E_{2}=\{y=0.5x+\lambda_{2}\},\ E_{3}=\{y=a_{2}x+\lambda_{1}\},

with symmetric branches

E_{4}=\tau(E_{2})=\{y=-0.5x+\lambda_{2}\},\ E_{5}=\tau(E_{3})=\{y=-a_{2}x+\lambda_{1}\}.

The deformation parameters are
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, and
a_{2}
. However, as in Section
7.2.5
, we can freeze the
a_{2}
variable, leading to
a_{2}
being constant. Such a deformation is normal to the stratum defining the strikethrough singularity. For explicitness, we assume
a_{2}=2
.

There are no tangencies or cusps, so we consider only multiple points. Along the loci
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
and
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, we have a fixed double point, leading to an
(M-1)
move. Along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\}
, we have an on-axis double point, leading to an
(M-3)
move. There are no other multiple points lying on
\mathcal{L}
. It is easily checked that
E_{1}
must be involved in any multiple point off of
\mathcal{L}
, so
y=0
. The intersection of
E_{2}
with
E_{1}
occurs at
x=-2\lambda_{1}
, while that of
E_{3}
with
E_{1}
occurs at
x=-\lambda_{2}/a_{2}
. We thus have an off-axis triple point along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=2a_{2}\lambda_{1}\}
. Replacing
E_{3}
by
E_{5}
gives a similar triple point locus
\{\lambda_{2}=-2a_{2}\lambda_{1}\}
. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.19
.

Figure 7.19.
Strikethrough of a fixed double point.

7.5.
On-axis and fixed-point tangencies

By Lemma
5.33
, the on-axis and fixed-point tangencies with codimension
2
are:

•

on-axis
2
-fold line tangency;

•

on-axis
4
-fold perpendicular tangency;

•

on-axis
1
-fold oblique tangency;

•

fixed-point
(1,2)
-fold tangency.

We also treat the exceptional case from Theorem
7.1
in this section:

•

a fixed-point branch of
\phi
intersecting an on-axis line tangency of order
1
.

We display these in Figure
7.12
.

Figure 7.20.
On-axis and fixed-point tangencies. From left-to-right:
2
-fold line tangency,
4
-fold perpendicular tangency,
1
-fold oblique tangency, fixed-point
(1,2)
-fold tangency, and a fixed-point branch intersecting a
1
-fold line tangency.

7.5.1.
2
-fold line tangency

By Lemma
5.36
, a
2
-fold line tangency has normal form
(x=t^{3},y=t)
and versal deformation
(x=t^{3}+\lambda_{1}t+\lambda_{2},y=t)
. The analysis is the same as for the off-axis
2
-fold tangency, except that the
(IR-2)
moves are replaced by
(R-2)
moves. The diagram is given in Figure
7.21
.

Figure 7.21.
2
-fold line tangency.

7.5.2.
4
-fold perpendicular tangency

By Lemma
5.37
, a
4
-fold perpendicular tangency has normal form
(x=t,y=t^{5})
and versal deformation
(x=t,y=t^{5}+\lambda_{1}t^{3}+\lambda_{2}t)
. Note this has symmetric branch
(x=-s,y=s^{5}+\lambda_{1}s^{3}+\lambda_{2}s)
.

Clearly, no cusps and triple points occur. Along
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
, the tangency persists as a
2
-fold perpendicular tangency, leading to an
(M-2)
move. To check for other tangencies, we observe that our branch intersects its symmetric copy only at points on the
x
-axis. Hence tangencies occur when
y(t)
and
y^{\prime}(t)
have a common root. This leads to

t^{4}+\lambda_{1}t^{2}+\lambda_{2}=0\quad\text{and}\quad 5t^{4}+3\lambda_{1}t^{2}+\lambda_{2}=0.

This implies
t^{4}+\lambda_{1}t^{2}=5t^{4}+3\lambda_{1}t^{2}
and hence
-2t^{2}=\lambda_{1}
. Substituting this back into the first equation gives
\lambda_{1}^{2}=4\lambda_{2}
. Along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}^{2}=4\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
we thus have an off-axis tangency, leading to an
(IR-2)
move. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.22
.

Figure 7.22.
4
-fold perpendicular tangency.

7.5.3.
1
-fold oblique tangency

Lemma
5.38
provides us with a normal form of an oblique tangency as well as with a versal deformation.
Choose coordinates
u=x+y
,
v=x-y
, so that
\mathcal{L}
is given by
u=v
and
\tau
exchanges
u
and
v
. A versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\{v=\lambda_{1}\},\ E_{2}=\{v=u^{2}+\lambda_{2}\}

with symmetric branches

E_{3}=\tau(E_{1})=\{u=\lambda_{1}\},\ E_{4}=\tau(E_{2})=\{u=v^{2}+\lambda_{2}\}.

There are clearly no cusps. For
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
, the tangency between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
persists, and for small values of
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
there are no other tangencies. Hence we obtain an off-axis tangency along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\}
, leading to an
(IR-2)
move. To understand further singularities, we first determine the intersection
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
. This is given by
(\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}},\lambda_{1})
, provided
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}
. If this lies on
\mathcal{L}
, we obtain an on-axis double point. Setting
\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}=\lambda_{1}
, this occurs along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}^{2}\}
, leading to an
(M-3)
move.

We now check for triple points. We see that
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}
is non-empty if
\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}=\lambda_{1}
, but this is subsumed by the previous paragraph. Likewise,
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{4}
is non-empty if
\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}=\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}
. Squaring both sides and applying various elementary manipulations gives the equivalent condition

(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{1}^{2})(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+1)=0.

The first factor vanishing is again subsumed by the previous paragraph, while the second factor does not vanish for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
small. There are no other singularities for small
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.23
.

Figure 7.23.
1
-fold oblique tangency.

7.5.4.
Fixed-point
(1,2)
-fold tangency

Lemma
5.43
allows us to find local coordinates in which a
(1,2)
-fold tangency has form with two symmetric branches
(x=t,y=t^{3})
and
(x=-t,y=t^{3})
, and fixed-point branch
(x=t,y=h(t))
, where
h(t)
is a symmetric function starting with
h(t)=h_{2}t^{2}+\dots
,
h_{2}\neq 0
. A deformation transverse to the defining set is given by

E_{1}=\{y=x^{3}+\lambda_{1}x+\lambda_{2}\},\ E_{2}=\tau(E_{1})=\{y=-x^{3}-\lambda_{1}x+\lambda_{2}\},\ E_{3}=\{y=h(x)\}.

Here
h(x)=h_{2}x^{2}+\dots
. On rescaling linearly
x
and
y
, we may assume that
h_{2}=1
.

As usual, we will describe the path of Reidemeister move as the deformation parameters go around the circle
\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=\varepsilon

for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. We will make the following simplification. Suppose we have proved that
for fixed
\varepsilon>0
, and
h(t)=t^{2}
, the
path intersects
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-stratum transversely. Then, adding higher order terms to
h
, and shrinking
\varepsilon
if needed, will not change the type of crossings on that path. Therefore, the case of general
h(t)
will be reduced to the case
h(t)=t^{2}
. This is what we will
henceforth assume.

Clearly, there are no cusps. Along
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
, the tangency between
E_{1}
and
E_{1}
persists; this is a perpendicular tangency and thus leads to an
(M-2)
move. To understand other singularities, we begin by understanding double points. The branches
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
intersect at
(0,\lambda_{2})
, as well as
(\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}},\lambda_{2})
if
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
. We consider when
E_{3}
intersects these. For the first double point this occurs at the origin along
\{\lambda_{2}=0\}
; this turns the singularity into a fixed double point and leads to an
(M-1)
move. For the latter two double points, this occurs when
\lambda_{2}=-\lambda_{1}
. We thus have an off-axis triple point along the locus
\{\lambda_{2}=-\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}\leq 0\}
, leading to an
(IR-3)
move.

Finally, we check for tangencies between
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
. This occurs when the equation
x^{3}+\lambda_{1}x+\lambda_{2}=x^{2}
has a double solution. Following the same procedure as in Section
7.4.4
yields the parameterized curve

C=\{\lambda_{1}=2s-3s^{2},\lambda_{2}=-s^{2}+2s^{3}\}.

It is straightforward to check that along
C
, we have a pair of off-axis tangencies, leading to an
(IR-2)
move. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.24
. For small values of
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
, the curve
C
is very close to the
\lambda_{2}
-axis. We thus zoom in on the two singularities corresponding to the intersection of
C
with
\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=\varepsilon
. These are shown in Figures
7.25
and
7.26
.

Figure 7.24.
Fixed-point
(1,2)
-tangency.

Figure 7.25.
Close-up picture for
\lambda_{1}\sim 0.2
and
\lambda_{2}\sim 0
.

Figure 7.26.
Close-up picture for
\lambda_{1}\sim-0.2
and
\lambda_{2}\sim 0
.

7.5.5.
Intersection of a fixed-point branch with
1
-fold line tangency

We now deal with the exceptional case from Theorem
7.1
, where a fixed-point branch passes through a
1
-fold line tangency. It is not difficult to check that this has normal form with tangency branch
(x=t^{2},y=t)
and fixed-point branch
y=0
, together with the symmetric branch
(-x=t^{2},y=t)
. A versal deformation is given by

E_{1}=\{x=y^{2}+\lambda_{1}\},\ E_{2}=\{y=\lambda_{2}\},\ E_{3}=\tau(E_{1})=\{x=-y^{2}-\lambda_{1}\}.

Along
\{\lambda_{1}=0\}
, the line tangency between
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
persists, leading to an
(R-2)
move. If
\lambda_{1}>0
, then
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
are disjoint, leading to a regular diagram. For
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
, we see that
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
intersect
\mathcal{L}
together at
(0,\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}})
. The fixed-point branch
E_{2}
passes through
(0,\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}})
when
\lambda_{2}=\pm\sqrt{-\lambda_{1}}
. Thus, along the locus
\{\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}^{2}\}
, we have an on-axis double point, leading to an
(M-1)
move. The diagram is drawn in Figure
7.27
.

Figure 7.27.
Intersection of a fixed-point branch with
1
-fold line tangency.

7.6.
Loops of Reidemeister moves

We now finally prove Theorem
1.3
from the introduction. As discussed at the beginning of this section, each of the
18
codimension
2
singularities explored above gives rise to a loop of Reidemeister moves. However, there is a slight distinction between
planar
Reidemeister moves and actual Reidemeister moves. The former refers to moves that relate two maps
\phi\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
; i.e., maps which do not have any overcrossing or undercrossing data. The latter refers to moves that relate two maps
\phi\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
with given lifts to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
; i.e., maps which have the overcrossing and undercrossings specified.

For clarity, we begin with a version of Theorem
1.3
which is purely for planar Reidemeister moves.

Theorem 7.7
.

Suppose we have two regular paths
\phi_{s,0}
and
\phi_{s,1}
between two regular maps
\phi_{0,0}=\phi_{0,1}
and
\phi_{1,0}=\phi_{1,1}
. By Theorem
6.4
, these give rise to two sequences of (planar) Reidemeister moves. We claim that one can pass from one sequence of Reidemeister moves to the other via a sequence of the following operations:

(C-1)

Replacing the order of two planar Reidemeister moves appearing in different places.

(C-2)

Introducing a birth or death of a pair of mutually inverse planar Reidemeister moves; that is, a planar Reidemeister move followed by its inverse.

(C-3)

Replacing a fragment of a local loop of planar Reidemeister moves from Table
2
by the complement of that fragment.

Off-axis
, Figure
7.1

(2,5)
-cusp

Fig.

(IR-1)
\mapsto
(IR-2)
\mapsto
(IR-1)
\mapsto\cdots

L-2

2
-fold tangency

Fig.
7.3

(IR-2)
\mapsto
(IR-2)
\mapsto\cdots

L-3

strikethrough of a
(2,3)
-cusp

Fig.
7.4

(IR-3)
\mapsto
(IR-2)
\mapsto
(IR-1)
\mapsto
(IR-2)
\mapsto
(IR-1)
\mapsto\cdots

L-4

strikethrough of a
1
-fold tangency

Fig.
7.5

(IR-2)
\mapsto
(IR-3)
\mapsto
(IR-3)
\mapsto
(IR-2)
\mapsto\cdots

L-5

four-tuple point

Fig.

loop of

(IR-3) moves

, Figure
