Table 2.
Local loops of equivariant Reidemeister moves. Each loop can be traversed in a positive (clockwise) or negative direction.

Proof.

We first observe that the two paths
\phi_{s,0}
and
\phi_{s,1}
are obviously path-homotopic via the linear homotopy

\phi_{s,t}=(1-t)\phi_{s,0}+t\phi_{s,1}

defined for
s,t\in[0,1]
. Note that each
\phi_{s,t}
is indeed an equivariant map from
\mathcal{S}
into
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We aim to perturb
\smash{\phi_{s,t}}
to be generic, but first we have to define the space
\mathcal{F}^{2}
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{0}
be the subset of maps in
\mathcal{F}
with a coincidence of two codimension 1 singularities. For
i=1,\dots,18
, let
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{i}
correspond to item (L-
i
) in Table
2
. These are the
18
codimension
2
singularities listed previously. We then set

\mathcal{F}^{2}=\mathcal{F}^{2}_{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{2}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{F}^{2}_{18}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{3}=\mathcal{F}\setminus(\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\mathcal{F}^{1}\cup\mathcal{F}^{2}).

By Theorem
7.1
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{3}
has codimension
3
. An argument similar to that of Theorem
6.2
then shows that we can perturb
\smash{\phi_{s,t}}
rel boundary so that it:

•

misses
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{3}
;

•

hits
\mathcal{F}^{2}
transversely at finitely many points;

•

intersects
\mathcal{F}^{1}
non-transversely at finitely many points.

We first show that each point of intersection with
\mathcal{F}^{2}
leads to a loop of Reidemeister moves. Let
(s_{0},t_{0})\in[0,1]^{2}
be such a point and write
\phi=\phi_{s_{0},t_{0}}
. First suppose
\phi
is not a coincidence, but rather lies in
\mathcal{F}^{2}_{j}
for
j>0
, corresponding to one of our
18
codimension
2
singularities.

Let
D
be a small disk around
(s_{0},t_{0})
. Then the pair
(\phi_{s,t},D)
as
(s,t)
varies over
D
is a deformation of
\phi
with
2
-dimensional base. If the singularity is simple, transversality implies that this deformation is versal. That is, it is induced from the deformation described in previous sections. Let
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
thus be coordinates as in Sections
7.2
through
7.5
and let
D^{\prime}
be an even smaller disk centered at
(s_{0},t_{0})
whose boundary corresponds to our small loop of parameters. Then we can understand the behavior of the path
\phi_{s,t}
as
t
crosses
t_{0}
as follows. The path
\phi_{s,t_{0}-\epsilon}
is homotopic to the path displayed in Figure
7.28
which has a segment that goes along the lower arc of
D^{\prime}
. Likewise, the path
\phi_{s,t_{0}+\epsilon}
is homotopic to the path in Figure
7.28
that goes along the upper arc of
D^{\prime}
. It is then clear that
\phi_{s,t_{0}-\epsilon}
and
\phi_{s,t_{0}+\epsilon}
are related by replacing a Reidemeister loop fragment with its complement.

Figure 7.28.
Proof of Theorem
7.7
. The two paths of Reidemeister moves differ by replacing a part of
\partial D^{\prime}

with the complement going in the opposite direction. The two arcs passing through the center of the disk represent the discriminant locus.

If the singularity is not simple, then a few more details are needed.
In each non-simple case instead of specifying a versal deformation, we produced a family transverse to the defining set
of singularity (as the singularity is not simple Theorem
4.20
does not apply). Each such family is a two-parameter family.
Whenever a family
\phi_{s,t}
hits the non-simple singularity at
(s_{0},t_{0})
, we locally replace
\phi_{t,s}
by the pre-defined
transverse family.

We illustrate this rather standard procedure of a strikethrough of an on-axis double point. Suppose
s_{0},t_{0}
is the parameter
at which
\phi_{s_{0},t_{0}}
acquires this singularity.

For a strikethrough of an on-axis double point, we have a 3-parameter versal deformation:
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, and
a
, where in all three cases
a
is the slope of the strikethrough line and
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0
corresponds to
\phi_{s_{0},t_{0}}
. By versality, for parameters
s,t
close to
s_{0},t_{0}
, the parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, and
a
can be regarded as function of
(s,t)
. In fact, as the multijet extension
of
\phi_{s,t}

is transverse to the defining equation
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0
, we may assume that
(s,t)\mapsto(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

is locally invertible near
(s_{0},t_{0})
. That is,
the functions
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
form a local coordinate
system near
(0,0)
. Replace the deformation in a neighborhood of the origin by freezing
a
(that is, letting
a
be constant near the origin). This can be done by an explicit formula that changes
\phi^{r}_{s}
locally.
Namely, let the points in the domain which are involved in the singularity be
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
. Then
\phi
near these points is given by

(t-t_{1},2.5(t-t_{1})+\lambda_{1}),\ (t-t_{2},0.4(t-t_{2})),\ (t-t_{3},a(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})(t-t_{3})+\lambda_{2}),

Write now the path

(t-t_{1},2.5(t-t_{1})+\lambda_{1}),\ (t-t_{2},0.4(t-t_{2})),\ (t-t_{3},a^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})(t-t_{3})+\lambda_{2}),

where

a^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=a(0,0)\eta(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})+a(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})(1-\eta(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})),

and
\eta
is a cutoff function, equal to
1
near
(0,0)
and vanishing away from
a small neighborhood of
(0,0)
.
It is routine to check that this change modifies each diagram by an isotopy. Once the parameter
a
has been frozen, the rest of the proof is as above. A similar procedure works for other non-simple singularities.

Now suppose
\phi
is a coincidence of two codimension 1 singularities
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
. Let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be as before. We have a versal deformation with parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
corresponding to
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
; this has discriminant locus
\{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=0\}
. Note that crossing the discriminant locus corresponds to doing the corresponding Reidemeister move. The resulting loop of Reidemeister moves consists of doing
S_{1}
, then
S_{2}
, and then undoing
S_{1}
, and then undoing
S_{2}
. Thus,
\phi_{s,t_{0}-\epsilon}
and
\phi_{s,t_{0}+\epsilon}
differ by replacing a fragment of this four-move loop with its complement. It is straightforward to check that this is some combination of
(C-1)
and
(C-2)
. For example, we can replace
S_{1}
-and-then-
S_{2}
with
S_{2}
-and-then-
S_{1}
; this is precisely
(C-1)
. Or, we can replace
S_{1}
-and-then-
S_{2}
-and-then-
S_{1}
-reverse with
S_{2}
; this can be regarded as first doing
(C-1)
and then canceling a pair of moves via
(C-2)
.

Finally, we show that each tangency between
\phi_{s,t}
and
\mathcal{F}^{1}
leads to a
(C-2)
move. As
\mathcal{F}^{1}
is a union of codimension 1 strata, it locally separates
\mathcal{F}
. Lack of transversality means that as
t
varies, the number of intersections of the path
\phi_{s,t}
with
\mathcal{F}^{1}
changes by
2
. This corresponds to a birth or death of a pair of Reidemeister moves, as drawn in Figure
7.29
.
∎

Figure 7.29.
A schematic of a path which is non-transverse to
\mathcal{F}^{1}
. The three vertical lines represent three paths in the function
space. The one to the right does not intersect the
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{7}
-stratum. The middle one is tangent to the stratum. Moving the path to the left
creates two transverse intersection points with the
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{7}
-stratum. Each of the two points corresponds to an (R-1) move.

Finally, we prove Theorem
1.3
from the introduction.

See
1.3

Proof.

The proof follows the pattern used in Corollary
2.16
and Corollary
2.21
.
Define
\phi_{s,t}=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t}
. Perturb
\phi_{s,t}
to
\phi_{s,t,n}
,
n=1,\dots
so that for each
n

\phi_{s,t,n}
is regular as a two-parameter family, that is,
it misses codimension 3 singularities and has only finitely many codimension 2 singularities. We assume that
\phi_{s,t,n}
converge,
as
n\to\infty
to
\phi_{s,t}
in the
C^{\infty}
topology. Set
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t,n}=\widetilde{\phi}_{t,s}-\phi_{t,s}+\phi_{t,s,n}
. Then,

\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t,n}
converges to
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t}
in the
C^{\infty}
topology, in particular, in the
C^{1}
-norm. That is, for sufficiently
large
n
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t,n}
is an embedding for all
s,t
.

Apply Theorem
7.7
to
\phi_{s,t,n}
. Note that the proof of Theorem
7.7

actually constructs the homotopy
\phi_{s,t,n}
. Here, we insist that the homotopy come from the path
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t,n}
.

As a consequence of Theorem
7.7
, we can connect
\phi_{0,t,n}
and
\phi_{1,t,n}
by a path of planar moves
(C-1)
–
(C-3)
. The data that
\phi_{s,t,n}
is actually a projection of the map
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t,n}
allows us to lift the planar loops to
loops of actual Reidemeister moves, because there is a consistent choice of which strand is a bridge and which strand is a tunnel.
∎

8.
Cobordisms of involutive links

It is a standard fact that a cobordism between knots can be decomposed into the sequence of elementary cobordisms.
The corresponding statement in the equivariant setting is less well-known. In this section we decompose equivariant cobordisms into “elementary” cobordisms by studying equivariant Morse functions.

8.1.
Introduction

Let
M
be a closed
m
-dimensional smooth manifold with a smooth involution
\tau\colon M\to M
such that
the fixed point set is an
n
-dimensional manifold

M^{\tau}=\operatorname{Fix}(\tau).

The following result is standard; we include it for completeness.

Lemma 8.1
.

Suppose
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
. There are local coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})
in a neighborhood of
u_{0}
such that

•

u_{0}=(0,\dots,0)
;

•

\tau(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})=(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})
, in particular,
M^{\tau}
is given by
x_{1}=\dots=x_{m-n}=0
.

Proof.

Consider the tangent space
V=T_{u_{0}}M
. As
u_{0}
is a fixed point, the derivative
of
\tau
acts as an automorphism on
V
. Call this action
T\colon V\to V
. Then
T^{2}=\operatorname{Id}
and so there is a basis

\widetilde{x}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{x}_{s},\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{m-s}
of
V
such that

T(\widetilde{x}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{x}_{s},\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{m-s})=(-\widetilde{x}_{1},\dots,-\widetilde{x}_{s},\widetilde{y}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{m-s}).

We need to show that
s=n
.
Choose an equivariant Riemannian metric on
M
. The map
\exp\colon V\to M
is a local diffeomorphism such that

\exp(T\widetilde{x})=\tau\exp(x)
. That is, there are open subsets
U_{V}\subset V
and
U_{M}\subset M

containing
0\in V
, respectively
u_{0}\in M
, such that
\exp\colon U_{V}\to U_{M}
is a diffeomorphism. On replacing
U_{V}

by
U_{V}\cap T(U_{V})
and
U_{M}
by
U_{M}\cap\tau(U_{M})
we may assume that
U_{V}
and
U_{M}
are
T
-invariant, respectively
\tau
-invariant.
The maps
\exp
being equivariant, takes
U_{V}\cap\operatorname{Fix}T
to
U_{M}\cap\operatorname{Fix}\tau
. In particular,
n=s
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n}
be the coordinates on
U_{M}
such that

\exp(\widetilde{x}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{m-n})=(x_{1},\dots,y_{m-n}).

These coordinates satisfy both items of Lemma
8.1
.
∎

To have a uniform notation, we sometimes write the coordinates as
z_{1},\dots,z_{m}
, with
z_{i}=x_{i}
for
i\leq n
and
z_{i}=y_{i-s}

for
i>n
. We set then

(8.2)

\delta_{i}=\begin{cases}-1&i\leq n\\
1&i>n.\end{cases}

With this notation, we write the coordinates of
\tau
as
(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{m})
, that is,
\tau(u)=(\tau_{1}(u),\dots,\tau_{m}(u))
. We have

(8.3)

\frac{\partial\tau_{j}}{\partial z_{i}}=\begin{cases}0&i\neq j\\
\delta_{i}&i=j.\end{cases}

Definition 8.4
.

A function
F\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
is
\tau
-invariant
(in short: invariant), if
F(\tau u)=F(u)
for all
u\in M
.
A function
F\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
is
anti-invariant
if
F(\tau u)=-F(u)
for all
u\in M
.

If
\delta\in\{\pm 1\}
we also say that
F
is
\delta
-invariant, if
F(\tau u)=\delta F(u)
.

Clearly, an anti-invariant function vanishes on
M^{\tau}
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Suppose
F
is invariant,
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
and
(x_{1},\dots,y_{n-s})
is a coordinate system in a neighborhood
U\subset M
of
u_{0}

as in Lemma
8.1
. Then:

(a)

for any
i=1,\dots,n
, the derivative
\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}
is anti-invariant;

(b)

for any
j=1,\dots,m-n
, the derivative
\frac{\partial F}{\partial y_{j}}
is invariant.

Proof.

We calculate

\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial z_{i}}\right|_{u}=\left.\frac{\partial F\circ\tau}{\partial z_{i}}\right|_{u}=\left.\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial z_{j}}\right|_{\tau u}\frac{\partial\tau_{j}}{\partial z_{i}}=\left.\delta_{i}\frac{\partial F}{\partial z_{i}}\right|_{\tau u}.

Here, the first equality is invariance of
F
. The second is the chain rule and third is (
8.3
). Hereafter, we use the notation

\frac{\partial F}{\partial z_{i}}|_{\tau u}
to emphasize the order of operations. This notation means ‘first differentiate, then substitute’.
The notation for the opposite operation is
\frac{\partial(F\circ\tau)}{\partial z_{i}}|_{u}
.

The displayed equation shows that the derivative is invariant if
\delta_{i}=1
, and anti-invariant if
\delta_{i}=-1
.
∎

Corollary 8.6
.

If
F
is invariant, then for any
u\in M^{\tau}
we have
\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{j}}(u)=0
.

Proof.

An anti-invariant function vanishes on the fixed point set.
∎

Our next result shows that the second derivative of
F
has a block structure. This will have important implications on Morse functions.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
F
be an invariant function,
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
and
(x_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})
coordinates as in Lemma
8.1

defined in an open set
U\ni u_{0}
. For any
u\in U\cap M^{\tau}
, the second derivative
D^{2}F(u)
has a block structure

D^{2}_{x}F(u)\oplus D^{2}_{y}F(u)
, where
D^{2}_{x}F(u)
is the matrix of derivatives over the
x
-variables,
D^{2}_{y}F(u)
is
the matrix of derivatives over the
y
-variables.

Proof.

The statement is equivalent to saying that
\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial x_{i}\partial y_{j}}(u)=0
for
u\in U\cap M^{\tau}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,

j=1,\dots,m-n
.

Note that by Lemma
8.5
, the derivative
\frac{\partial F}{\partial y_{i}}
is
\tau
-invariant. Therefore,
the derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}
is
anti-invariant, so by Corollary
8.6
, it vanishes on
U\cap M^{\tau}
.
∎

8.2.
Ambient equivariant Morse theory

Let
M
be a closed smooth
m
-dimensional
manifold endowed with a smooth involution
\tau\colon M\to M
, preserving orientation and such that
M^{\tau}

is a smooth
n
-dimensional manifold.

Definition 8.8
.

A smooth,
\tau
-invariant function
F\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
is called an
equivariant Morse function
, if the determinant
\det D^{2}F(u)
is
nonzero at any point
x
such that
DF(u)=0
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Suppose
F
is an equivariant Morse function. Set
f=F|_{M^{\tau}}
. Then
f
is a Morse function on
M^{\tau}
.

Proof.

Take
u\in M^{\tau}
, choose coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})
as in Lemma
8.1
.
Suppose
u
is a critical point of
f
. This amounts to saying that
\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}(u)=0

for all
i=1,\dots,m-n
. As
y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n}
are coordinates on
M^{\tau}
, this means that
\frac{\partial F}{\partial y_{i}}(u)=0

for all
i=1,\dots,m-n
. By Corollary
8.6
,
\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{j}}(u)=0
for
j=1,\dots,n
. This means
that
u
is a critical point of
F
.

By Lemma
8.7
, the second derivative
D^{2}F(u)
has a block structure
D^{2}F(u)=D^{2}_{x}F(u)\oplus D^{2}_{y}F(u)
.
The Morse condition implies that
\det D^{2}F(u)\neq 0
, but
\det D^{2}F(u)=\det D^{2}_{x}F(u)\cdot\det D^{2}_{y}F(u)
.
Hence,
\det D^{2}_{y}F(u)\neq 0
, but
D^{2}_{y}F(u)=D^{2}f(u)
. Hence,
u
is a Morse critical point.
∎

In the proof of Lemma
8.9
, we have proved the following result, which we state explicitly for future reference.

Corollary 8.10
.

A critical point
u\in M^{\tau}
of
f
is also a critical point of
F
.

We will now continue our study of equivariant Morse functions by recalling the equivariant Morse Lemma, proved
originally by Wassermann.

Theorem 8.11

(Equivariant Morse Lemma)
.

Let
F
be an equivariant Morse function. If
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
is a critical point, then there are coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n}

on
M
near
x_{0}
, such that
u_{0}=(0,0,\dots,0)
,
\tau(x_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})=(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n})
(in particular,

x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are coordinates normal to
M^{\tau}
,
y_{1},\dots,y_{m-n}
are coordinates on
M^{\tau}
. Furthermore,
in these coordinates

F(x_{1},\dots,y_{n-m})=F(u_{0})-x_{1}^{2}-\dots-x_{k}^{2}+x_{k+1}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}-\dots-y_{\ell}^{2}+y_{\ell+1}^{2}+\dots+y_{m-n}^{2}

for some integers
k
and
\ell
.

Proof.

The statement is proved in
[
Was69
, Lemma 4.1]
.
∎

Definition 8.12
.

The pair
(k,\ell)
is called the
multi-index
of a critical point. The number
k
is the
anti-invariant index
,
while
\ell
is called the
invariant index
.

The next result is also proved by Wassermann
[
Was69
, Density Lemma 4.8]
:

Theorem 8.13
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of functions
F\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
that are
\tau
-invariant and of class
C^{N}
for
N\geq 3
. Then, the subspace of equivariant Morse
functions is open-dense.

8.3.
Embedded equivariant Morse theory

Suppose
\Omega
is a closed,
m+k
-dimensional manifold,
k>0
,
and
M\subset\Omega
is a closed submanifold of dimension
m
.
We assume that there is an action
\tau\colon\Omega\to\Omega
with fixed point set
\Omega^{\tau}
, which is an
n
-dimensional manifold.
We assume that
M\cap\Omega^{\tau}
is a manifold of dimension
r<n
, but we do not insist that the intersection of
M
and
\Omega^{\tau}
be transverse.
The following notion generalizes the definition of
[
BP16
]
.

Definition 8.14
.

A smooth invariant function
F\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
embedded equivariant Morse
, in short EEM,
if it has the following properties

(EEM-1)

(Morse on
\Omega)

F
is a Morse function on
\Omega
;

(EEM-2)

(Morse on
M
) the restriction
f=F|_{M}
is Morse on
M
.

(EEM-3)

(genericity of position of critical points) all critical points of
F
are on
\Omega\setminus M
;

By convention, we will also refer to critical points of
F_{M}
occasionally as critical points of
F
, when it will not cause confusion.

Suppose
u_{0}
is a critical point of
F
or of
f=F|_{M}
.
To study the local behavior of
F
near
u_{0}
, we consider the following cases. We recall that
M^{\tau}
and
\Omega^{\tau}
denote

•

if
u_{0}\notin M
and
u_{0}\notin\Omega^{\tau}
, we use the standard Morse Lemma to find local behavior of
F
. Note, that the point
\tau u_{0}

is also a critical point of
F
;

•

if
u_{0}\notin M
, but
u_{0}\in\Omega^{\tau}
, we use the Equivariant Morse Lemma (Theorem
8.11
above);

•

if
u_{0}\in M
, but
u_{0}\notin\Omega^{\tau}
, we use the Embedded Morse Lemma
[
BP16
, Lemma 2.17]
. In this case,
\tau u_{0}
is another
critical point;

•

if
u_{0}\in M\cap\Omega^{\tau}
, we need an additional tool, that is, Equivariant Embedded Morse Lemma. The statement and proof follow promptly.

For a critical point
u_{0}
of
f
on
M\cap\Omega^{\tau}
, we have the following variant of Morse lemma.

Theorem 8.15

(Equivariant Embedded Morse Lemma)
.

Suppose
u_{0}\in M\cap\Omega^{\tau}
is a critical point of
f
.
There exists coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-r},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-r},w_{1},\dots,w_{s}
(with
n-r+s=k
)
on
\Omega
near
u_{0}
(i.e.
u_{0}=(0,\dots,0)
) such that

(EML-1)

\tau
is linear, that is,

\tau(x_{1},\dots,w_{s})=(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-r},-v_{1},\dots,-v_{n-r},w_{1},\dots,w_{s}).

(EML-2)

M
is given by
\{v_{1}=\dots=v_{n-r}=w_{1}=\dots=w_{s}=0\}
;

(EML-3)

\Omega^{\tau}
is given by
\{x_{1}=\dots=x_{r}=v_{1}=\dots=v_{n-r}=0\}
;

(EML-4)

the function
F
in these coordinates is given by:

F(x_{1},\dots,w_{s})=F(u_{0})-x_{1}^{2}-\dots-x_{a}^{2}+x_{a+1}^{2}+\dots+x_{r}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}-\dots-y_{b}^{2}+y_{b+1}^{2}+\dots+y_{m-r}^{2}+w_{1}.

Proof.

We first construct a local coordinate system refining the one of Lemma
8.1
. To this end, we
need to impose conditions on the Riemannian metric used to define the
\exp
map.

To this end, choose a neighborhood
U
of
u_{0}
in
\Omega
such that
U
is equivariantly diffeomorphic to
U_{M}\times U_{T}
,
where
U_{M}\subset M
and
U_{T}
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Such
U
exists by the equivariant tubular neighborhood theorem,
see
[
Bre72
, Theorem VI.2.2]
.
We can choose coordinates on
U_{M}
and on
U_{T}
separately using Lemma
8.1
. We call the coordinates on

M

x_{1},\dots,x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-r}
and the coordinates on
U_{T}
are
v_{1},\dots,v_{n-r},w_{1},\dots,w_{s}
. Conditions
(EML-1)
,

(EML-2)
, and
(EML-3)
are automatically satisfied.

It remains to prove
(EML-4)
. We will follow the proof of
[
BP16
, Lemma 2.17]
with adaptations to the equivariant case.
By Theorem
8.11
, we can make
an equivariant transformation to variables
x_{1},\dots,y_{n-r}
in such a way that
f=F|_{M}
has form

f(x_{1},\dots,y_{n-r})=f(x_{0})-x_{1}^{2}-\dots-x_{a}^{2}+x_{a+1}^{2}+\dots+x_{r}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}-\dots-y_{b}^{2}+y_{b+1}^{2}+\dots+y_{n-r}^{2}.

Next, the point
u_{0}
is not a critical point of
F
, thus, there exists either
i=1,\dots,m-r
or
j=1,\dots,s

such that
\frac{\partial F}{\partial v_{i}}(x_{0})\neq 0
or
\frac{\partial F}{\partial w_{j}}(x_{0})\neq 0
. The first option contradicts
Corollary
8.6
. Hence,
\frac{\partial F}{\partial v_{i}}(x_{0})\neq 0
for some
i
. On reindexing
variables, we may assume that
\frac{\partial F}{\partial w_{1}}(x_{0})\neq 0
.
Consider the map

\Phi(x_{1},\dots,y_{n-r},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,w_{1},\dots,w_{s})=(x_{1},\dots,y_{n-r},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,w_{1}^{\prime},\dots,w_{s}),

where

w_{1}=F(x_{1},\dots,w_{s})-(-x_{1}^{2}-\dots+y_{n-r}^{2})-F(u_{0}).

The map
\Phi
is equivariant. Moreover, by the implicit function theorem,
\Phi
is a local diffeomorphism. Furthermore,

\Phi
preserves
M
. In the
coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,y_{n-r},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,w_{1}^{\prime},w_{2},\dots,w_{s})
,
F
has the form
(EML-4)
.
∎

We now pass to proving openness and density of the space of Equivariant Embedded Morse functions. The result relies on
the density for ambient equivariant Morse functions, Theorem
8.13
, but some effort has to be made.

Let
N\geq 2
be an integer. We know that if
F,G\in C^{N}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
,
then
FG\in C^{N}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
. There is a constant
\Pi_{N}
, depending only on
\dim\Omega
and
N
, and on conventions
used to define the
C^{N}
-norms, such that

||FG||_{C^{N}}\leq\Pi_{N}||F||_{C^{N}}||G||_{C^{N}}
.

Theorem 8.16
.

Let
C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
be the space of
C^{N}
-smooth functions
F\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
F(\tau u)=F(u)
for all
u
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be the
subspace of functions satisfying conditions
(EEM-1)
–
(EEM-3)
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is open-dense in
C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Openness of each condition is straightforward. Fix an invariant metric on
\Omega
. Let
F
be an
\tau
-invariant function
on
\Omega
with bounded
C^{N}
-norm. Let
\varepsilon>0
. To prove Theorem
8.16
we need several lemmas.

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}\subset C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
be the subspace of functions satisfying
(EEM-1)
. Then,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is open-dense.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Theorem
8.13
.
∎

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{2}\subset C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
be the subspace of functions whose restriction to
M
is Morse (that is, which satisfy
(EEM-2)
). Then
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is open-dense.

Proof.

Openness is clear, we focus on density.
Suppose
F\in C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
.
Let
f=F|_{M}
. Choose an invariant tubular neighborhood
U
of
M
in
\Omega
(which exists by
[
Bre72
, Theorem VI.2.2]
). Fix an equivariant projection
\pi\colon\overline{U}\to M
. Let
\kappa_{N}
be the norm of the map
P_{\pi}\colon C^{N}(M)\to C^{N}(\overline{U})

given by
P_{\pi}(g)=g\circ\pi
.

Let
U^{\prime}
be an invariant tubular neighborhood of
M
contained in
U
.
Let
\phi\colon\Omega\to[0,1]
be a
C^{N}
-smooth
bump function supported on
U
and equal to
1
on
U^{\prime}
.
On replacing
\phi
by
\frac{1}{2}(\phi+\phi\circ\tau)
we may assume that
\phi
is
\tau
-invariant. Let
\lambda

be the
C^{N}
-norm of
\phi
.

As equivariant Morse functions on
M
are dense, there exists a function
f^{\prime}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
such
that
||f-f^{\prime}||_{C^{N}(M)}<\Pi_{N}^{-1}\kappa_{N}^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\varepsilon
.

Define

F^{\prime}=F+\phi(\pi^{-1}(f-f^{\prime})).

Then
F^{\prime}|_{M}=f^{\prime}
is Morse on
M
. By construction,
F^{\prime}
is invariant and
C^{N}
-smooth. Moreover,

||F-F^{\prime}||_{C^{N}(\Omega)}=||\phi(\pi^{-1}(f-f^{\prime}))||_{C^{N}(\overline{U})}\leq\Pi_{N}||\phi||_{C^{N}(\overline{U})}||\pi^{-1}(f-f^{\prime})||_{C^{N}(\overline{U})}\leq\\
\Pi_{N}\lambda_{N}||\pi^{-1}(f-f^{\prime})||_{C^{N}(\overline{U})}\leq\Pi_{N}\kappa_{N}\lambda_{N}||f-f^{\prime}||_{C^{N}(M)}<\varepsilon.

That is, near each function
F\in C^{N}_{\tau}(M)
, we can find a function
F^{\prime}
, arbitrarily close to
F
in the
C^{N}
-norm,
such that
F^{\prime}|_{M}
is Morse.
∎

Lemma 8.19
.

The set
\mathcal{M}_{3}\subset C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
of functions satisfying
(EEM-3)
is open-dense.

Proof.

As
M
is closed, a function without critical points on
M
has in its
C^{N}
-neighborhood only
functions without critical points on
M
. This shows openness.

For density, suppose
F\in C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and choose a function
F_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cap\mathcal{M}_{2}
such that
||F-F_{1}||<\varepsilon/2
. This is possibly by Lemmata
8.17
and
8.18
. We aim
to push all critical points of
F_{1}
off the submanifold
M
. If
F_{1}
has no such critical points, we have already found a function
in
C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
satisfying
(EEM-3)
. If not, as critical points of
F_{1}
are Morse, they are isolated. In particular,
there is a finite number
p
of critical points of
F_{1}
on
M
. Choose such a critical point
u
.
Let
U
be an equivariant neighborhood of
u
on which
\tau
acts linearly. We may assume that there are coordinates

x_{1},\dots,w_{s}
on
U
satisfying
(EML-1)
,
(EML-2)
, and
(EML-3)
. Shrink
U
to ensure that
F|_{M}
has no
critical points on
U
other than
u
.

We assume that such neighborhoods corresponding to different
critical points are pairwise disjoint. Let
U^{\prime}
be a neighborhood of
u
such that
\overline{U^{\prime}}\subset U
. Choose a bump function
\phi\colon\Omega\to[0,1]
that is supported on
U
and equal to
1
on
U^{\prime}
. Let
\lambda_{N}
be the
C^{N}
-norm of
\phi
.

Given that
n>r
, for
\eta>0
, we define a function

(8.20)

F^{\prime}_{1}=F_{1}+\phi\varepsilon\eta w_{1}.

Lemma 8.21
.

If
\eta
is sufficiently small, then:

•

||F^{\prime}_{1}-F_{1}||_{C^{N}}<\varepsilon/2p
;

•

F^{\prime}_{1}
has no critical points on
U\cap M
.

Proof.

The first item is straightforward, we need to show that
||\eta\phi w_{1}||_{C^{N}(U)}<1/p
. This can be done by estimating the
C^{N}
-norm
of
w_{1}
(which is finite) and using the finiteness of the norm of
\phi
.

For the second part, we note that
F^{\prime}_{1}
has no critical points on
U^{\prime}\cap M
regardless of
\eta
(as long as it is positive).
In fact, on
U^{\prime}
we have
\phi\equiv 1
, so
F^{\prime}_{1}=F_{1}+\varepsilon\eta w_{1}
. As
u
is an isolated critical point of
F_{1}|_{M}
,
at each point of
U^{\prime}|_{M}
except at
u
, the derivative of
F_{1}|_{M}
is not zero. The derivative of
F_{1}^{\prime}
with respect to
w_{1}

is not zero at
u
(it can be zero at some points of
U^{\prime}|_{M}
). That is,
F^{\prime}_{1}
has no critical points on
U^{\prime}|_{M}
.

On
(U\setminus U^{\prime})|_{M}
the function
F_{1}
has no critical points, hence its derivative is bounded from below by a constant. Denote it
\alpha>0
. Next, let
\beta
be the upper bound on the derivative of
\phi\cdot w_{1}
on
\overline{U\setminus U^{\prime}}|_{M}
.
If
\varepsilon\eta<\frac{\alpha}{\beta}
, then
F_{1}+\varepsilon\eta\phi w_{1}
has non-vanishing derivative
on
U\setminus U^{\prime}
.
∎

Given Lemma
8.21
, we continue with the proof of Lemma
8.19
.
The replacement (
8.20
) can be done near all critical points independently.
The resulting function
\widetilde{F}\in C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)

satisfies
||\widetilde{F}-F||_{C^{N}(\Omega)}<\varepsilon
and
\widetilde{F}
has no critical points on
M
, that is, it satisfies
(EEM-3)
.
This means, that if
\mathcal{M}_{3}
denotes the subset of functions satisfying
(EEM-3)
, then it is dense (openness is clear).
∎

Finishing the proof of Theorem
8.16
.

We have defined three subspaces
\mathcal{M}_{1}
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{3}
of functions of
C^{N}_{\tau}(\Omega)
satisfying respectively
(EEM-1)
,

(EEM-2)
, and
(EEM-3)
. We have showed that each of the three spaces is open-dense. Their intersection consists
of Morse functions.
∎

8.4.
Equivariant cobordisms of involutive links

In this section we apply equivariant embedded Morse theory to study cobordisms of involutive links.
Let
\Omega=S^{3}\times[0,1]
and
\tau\colon\Omega\to\Omega
be a smooth involution fixing
S^{3}\times\{t\}

for all
t\in[0,1]
. That is, we choose the standard involution of
S^{3}\times[0,1]
, by rotation about a fixed axis in
S^{3}
.
We will often use the fact that
\Omega^{\tau}=\operatorname{Fix}\tau
is a 2-dimensional manifold whose intersection with
S^{3}\times\{0\}
and
S^{3}\times\{1\}
is a circle.

Consider an equivariant link cobordism
M\subset S^{3}\times[0,1]
, that is, a smooth oriented two-dimensional surface fixed by
\tau
. We make the following assumption.

Condition 8.22
.

The action of
\tau\colon M\to M
has no isolated fixed points, and
\tau
is not the identity on any connected component of
M
.

This assumption is justified by the following result.

Lemma 8.23
.

Suppose
z_{0}
is an isolated fixed point of the involution
\tau\colon M\to M
. Then the action of
\tau
on the connected
component
M_{0}
of
M
containing
z_{0}
is orientation-preserving.

Proof.

Let
N=M^{\tau}
be the fixed set. By the assumption

z_{0}\in N
.
As
\tau(z_{0})=z_{0}
,
\tau
acts on
T_{z_{0}}M
linearly. Since
\tau
acts smoothly,
(T_{z_{0}}M)^{\tau}
is the tangent space
T_{z_{0}}N
.
The point
z_{0}
is an isolated fixed point, therefore
\tau\colon T_{z_{0}}M\to T_{z_{0}}M
is a linear involution of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
only
0
as the fixed point. Hence,
\tau
is the symmetry about
0
, so it preserves the orientation of
T_{z_{0}}M
. Hence, it
preserves the orientation of the whole connected component of
M
containing
z_{0}
.
∎

Perturb
F
to be an embedded equivariant Morse function on
\Omega
with respect to
M
. As the original function did not have critical points on
\Omega
, its perturbation can be chosen not to have such points either.
In the notation of Subsection
8.3
we have
m=2
,
k=2
,
n=2
and
r=1
.
The embedded Equivariant Morse Lemma (Theorem
8.15
) gives the following possibilities for critical points on
M
.

•

Critical points outside
M\cap\Omega^{\tau}
. The local form is
\pm x_{1}^{2}\pm x_{2}^{2}+w_{1}
. Depending on signs we have:

–

A pair of births with the local form
x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+w_{1}
;

–

A pair of saddle points with the local form
-x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+w_{1}
;

–

A pair of deaths with the local form
-x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}+w_{1}
.

•

A critical point in
M\cap\Omega^{\tau}
has local form
\pm x_{1}^{2}\pm y_{1}^{2}+w_{1}
. Depending on signs, we distinguish:

–

An invariant birth with local form
x_{1}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+w_{1}
, creating an invariant circle on which
\tau
acts by reflection;

–

Invariant saddles with local forms either
-x_{1}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+w_{1}
or
x_{1}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}+w_{1}
. Replacing
F
by
-F
and
w_{1}
by
-w_{1}

swaps the two local forms;

–

An invariant death with local form
-x_{1}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}+w_{1}
.

It is worth recalling that
x_{i},y_{i}
are the coordinates on
M
, while
v_{j},w_{j}
are the coordinates normal to
M
. The
\tau
-action
changes the sign of the
x_{i},v_{j}
coordinates, and fixes the
y_{i},w_{j}
coordinates.

The change of the topology of the link
M\cap F^{-1}(t)
near the critical point can be presented as a change of the link diagram:
a birth should create an unknotted circle or a pair of unknotted circles etc. In order to describe this change rigorously, we need to find
a projection of a link that is compatible with the change of topology, for example, the circle created during the birth should not be mapped to a segment. In Theorem
8.31
below, we prove that this is indeed the case. In particular, we show that under some precise genericity conditions, any critical point induces an equivariant Morse move, which we are now going to define.

birth
death
saddle

Figure 8.1.
Critical points outside the symmetry axis

birth
death
saddle

Figure 8.2.
Critical points on the symmetry axis

Definition 8.24
.

A transformation of an equivariant link depicted on Figure
8.1
or Figure
8.2
is called an
equivariant Morse move
. The moves on Figure
8.1
are called
outer
or
off-axis
moves, while the moves
on Figure
8.2
are called
inner
or
on-axis
moves.

Our study is now divided into steps. First, we specify local coordinates. This step does not require the function to be Morse. Next,
in Subsection
8.4.2
, we define a local compression of
M
, and show that a Morse critical point. In Subsection
8.4.3
,
we prove Theorem
1.2
about the movies associated with a cobordism. Finally, in Subsection
8.4.4
, we show
that good projections (see Definition
8.27
) exist.

8.4.1.
Local coordinates and good projections

We now start the discussion of local changes of the diagram after passing through a critical point. This analysis will culminate in
Theorem
8.28
, which is the key step in proving Theorem
8.31
.
We begin with choosing coordinates.
Suppose a critical point
u_{0}\in M

of
F|_{M}
occurs at a level set
t_{0}
.
Set
\varepsilon>0
, which we will eventually adjust to be sufficiently small.
Choose an arc
\omega\in F^{-1}[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]\cap\Omega^{\tau}
, missing
M
and transverse to the
level sets of
F
. Such arc is constructed by picking first a point in
\Omega^{\tau}\setminus M
at the level set
F^{-1}(t_{0})
and
extending it in a normal direction to the level set. We think
of this arc as a choice of the point at infinity for the level set
F
.

We let
S_{t}\cong S^{3}
denote
F^{-1}(t)
, and
R_{t}=S_{t}\setminus\omega
. Then,
R_{t}
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for all
t\in[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]
. Moreover, these diffeomorphisms can be chosen by a fixed stereographic projection from
\omega\cap S_{t}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Hence,
the identification of
R_{t}
with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
depends smoothly on
t
.
Set
X_{t}=M\cap R_{t}
, with
X_{t_{0}}
being the singular level set. Near
u_{0}
, we choose coordinates
(x_{1},y_{1})
on
M
.
If
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
, we require that
\tau(x_{1},y_{1})=(-x_{1},y_{1})
.
The next result shows a local form of
f
. Note that we
do not
assume that
u_{0}
is a Morse critical point of
F|_{M}
.

Lemma 8.25
.

The coordinate system on
M
can be completed to a local coordinate system
(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},w_{1})
near
u_{0}
on
\Omega

in such a way that
F
in these coordinates is equal to
F(u_{0})+f(x_{1},y_{1})+w_{1}
, where in the local coordinates,

f=F|_{M}
. Moreover, if
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
,
then
\tau(v_{1},w_{1})=(-v_{1},w_{1})
.

Proof of Lemma
8.25
.

The proof is similar for cases
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
and
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
. We give the proof in the case
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
, which
Extend
(x_{1},y_{1})
to equivariant coordinates
(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},w_{1})
near
u_{0}
on
\Omega
in such a way that
M=\{v_{1}=w_{1}=0\}
,
and
\tau(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},w_{1})=(-x_{1},y_{1},-v_{1},w_{1})
.
By Lemma
8.5

\frac{\partial F}{\partial v_{1}}(u_{0})=0
, and as
F|_{M}
has critical point at
u_{0}
, we have

\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{1}}(u_{0})=\frac{\partial F}{\partial y_{1}}(u_{0})=0
. As
DF(u_{0})\neq 0
,
we must have
\frac{\partial F}{\partial w_{1}}(u_{0})\neq 0
.
Then, the change of variables
(x_{1},y_{1},u_{1},w_{1})\mapsto(x_{1},y_{1},u_{1},F(x_{1},y_{1},u_{1},w_{1})-F(x_{1},y_{1},0,0))
is a local equivariant diffeomorphism. Applying this, we arrive at the situation where

(8.26)

F(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},w_{1})=w_{1}+f(x_{1},y_{1}),

where
f(x_{1},y_{1}):=F(x_{1},y_{1},0,0)
is smooth and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant.
∎

symmetry plane
\Omega^{\tau}
singular level set
R=R_{t_{0}}
y_{1}
x_{1}
w_{1}
M
symmetry axis
y_{1}
x_{1}

Figure 8.3.
The surface
M
(left) and its intersection with the singular level set (right). We omit the
v_{1}
–coordinate on both pictures.

Choose a neighborhood
U
of
u_{0}
such that the coordinates are specified. We assume that the arc
\omega_{t}
misses
t
.
As
w_{1}
is transverse to the level sets of
F
, the coordinates
x_{1},y_{1},v_{1}
provide a coordinate system on
U\cap R_{t}

for each
t\in[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]
. We extend these coordinates to the whole of
R_{t}
arbitrarily, but
if
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
, we require that

\tau(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1})=(-x_{1},y_{1},-v_{1})
, see Figure
8.3
.

In these coordinates, we can specify what we mean by a good projection. Here, by a projection, we mean a smooth equivariant fibration
\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with fibers
\mathbb{R}
. In general, we will consider projections that are
C^{1}
-small perturbations of linear projections.

Definition 8.27
.

A projection
\pi\colon R_{t_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
good
if it satisfies the following properties.

(P-1)

If
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
, then
\pi(u_{0})\notin\mathcal{L}
;

(P-2)

The vectors
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}
have linearly independent images under
D\pi(0,0,0)
.

(P-3)

The only points on
X_{t_{0}}
mapped to
\pi(U\cap R_{t_{0}})
are in
X_{t_{0}}\cap(U\cap R_{t_{0}})
.

We defer the proof that good projections exist until Subsection
8.4.4
.

8.4.2.
Local compression of
M
and equivariant Morse moves

Suppose
u_{0}
is a critical point of
F|_{M}
, but not of
F
itself. Let
\pi
be a good projection. Define the surface

M^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]=\{(\pi(u),F(u))\colon u\in M\cap F^{-1}[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]\}.

We refer to
M^{\prime}
as the
compressed surface
and the map
(\pi,F)
as the
compression map
.
While
M^{\prime}
might be singular, by items
(P-2)
,
(P-3)
of Definition
8.27
, there exists a neighborhood
U^{\prime}

of
(0,0,F(u_{0}))
such that
M^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime}
is smooth. Indeed,
(P-2)
indicates that a small neighborhood of
u_{0}
in
M
is mapped
diffeomorphically onto its image, and
(P-3)
prohibits existence of multiple points in
U^{\prime}
. Write
U^{\prime\prime}
for
U^{\prime}\cup\tau U^{\prime}
.
This allows us to handle the situation, where
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
, so
\tau u_{0}
is another critical point. Notice that if
\pi
is a good
projection for
u_{0}
, by symmetry, it is also a good projection for
\tau u_{0}
.

By construction,
M^{\prime}\cap F^{-1}(t)
for
t\in[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]
is the diagram of the link. Perturb the compression map
away from the preimage of
U^{\prime\prime}
in such a way that
M^{\prime}\cap F^{-1}(t_{0})
is a regular link diagram (in the sense of Definition
3.19
). This is possible using the techinques from the proof of Theorem
3.21
applied to the curve

\mathcal{S}:=(M\setminus(\pi,F)^{-1}(U^{\prime\prime}))\cap R_{t_{0}}

and then extending the perturbation to other level sets. On shrinking
\varepsilon
if necessary, we may assume that
M^{\prime}\cap F^{-1}(t)

is a regular link diagram away from
U^{\prime\prime}
for all
t\in[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]
. That is, the only change of the diagram
occurs in
U^{\prime\prime}
. This change depends on the exact properties of the function
f
.

Theorem 8.28
.

Suppose
\pi\colon R\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a good projection. Let
L_{t}^{0}=(M\cap U^{\prime}\cap F^{-1}(t)
,
D^{0}_{t}=\pi_{t}(L_{t})
, where
t\in[t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon]
. Suppose
f(x_{1},y_{1})
is Morse.

•

if
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
, then
D_{t_{0}+\varepsilon}^{0}
differs from
D_{t_{0}-\varepsilon}^{0}
by a pair of births, saddle moves or births (depending on the index), see Figure
8.1
.

•

if
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
, then the change is as in Figure
8.2
. More precisely:

–

if
u_{0}
is a birth, then
D^{0}_{t}=\emptyset
for
t<t_{0}
. For
t>t_{0}
,
D^{0}_{t}
is a circle intersecting the symmetry axis
at two points;

–

if
u_{0}
is a death, then the situation is the same as for birth but with time reversed;

–

if
u_{0}
is a saddle point, for
t\neq t_{0}
,
D^{0}_{t}
is a union of two arcs.

Proof.

The change of the diagram is controlled by the function
f
in
U^{\prime\prime}
. If
f
has local maximum at
u_{0}
(and at
\tau u_{0}
if
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
), then the change creates a circle. For
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
, the circle is given locally by
x_{1}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}=t-t_{0}
, so it intersects
the symmetry axis at two points. The same argument holds if
f
has local maximum.

If
f
has a saddle point, the change of the link is by performing a saddle move. Note that in the case
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
, the hyperbolas

x_{1}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}=\pm(t-t_{0})
intersect the axis
\mathcal{L}=\{x_{1}=0\}
at zero or two points depending on the sign of
\pm(t-t_{0})
.
∎

8.4.3.
Movies

Definition 8.29
.

A
movie
(more precisely, an
equivariant movie
) is a sequence
D_{i}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
of equivariant link diagrams, together with a sequence
\mathscr{S}_{i}
of terms, each of which is either an equivariant Morse move, equivariant Reidemeister move, or equivariant plane isotopy, from
D_{i}
to
D_{i+1}
.

Associated to an equivariant movie, there is an equivariant cobordism as follows. Each move
\mathscr{S}_{i}
specifies a cobordism from
D_{i}
to
D_{i+1}
in
S^{3}\times[0,1]
in an apparent way (e.g. equivariant Reidemeister moves by their trace, etc.). For convenience, say the cobordisms built from each
\mathscr{S}_{i}
this way are product cobordisms near
S^{3}\times\{0\}
and
S^{3}\times\{1\}
. The cobordisms from each
\mathscr{S}_{i}
may be stacked together to obtain a composite
D_{0}\to D_{n}
.

Definition 8.30
.

The cobordism associated to a movie as above is called the
trace
of the (equivariant) movie.

It is evident from the construction that the equivariant isotopy class of the trace of a movie is well-defined, although the trace itself is not well-defined (it depends on choosing a diffeomorphism
[0,n]\to[0,1]
).

We combine the proofs of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
8.28
to obtain the following statement, proving
Theorem
1.2
from the introduction.

Theorem 8.31
.

Suppose there exists an equivariant cobordism
\Sigma
between two involutive links
L_{0},L_{1}\subset S^{3}
, where
L_{0},L_{1}
are in general position with underlying transvergent diagrams
D_{0},D_{1}
. Assume that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
on
\Sigma
has no isolated fixed points. Then there is a movie
\mathcal{M}
from
D_{0}
to
D_{1}
whose trace is equivariantly isotopic to
\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let
M\subset S^{3}\times[0,1]
be an equivariant cobordism between
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
. Let
F\colon S^{3}\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
be the projection
onto the second factor. Assume that
F
is an equivariant embedded Morse function (see Definition
8.14
). If it is not the case, we perturb
F
to
F^{\prime}
. Such perturbation does not create any critical points of
S^{3}\times[0,1]
. In particular, by stability of Morse functions
(see
[
GG73
, Theorem III.2.2]
, but the proof applies in the equivariant setting), there is an equivariant diffeomorphism of
S^{3}\times[0,1]
taking
F
to
F^{\prime}
. Such a diffeomorphism can be thought of as an equivariant perturbation of
M
. From now on, we assume that

F|_{M}
is equivariant Morse.

If
F|_{M}
has no critical points at all, we conclude that
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
are equivariantly isotopic, and the statement follows directly from Theorem
1.1
. If
F|_{M}
has more than one critical points, we can split the cobordisms into pieces, when each piece has precisely one critical point of
F
. Therefore, it is enough to consider only the situation when
F|_{M}
has precisely one critical point. We assume the critical value is
t_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
. The discussion below deals with the case of the critical point on the axis, while the critical point off the axis can be done in a similar manner.

Choose
\omega_{1/2}\in(S^{3}\times\{1/2\})^{\tau}
disjoint from
M
,
where we recall that the superscript denotes the set of fixed points of
\tau
.
Set
R=(S^{3}\times\{1/2\})\setminus\{\omega_{1/2}\}
, and let
\pi\colon R\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a good projection. Extend
\omega_{t}
to a path of points disjoint from
M
as above, and
\pi
to the projection from
(S^{3}\times\{t\})\setminus\omega_{t}
, where
t\in[1/2-\varepsilon,1/2+\varepsilon]
.

Let
L_{t}
be the link
M\cap(S^{3}\times\{t\})
(it is singular at
t=1/2
). Finally, let
D_{t}
be the diagram of
L_{t}
,
D_{t}=\pi_{t}(L_{t})
.
Note that fixing an equivariant parameterization
\Phi\colon\Sigma\to S^{3}\times[0,1]
of
M
, where
\Sigma
is an abstract surface
endowed with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action, gives us an equivariant parameterization
\widetilde{\phi}_{t}\colon\mathcal{S}_{t}\to S^{3}\times\{t\}
of
L_{t}
for all
t\neq\frac{1}{2}
.
On choosing an appropriate good projection, by Theorem
8.28
, we may find
\varepsilon>0
such that
for
t\in[\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon]
, the change of the diagrams is a single equivariant Morse move.

For
t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon]
,
\mathcal{S}_{t}=\mathcal{S}_{0}
, so
\widetilde{\phi}_{t}
can be regarded as a map from
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. That is, this is a path of maps from
\mathcal{S}_{0}
to
S^{3}
. Note that for
t=0,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon
, the map
\widetilde{\phi}_{t}
is regular. On perturbing the path
\phi_{t}:=\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}_{t}

as in Theorem
1.1
, we may construct a regular path
\phi^{\prime}_{t}
equal to
\phi_{t}
at the end points and as close as we please
to the original path. That is,
\phi_{t}
induces a movie consisting of
Reidemeister moves and Morse moves. Note that sufficiently perturbation preserves isotopy class.
That is, with
\widetilde{\phi^{\prime}}_{t}\colon\mathcal{S}_{0}\to S^{3}

being the lift, we note that the trace

\{(\widetilde{\phi}_{t}(x),t)\subset S^{3}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon]\colon x\in\mathcal{S}_{0},\ t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon]\}

is equivariantly isotopic to
M\cap S^{3}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon]
.
This shows two things at once.

•

The links
L_{0}
and
L_{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
are connected by a movie consisting of equivariant Reidemeister moves and the I-move;

•

The trace of this movie is equivariantly isotopic to
M\cap S^{3}\times[0,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon]
.

An analogous discussion can be done for
t\in[\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon,1]
.
Combining this with the part for
t\in[\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon]
, we obtain the statement of the theorem.
∎

8.4.4.
Existence of good projections

The good projection of Definition
8.27
will be obtained by perturbing
the following
canonical projection
:

\pi_{\phi}(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1})=(\cos(\phi)x_{1}+\sin(\phi)v_{1},y_{1}),

where
\phi
is a parameter, and we require that
\phi
is not an integer multiple of
\frac{\pi}{2}
.
We begin with
(P-1)
, which we prove for
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
.

Lemma 8.32
.

There are two parameters
\phi\in S^{1}
for which
\pi_{\phi}
takes
u_{0}
to
\mathcal{L}
. That is, for all but two parameters
\phi
,

(P-1)
is satisfied.

Proof.

Suppose
u_{0}=(u_{x},u_{y},u_{v})
. Write
(u_{x},u_{v})=r_{u}(\cos\phi_{u},\sin\phi_{u})
. As
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
,
r_{0}>0
. The parameters such that
\pi_{\phi}

takes
u_{0}
to
(0,u_{y})\in\mathcal{L}
are
\phi_{u}\pm\pi
.
∎

The remaining part of the proof assumes
u_{0}\in M^{\tau}
. The case
u_{0}\notin M^{\tau}
can be easily adapted.

Example 8.33
.

Suppose
M\cap R_{t_{0}}
contains a circle
C
parameterized by
\tau\mapsto(0,r_{1}(\tau)\cos\tau,r_{2}(\tau)\sin\tau)
, where
r_{1},r_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
are smooth

2\pi
-periodic functions. Then, for any
\phi
,
\pi_{\phi}(C)
contains
(0,0)
. That is,
(P-3)
is not satisfied. Compare Figure
8.4
.

\pi

Figure 8.4.
Failure to regularity. Each linear equivariant projection takes the circle to the interval containing the image of the center.

Lemma 8.34
.

The conditions in Definition
8.27
can be satisfied after an arbitrary small perturbation of
\pi_{\phi}
.

Proof.

Condition
(P-2)
is satisfied for all map
\pi_{\phi}
with
\sin(2\phi)\neq 0
without even a need of perturbation.
Choose an equivariant bump function
\theta\colon R_{t_{0}}\to[0,1]
,
with supported on
U\cap R_{t_{0}}
and equal to
1
on a smaller neighborhood of
(0,0,0)\in R_{t_{0}}
.
Consider an equivariant map
\Xi\colon R_{t_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\Xi(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},\sigma,\phi)=(\cos(\phi)x_{1}+\sin(\phi)v_{1},y_{1}+\sigma(1-\theta)x_{1}^{2},\sigma,\phi).

If for some
\phi,\sigma
, the preimage
\Xi^{-1}(0,0,\sigma,\phi)
does not contain any point of
X
outside
U\cap R_{t_{0}}
(and
\cos(\phi)\neq 0
), then we replace the coordinate system on
R_{t_{0}}
by an automorphism
(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1})\mapsto(x_{1},y_{1}+\sigma(1-\theta)x_{1}^{2},v_{1})
and
(P-3)
is satisfied for this particular value of
\phi,\sigma
. Note that the derivative of this automorphism at
(0,0,0)
is the identity, hence replacing the coordinate system does not affect
(P-2)
. Therefore, we need to find such
\phi,\sigma
.

Suppose towards contradiction that there exists a compact set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{\phi=\frac{k}{2}\pi\}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, of positive 2-dimensional Lebesgue measure such for
(\phi,\sigma)\in A
, the preimage
\Xi^{-1}(0,0,\phi,\sigma)
contains a point of
X\times\{(\phi,\sigma)\}
outside
(U\cap R_{t_{0}})\times\{(\phi,\sigma)\}
. Define
\widetilde{X}\subset(R_{t_{0}}\setminus U)\times A
as the intersection of
(R_{t_{0}}\setminus U)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the preimage of
(0,0)\times A
.
As
\Xi\colon\widetilde{X}\to(0,0)\times A
is onto, by coarea formula (see e.g.
[
Fed69
, Theorem 3.2.11]
), the subset
\widetilde{X}

has positive 2-dimensional Hausdorff measure.

Now, consider the projection of
\widetilde{X}
onto
X
. We claim the fibers of the projection have at most two points.
To see this, suppose the points
(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},\sigma,\phi)
and
(x_{1},y_{1},v_{1},\sigma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
both belong to
\widetilde{X}
. Then,

\displaystyle y_{1}+\sigma(1-\theta)x_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=y_{1}+\sigma^{\prime}(1-\theta)x_{1}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle\cos(\phi)x_{1}+\sin(\phi)v_{1}

\displaystyle=\cos(\phi^{\prime})x_{1}+\sin(\phi^{\prime})v_{1}=0.

As the points are outside
U
,
\theta=0
. Hence
\sigma x_{1}^{2}=\sigma^{\prime}x_{1}^{2}
. If
x_{1}=0
then
y_{1}=0

and
\sin(\phi)v_{1}=\sin(\phi^{\prime})v_{1}=0
. As we excluded
\phi=\frac{k}{2}\pi
from
A
,
\sin(\phi),\sin(\phi^{\prime})\neq 0
, so
v_{1}=0
. But then
(x_{1},y_{1},w_{1})=(0,0,0)\in U
, contradicting that these points are away from
U
.

Therefore,
x_{1}\neq 0
. Then,
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
. Now let
\vec{a}=(\cos\phi,\sin\phi)
,
\vec{b}=(\cos\phi^{\prime},\sin\phi^{\prime})
,
\vec{c}=(x_{1},v_{1})
. The second displayed equation reads
(\vec{a}-\vec{b})\perp\vec{c}
. With
\vec{c}
and
\vec{a}
known, we see that

\vec{b}
belongs to an affine line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. But
||\vec{b}||=1
, so
\vec{b}
belongs to an intersection of a circle and a line, yielding
at most two possibilities for
\phi^{\prime}
, one being
\phi
.

Given that the projection of
\widetilde{X}
onto
X
has at most
2
points in each fiber, and that the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
X

is one, we invoke the coarea formula once again to deduce that the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
\widetilde{X}
is finite. But this is impossible, since the two-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
\widetilde{X}
was shown to be positive.
∎

9.
Movie moves

We continue the study of cobordisms of involutive links started in Section
8
. Our goal is to explain possible movie moves
for 1-parameter families. The main result is Theorem
9.9
below, echoing Theorem
1.4
from the introduction.

9.1.
Genericity conditions for cobordisms

To fix the notation, suppose
L_{0},L_{1}
are involutive links, parameterized as
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}\colon\mathcal{S}_{0}\to S^{3}
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}\colon\mathcal{S}_{1}\to S^{3}
,
where
\mathcal{S}_{0},\mathcal{S}_{1}
are two closed
1
-dimensional manifold with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action admitting at most two fixed points on each connected component.
Suppose
\Sigma
is a compact oriented surface whose boundary is
\mathcal{S}_{0}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{0}
endowed with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action
\tau\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
, such that the fixed point set,
\operatorname{Fix}\tau
, intersects each boundary component
of
\Sigma
at at most two points. Assume that
\Phi_{s}
,
s\in[0,1]
is a family of smooth equivariant maps from
\Sigma
to
S^{3}\times[0,1]

such that
\Phi_{s}
extends
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\phi_{1}}
, i.e. it takes
\mathcal{S}_{0}
to
L_{0}\subset S^{3}\times\{0\}
and
\mathcal{S}_{1}
to
L_{1}\subset S^{3}\times\{1\}
. Recall that we assume that the action of
\tau
on
S^{3}\times[0,1]
is standard.

For each
s
,
\Phi_{s}(\Sigma)
is an equivariant cobordism between
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
. As such, under genericity conditions, which we will spell out in a moment, it introduces a sequence of Reidemeister moves, I-moves and Morse moves between strongly invertible diagrams of
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
.
Suppose these genericity conditions hold for
s=0
and
s=1
. We ask, how differ the sequence of Reidemeister moves, I-moves and Morse moves for
s=0
and
s=1
.

Genericity conditions are obtained by summarizing the discussion in Subsections
6.4
,
6.6
and
8.4
.
Before passing to a formal definition, we recall the main ideas.

In Subsection
8.4
, our main assumption was
that the composition
H_{s}=F\circ\Phi_{s}
, where
F
is the projection
F\colon S^{3}\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
, is Morse.
For the critical points of
H_{s}
there was a list of conditions, we imposed
(P-1)
,
(P-2)
, and
(P-3)
,
to hold for a projection
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
used to define the Morse moves, see Subsection
8.4.1
.
These conditions guaranteed that at critical points of
H_{s}
, the
local change is via the equivariant Morse moves; compare Subsection
8.4.2
.

For non-critical values of
H_{s}
, there are two types of conditions. We imposed conditions for transversality at infinity in Subsection
6.6
. Finally, for the parts of diagrams in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we required that the suitably defined path
\phi_{s}
is regular in the sense of
Definition
6.1
.

To formalize the discussion,
set
\mathcal{S}_{ts}=H_{s}^{-1}(t)
, and let

\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
be the embedding
\Phi_{s}
restricted to
\mathcal{S}_{ts}
. If
t
is a non-critical level set of
H_{s}
, then
\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
is
a link diagram.

With
\mathcal{S}_{ts}^{0}=\{u\in\mathcal{S}_{ts}\colon\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}(u)\neq\infty\}
, we set
\phi_{ts}\colon\mathcal{S}_{ts}^{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
to be the diagram. Note that
if
\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
actually hits infinity, the space
\mathcal{S}_{ts}^{0}
is not compact.
If
(t,s)
vary in a neighborhood of a point
(t_{0},s_{0})
and all
\mathcal{S}_{ts}^{0}
in that neighborhood are equivariantly diffeomorphic,
we simply write
\mathcal{S}^{0}
instead of
\mathcal{S}_{ts}^{0}
.

We are now ready to specify regularity conditions. Throughout the list,
s\in[0,1]
is fixed. Also, the points
t_{i}^{\infty}
,

t^{M}_{j}
,
u_{i}^{M}
,
w_{i}^{M}
and
t_{i}^{M}
depend on
s
. Even their number is not fixed for
s\in[0,1]
.

(CS-1)

For any
s\in[0,1]
, the path
t\mapsto\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
is regular at infinity in the sense of Definition
6.6
.
For any
s
, we specify the points

0<t_{1}^{\infty}<\dots<t_{r}^{\infty}<1
, for which
\Phi_{s}
hits the point at infinity.

(CS-2)

We require that each point
t_{i}^{\infty}
is a non-critical value of the map
H_{s}
.

(CS-3)

We assume that for any
t_{i}^{\infty}
,
\phi_{t_{i}^{\infty}s}
is a regular map in the sense of Definition
2.13
;

(CS-4)

Let
t_{1}^{M},\dots,t_{r^{\prime}}^{M}
be the set of critical values of
H_{s}
. Set
K^{M}_{i}=\Phi_{s}(H_{s}^{-1}(t_{i}^{\infty}))
. We require that the diagram
D_{i}^{M}
obtained as
\pi(K_{i}^{M})
is regular except at the images of the orbit of critical points;

(CS-5)

To each critical value of
H_{s}
corresponds a unique orbit of critical points
u_{i}^{M}\in\Sigma
;

(CS-6)

If
u_{i}^{M}
is a critical point of
H_{s}
, and
u_{i}^{M}
is not fixed by the
\tau
-action, then
\pi(u_{i})
is not fixed
by the
\tau
-action, that is
(P-1)
is satisfied;

(CS-7)

For any critical point
u_{i}^{M}
of
H_{s}
, there are local coordinates
x_{1},y_{1}
on
\Sigma
, (if
u_{i}^{M}\in\operatorname{Fix}\tau
, then
\tau

fixes
y_{1}
and multiplies
x_{1}
by
-1
), such that
(P-2)
is satisfied;

(CS-8)

For any critical point
u_{i}^{M}
of
H_{s}
, write
z_{i}^{M}=\Phi_{s}(u_{i}^{M})\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\{t_{i}^{M}\}
and
w_{i}^{M}(s)=\pi(z_{i}^{M}(s))
. Then,
the preimage
\pi^{-1}(w_{i}^{M})
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\{t_{i}^{M}\}
consists only of the orbit of
z_{i}^{M}
;

(CS-9)

If
u_{i}^{M}
is a critical point of
H_{s}
, and
(CS-7)
is satisfied, then the compressed surface

\Sigma^{\prime}:=\{(\pi\Phi_{s}(u),H_{s}(u))\colon u\in\Sigma\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}

is smooth near the image
v_{i}^{M}
of
u_{i}^{M}
(the map
u\mapsto(\pi\Phi_{s}(u),H_{s}(u))
takes
\Sigma
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
).
The projection
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
restricts to
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and
u_{i}^{M}
is a critical point of it. We require it to be Morse.

(CS-10)

If
I
is an interval in
[0,1]
not containing any of
t_{i}^{M}
and
t_{i}^{\infty}
, then the path
I\ni t\mapsto\phi_{ts}
is regular in the sense of Definition
6.1
.

We comment on the above conditions. Item
(CS-8)
is an analog of
(P-3)
. We could require that
H_{s}
is Morse,
and then deduce
(CS-9)
from that condition. Instead, we require the projection
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
to restrict
to a Morse function in
(CS-9)
. This leads to a simpler description of possible singularities: we can invoke a result of
[
BB25
]

in that case.

We conclude the subsection with a statement that follows from previous discussions, but the explicit statement will be helpful for
the later statement of Theorem
9.9
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Suppose
L_{0},L_{1}
are two involutive links in
S^{3}\times\{0\}
and
S^{3}\times\{1\}
, respectively. Let
\Sigma
be a surface with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action whose boundary is divided into two parts (each part being a union of circles).

Let
\mathcal{G}(\Sigma)
be the space of smooth equivariant maps from
\Sigma\to S^{3}\times[0,1]
that take part of the boundary of
\Sigma
to
L_{0}

and the other part to
L_{1}
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{0}(\Sigma)
be the space of maps satisfying
(CS-1)
–
(CS-10)
(where we ignore
parameter
s
).

Then
\mathcal{G}^{0}(\Sigma)
is residual in
\mathcal{G}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The proof is done on a case-by-case analysis. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i}^{1}
the space of maps violating condition (CS-
i
).
We will show that the complement of each
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i}^{1}
is residual. In most cases we will invoke previously proved results. Sometimes,
the results from previous sections do not literally prove residuality of the complement, but rather the arguments will carry over.

For
(CS-1)
, that is
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{1}_{1}
, we use the arguments of Theorem
6.7
, where a path fails to be regular if it hits
\mathcal{I}^{2}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{3}
, and this union is essentially
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{1}_{1}
. The only technical difference is that in Subsection
6.6
, we considered families of maps from
\mathcal{S}
, that is, maps from
\mathcal{S}\times[0,1]
to
\mathcal{I}
. Here, the source is
\Sigma
, usually not a product. However, the argument is valid in the present situation.

For
(CS-2)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{1}_{2}
, coincidence of critical values of
H
and hitting a point at infinity is given by conditions that there are points
x,y\in\Sigma
such that
\Phi_{s}(x)=\{\infty\}\times\{t_{i}^{\infty}\}
,
\Phi_{s}(y)\in S^{3}\times\{t_{i}^{\infty}\}
,
DH_{s}(y)=0
. A straightforward
argument involving equivariant jet transversality (this time, the source is the surface) leads to showing that
\mathcal{G}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{1}_{2}
is residual.

Failure of regularity of
\phi_{t_{i}^{\infty}}
is of codimension 1; we have a family
\phi_{t_{i}^{\infty}}
parameterized by a finite set of
indices. At each index, we use the arguments of Theorem
3.21
. This takes care of
(CS-3)
. An
analogous argument works for
(CS-4)
.

Item
(CS-6)
is handled in Lemma
8.32
.
Items
(CS-7)
and
(CS-8)
are dealt with as in the proof of Lemma
8.34
.
Items
(CS-9)
and
(CS-5)
are consequence of density of regular Morse functions (i.e. Morse functions for which distinct critical points have different critical values), and item
(CS-10)

is proved in Theorem
6.2
.
∎

9.2.
Codimension 1 singularities of cobordisms

In Lemma
9.1
, we essentially proved that any equivariant cobordism can be perturbed to a cobordism satisfying items
(CS-1)
through
(CS-10)
. However, a generic one-parameter family can violate some conditions. In the following, we list all possible
singularities that may occur in such families. For the reader’s convenience we divide them into two groups. The first one corresponds to singularities occurring at distinct places. They correspond to coincidences of moves. The second group are more complex singularities corresponding
either to loops of Reidemeister moves, or to interactions between Morse moves and Reidemeister moves.

The first group of singularities is as follows.

(Coi-1)

The map
\phi_{t_{i}^{\infty}s}
of item
(CS-3)
is not regular;

(Coi-2)

The diagram
D_{i}^{M}(s)
of item
(CS-4)
has a single codimension 1 singularity;

(Coi-3)

There is an equality
t_{i}^{\infty}(s)=t_{i^{\prime}}^{M}(s)
for one pair of indices
i,i^{\prime}
;

(Coi-4)

There are two orbits of critical point of
H_{s}
at the same level set,
violating
(CS-5)
;

(Coi-5)

The family
\alpha\times I\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
of item
(CS-10)
has a member
s\in I
for which two separate codimension 1 singularities
occur.

All these items are of codimension 1 and may occur on a generic one-parameter family. These singularities correspond to:

•

coincidence of a Reidemeister move and an I-move or an S-move;

•

coincidence of an equivariant Morse move and a Reidemeister move;

•

coincidence of an I-move or an S-move and an equivariant Morse move;

•

coincidence of two equivariant Morse moves;

•

coincidence of two Reidemeister moves.

Note that there cannot be any coincidence of two I-moves, or S-moves because these would imply failure of regularity at infinity. Such a coincidence is in fact a Reidemeister move at infinity, described below.

The second group of singularities is more complex and leads to loops of Reidemeister moves, I-moves, S-moves and equivariant Morse moves.

(ML-1)

The map
\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
is not regular at infinity for some
(t,s)
, so
(CS-1)
is violated;

(ML-2)

The map
H_{s}
has a critical point at infinity, violating
(CS-2)
;

(ML-3)

A critical point
u_{i}^{M}
of
H_{s}
such that
u_{i}^{M}\neq\tau u_{i}^{M}
can be mapped by
\pi
to the symmetry axis (violating
(P-1)
and

(CS-6)
;

(ML-4)

There is a Morse critical point
u_{i}^{M}
for which condition
(P-2)
(equivalently
(CS-7)
)
is violated;

(ML-5)

There is a Morse critical point
u_{i}^{M}
for which condition
(P-3)
(equivalently
(CS-8)
) is violated;

(ML-6)

There is a critical point
u_{i}^{M}
such that
(P-2)
and
(P-3)
are satisfied, but
v_{i}^{M}
is not a Morse
critical point;

(ML-7)

With
I
as in
(CS-10)
, there is a point
t_{0}\in I
such that the family
t\mapsto\phi_{ts}
has a codimension 2 singularity at
t_{0}
.

We describe these singularities in separate subsections. Each of these singularities will lead to a loop. Several loops of Reidemeister moves
were described in Section
7
: that notion does not require further explanations. If we take into account Morse moves,
it might be worthwile to give a precise definition.

Definition 9.2
.

A
loop
of moves (i.e. Reidemeister moves, I-move, S-moves and Morse moves)
is a movie (see Definition
8.29
) such that
D_{0}
and
D_{n}
are the same link diagram.

We stress that the trace of a loop of moves need not be a product cobordism.

Subsections
9.3
– Subsection
9.9
will describe 39 loops of moves, which we refer to as
elementary loops
.
6
6

6

In the count of 39, we do not add collisions, i.e. loops of moves occurring at different places of the diagram.

9.3.
Case
(ML-1)
. Singularities at infinity

Suppose
\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
(with
t\in[0,1]
and
s
fixed)
is not regular at infinity at a point
t_{0}
, but we assume that
\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
is regular at infinity as a 2-parameter family
as in Definition
6.10
.
We assume that
t_{0}
is not a critical value of
\Phi_{s}
. In fact,
if it is, then either this is a critical point away from infinity, leading to a collision as in item
(Coi-3)
, or there is a singularity at infinity, in which case we land in Case
(ML-2)
.

As
t_{0}
is not a critical value of
H_{s}
, if we set
\mathcal{S}=H_{s}^{-1}(t_{0})
, then by implicit function theorem,
for
s^{\prime}
close to
s
and
t^{\prime}
close to
t_{0}

there is a map
\widetilde{\phi}_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{S}\to S^{3}
whose image is equal to the image of
\Phi_{s^{\prime}}
intersected with
H_{s^{\prime}}^{-1}(t^{\prime})
.
These maps depend on two parameters, so we are in the situation of Definition
6.6
. That is, the two-parameter
family of maps
\phi^{\iota}_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
(where
\iota
indicates that we are composing with inversion of
S^{3}
as in Subsection
6.6
)
acquires a codimension
1
singularity at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
; compare
(

Σ

-4)
of Definition
6.10
. These singularities were described in Subsection
6.4
.
More precisely, we can have.

•

Cusp on the axis at
0
;

•

Central tangency on the axis at
0
;

•

Boundary double point at
0
;

•

Central perpendicularity at
0
;

•

Central double point on the axis at
0
;

The family
\phi^{\iota}_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
is a versal deformation of the singularity by a deformation (leading to a Reidemeister move) combined with
shifting the singular point
0
up and down along the symmetry axis
\mathcal{L}
. After returning to the diagram
\phi_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
(without the inversion
\iota
), we see the following five loops of moves. They correspond to a versal deformation of the singularity after or before passing through
\infty
. These are:

(RI-1)

The R-1 move at infinity, see Figure
9.1
;

(RI-2)

The R-2 move at infinity, see Figure
9.2
;

(RI-3)

The M-1 move at infinity, see Figure
9.3
;

(RI-4)

The M-2 move at infinity, see Figure
9.4
;

(RI-5)

The M-3 move at infinity, see Figure
9.5
.

Figure 9.1.
R-1 move at infinity induces a loop. We have R-1 move, then a sequence of I- and S-moves, the inverse of R-1 move and the I-move again.

Figure 9.2.
R-2 move at infinity induces a loop. We have R-2 move, then a sequence of two S-moves, the inverse of R-2 move and then an isotopy.

Figure 9.3.
The loop of M-1 moves around infinity. We have M-1 move, then a sequence of I- and S-moves, the inverse of M-1 move and then a sequence of I- and S- moves.

Figure 9.4.
The loop of M-2 moves around infinity. The M-2 move is followed by S-moves and Reidemeister moves, then M-2 is undone and the S-move is applied.

Figure 9.5.
The loop of M-3 moves around infinity. The M-3 move is done. Then follows a sequence of S-moves and Reidemeister moves, then M-3 is undone. Finally, a sequence of S-moves and Reidemeister moves is done.

We pause for the moment to describe the loops involving S-moves, but not I-moves that is, corresponding to R-2, M-2, and M-3 moves at infinity.
As an S-move can be replaced by a series of equivariant Reidemeister moves (different link diagrams yield possibly different series),
the loops involving an S-move and Reidemeister moves can be replaced by loops of Reidemeister moves. As such, via Theorem
1.3
,
they can be decomposed as loops of Reidemeister moves given by Table
2
.

In other words, these three loops can be written as other loops. We include them because it might sometimes be easier to verify invariance
of a map associated to a cobordism (e.g. in Khovanov theory)
under a short local loop involving an S-move.

9.4.
Case
(ML-2)
. The Morse point at infinity.

This is the situation where there is a point
x\in\Sigma
that is mapped to
\infty\times\{t^{\infty}_{i}(s)\}
and it is a
critical point of
H_{s}
at the same time. A dimension counting argument does not prohibit this situation.
However, again by a dimension counting argument, we can show
that the non-Morse situation, or the situation such that
\pi^{\iota}=\pi\circ\iota
violates either
(P-2)
or
(P-3)
is of higher
codimension and does not occur on a 1-parameter family of maps
H_{s}
.

The discussion in Subsection
8.4
implies that the diagram of the link obtained via
\pi^{\iota}
changes as in Figure
8.2
. That is, we might have an equivariant birth at infinity, equivariant saddle at infinity and equivariant death.

With that type of critical points we associate a loop as in Subsection
9.3
. The loop consists of moving the critical point
along the axis but off the infinity point, or performing the Morse move.
Eventually, we have the following two loops.

(IM-1)

Birth/death at infinity, see Figure
9.6
;

(IM-2)

Saddle at infinity, see Figure
9.7
.

Said differently, for the movie at
s<s_{0}
, there is a saddle near infinity (to the south) while for
s>s_{0}
, part of the diagram is pulled through infinity (to the south), and a saddle happens near north infinity.

9.5.
Case
(ML-3)
. An orbit of handles hitting the axis

Suppose
u_{0}
is one of the
u_{i}^{M}
: a critical point of
H_{s}
such that
u_{0}\notin\Sigma^{\tau}
, but
z_{0}:=\pi\Phi_{s}(u_{0})
belongs to the axis

\mathcal{L}
. Set
u_{1}=\tau u_{0}
. It is a critical point of
H_{s}
by equivariance,
u_{1}\neq u_{0}
, but
\pi\Phi_{s}(u_{0})=\tau z_{0}=z_{0}
, so the two critical points occur on the axis.

If
u_{0}
is a birth (then
u_{1}
is a birth, too), after passing the critical level, two ellipses are created on the diagram (we assume that

(P-2)
is satisfied, and that
(P-3)
except that
(\pi\Phi_{s})^{-1}=\{u_{0},u_{1}\}
). In a generic situation, the axes of the
ellipses are transverse (it is enough that neither axis is parallel to or perpendicular to the symmetry axis
\mathcal{L}
), the non-generic leads
to a codimension 2 behavior. With these choices, the diagram above the level set looks locally as in the middle picture
in the bottom row of Figure
9.9
. The two ellipses are mapped to each other by a
\tau
-action.

A deformation moves the point
u_{0}
off-axis in both directions, leading to a situation above the critical level set as in the
row in Figure
9.9
, whereas before the critical level set, that part of the diagram is empty, see Figure
9.9
for the full loop of moves.

The situation is analogous if
u_{0},u_{1}
are saddles. The singular level set for each point is a pair of lines passing through
z_{0}
. In a generic situation, these lines are pairwise transverse, see Figure
9.8
. Changing the level set leads to a resolution of the intersection
points: each pair of lines is replaced by a pair of hyperbolas. This can be seen in the middle part of Figure
9.10
.

Moving
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
off the axis separates the two crossings of the singular level set. Resolutions lead to the loop as in Figure
9.10
.

9.6.
Case
(ML-4)
. Singular equivariant handles

This situation corresponds to a critical point
x_{0}\in\Sigma
for which
(P-2)
is violated.
That is to say, in the local coordinates
(u_{1},u_{2})
on
\Sigma
near the critical point
x_{0}
, the map
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
(where
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the projection) has a critical point. This situation, depicted in
[
CRS97
, Figure 14]
, was first studied by Giller
[
Gil82
]
.
Recall that the changes of the link diagram are given by images of level sets of
H_{s}
under the map
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}
.

The non-equivariant case.

Dimension counting arguments indicate that
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}
must have the mildest possible (codimension 1)
singularity as a map from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Low-codimension singularities of such maps have been classified, and the only case in codimension 1 is the fold, see
[
Whi55
]
and
[
AGZV12
, Chapter 1.5]
. That is, in
local coordinates, it is given by
(u_{1},u_{2})\mapsto(u_{1}^{2},u_{2})
.

The map
H_{s}
in the coordinates
(u_{1},u_{2})
cannot, in general, be put in the form
\pm u_{1}^{2}\pm u_{2}^{2}
, because the
u_{1},u_{2}
coordinates
have already been fixed by choosing the local form of the fold. Instead, we assume that
H_{s}(u_{1},u_{2})=B(u_{1},u_{2})+\dots
, where
B
is a quadratic part and the dots denote higher order terms (of order 3 and more). The quadratic part is nondegenerate.

Suppose
B
is definite, which corresponds to a birth or a death. The level sets of
B
are concentric ellipses. These ellipses
are mapped under the fold map to curves with precisely one transverse double point, see Figure
9.11
. Since

H_{s}
differs from
B
by terms of order
3
or more, the level sets of
H_{s}
have the same qualitative behavior as
B
: they are approximate
ellipses such that the fold map takes them to curves with precisely one transverse double point. We leave the details to the reader.

Figure 9.11.
Case
(ML-4)
. The dotted vertical line is the fold axis. The images of level sets of
H_{s}
under the fold map are circles with one double point.

Figure 9.12.
Case
(ML-4)
, continued. If the critical point is moved away from the fold line, the level sets for small parameter
are mapped into simple closed curves. A crossing occurs when the level sets cross the fold line.

Figure 9.13.
Case
(ML-4)
, saddle point. The dotted vertical line is the fold axis. One set of hyperbolas (corresponding
to the level sets of
H_{s}
below or above the critical point) are mapped bijectively by the fold map. The other set (corresponding
to the opposite level sets) acquire a double point after the fold map.

The movie is obtained as follows: changing the parameter
s
moves the critical point away from the fold line (see leftmost figure of Figure
9.12
). The level sets that do not intersect the fold line will be mapped to circles with no double point. At some moment, a cusp
is acquired and the double point is created. Moving the critical points from the left of the fold line to the right will lead
to a movie as in Figure
9.14
.

The situation of the saddle point is analogous. The analog of Figure
9.11
is
sketched in Figure
9.13
. It leads to a local loop in Figure
9.15
. This concludes the standard, non-equivariant situation.

This case is analogous to the non-equivariant case: the same movie is performed on both sides of the axis.
That is, we have the following two off-axis movies from case
(ML-4)
.
We refer to them as
singular Morse handles off-axis
:

(SM-1)

Singular birth off-axis, see Figure
9.14
;

(SM-2)

Singular saddle off-axis, see Figure
9.15
.

by an IR-1 move.

.

Equivariant case on-axis

We have a point
u_{0}\in\Sigma^{\tau}
that is mapped by
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}
to a point in
\mathcal{L}
. We choose
local coordinates
x_{1},y_{1}
near
u_{0}
, with
\tau x_{1}=-x_{1}
,
\tau y_{1}=y_{1}
.
By equivariant Morse Lemma (Theorem
8.11
),
we can adjust the coordinates equivariantly in such a way that
H_{s}(x_{1},y_{1})=H_{s}(u_{0})+\pm x_{1}^{2}+\pm y_{1}^{2}
.

Choose
x_{2},y_{2}
the coordinates in the target, with
\tau x_{2}=-x_{2}
,
\tau y_{2}=y_{2}
. The map
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}
can be given as

x_{2}=\rho_{x}(x_{1},y_{1})
,
y_{2}=\rho_{y}(x_{1},y_{1})
.
The maps
\rho_{x},\rho_{y}
must satisfy equivariance conditions, so that we can write the local Taylor expansion:

\begin{cases}\rho_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})&=x_{1}(a_{1}+a_{2}y_{1}+a_{31}x^{2}_{1}+a_{32}y^{2}_{1}+\dots)\\
\rho_{2}(x_{1},y_{1})&=b_{0}+b_{1}y_{1}+b_{21}x^{2}_{1}+b_{22}y^{2}_{1}+b_{31}y^{3}_{1}+b_{32}x^{2}_{1}y_{1}+\dots.\end{cases}

Above, the first number in the subscript denotes the order of the polynomial, while the second number, if present, enumerates
polynomials of the fixed order. We may assume that
b_{0}=0
.
The condition that
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}
has a critical point at
u_{0}
implies that
a_{1}b_{1}=0
.

Case 1.
b_{1}=0
.
We show that this case cannot occur. To see this, choose a nonzero vector
v\in T_{u_{0}}\Sigma^{\tau}
. As
\Phi_{s}
is an embedding,
v^{\prime}:=D\Phi_{s}(v)\in T_{u^{\prime}}(S^{3}\times[0,1])
is nonzero, where
u^{\prime}=\Phi_{s}(u_{0})
. Next,
F_{s}|_{\Phi_{s}(\Sigma)}
has a critical point at
u^{\prime}
, that is,
T_{u^{\prime}}(\Phi_{s}(\Sigma))\subset\ker DF_{s}
. Hence,
v^{\prime}\in\ker DF_{s}
. Let
t_{0}=F_{s}(u_{0})
. The fact that
v^{\prime}\in\ker DF_{s}
implies that
v^{\prime}\in T_{u^{\prime}}R_{t_{0}}
.

The map
\pi\colon R_{t_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a projection taking the set
R_{t_{0}}^{\tau}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
to
\mathcal{L}=(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{\tau}
. In particular, even
if
\pi
has one-dimensional kernel,
\pi
restricted to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is an isomorphism onto its image. As
v^{\prime}
is tangent to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
,
it follows that
D\pi(v^{\prime})\neq 0
. That is the original vector
v
gets mapped to a nonzero vector under
\pi\circ\Phi_{s}
.

To show that this contradicts
b_{1}=0
, we note that in the local coordinates we can take
v=(0,1)
. Then, the image of
v
under

DH_{s}
is
D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
acted on
(0,1)
, that is
(0,b_{1})
.

Case 2.
a_{1}=0
.
On counting dimensions, we may assume that
b_{1}a_{2}\neq 0
(the case
b_{1}a_{2}=0
has higher codimension singularity). Up to higher order terms, the map

(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
is given by
(x_{1},y_{1})\mapsto(a_{2}x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})
, that is, it contracts the line
y_{1}=0
to a single point.

If
D^{2}H_{s}
is definite, the level sets of
H_{s}
are approximate ellipses. Each circle intersects the line
\{y_{1}=0\}
at two points. The map

\pi\circ\Phi_{s}=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
will glue these points to create a (generically) transverse self-intersection. The diagram is stable, in
the sense that higher order terms of
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
will not change the picture.
The situation is very similar to the off-axis case: if the critical point is moved above the line
\{y_{1}=0\}
, small ellipses (level sets of
H_{s}
)
are disjoint from
\{y_{1}=0\}
, and so, they are mapped curves with no self-intersections. Once they touch
\{y_{1}=0\}
, they acquire a cusp and higher values of
H_{s}
lead to the circle with self-intersection.

The situation for saddles is analogous. We are led to the following two loops of moves.

(OM-1)

Singular birth on-axis, see Figure
9.17
;

(OM-2)

Singular saddle on-axis, see Figure
9.18
.

R-1
R-1
R-1
Morse
Morse

Figure 9.17.

R-1
R-1

Figure 9.18.
Twisted saddle on-axis. The arrows indicate the Morse moves (saddles). The row consists of Reidemeister moves.

9.7.
Case
(ML-5)
. Handles crossing the diagram

We discuss now the situation, where
(P-3)
holds, but
(P-2)
is violated. In the codimension 1 situation, we might have
the following cases.

(DM-1)

A birth off-axis over a smooth point of the diagram, see Figure
9.19
;

(DM-2)

A saddle off-axis over a smooth point of the diagram, see Figure
9.20
;

(DM-3)

A birth on-axis over a double point, see Figure
9.21
;

(DM-4)

A saddle on-axis over a double point, see Figure
9.22
.

Notice that neither the birth nor the saddle can possibly occur at the image of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
. Indeed, this would mean that two fixed point of the
\tau
action (the critical point and the point of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
) are mapped to the same point on
\mathcal{L}
. This would imply that they are the same point, violating the assumption that
\phi_{s}
is an embedding.

The deformation in each of the four cases consists of moving the image of the critical point off the diagram. In the equivariant setting,
the image of critical point is moved off the diagram along the axis.
