9.8.
Case
(ML-6)
. Non Morse critical point

This is the case when
(CS-9)
is violated. We recall that near
u_{i}^{M}(s)\in\Sigma
, the map

\Sigma\ni u\mapsto(\pi\Phi_{s}(u),H_{s}(u))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}

locally parameterizes a surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We denote it
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The projection
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
restricted to
\Sigma^{\prime}

has a non-Morse critical point
v_{i}^{M}
, which is the image of
u_{i}^{M}(s)
. For near parameters
s^{\prime}
, the non-Morse critical points
splits into Morse critical points. That is, on
v_{i}^{M}
we have a codimension 1 singularity of Morse functions.

Codimension 1 non-Morse singularities of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant functions were studied
in detail
in
[
BB25
]
; compare also
[
Aka07
,
Aka14
]
.
Each such singularity induces a loop of equivariant Morse moves.
Degeneracies are the following:

(NM-1)

birth+saddle off-axis, see Figure
9.23
;

(NM-2)

birth+saddle on-axis, see Figure
9.24
;

(NM-3)

collision of two saddles and a birth, see Figure
9.25
;

(NM-4)

collision of two births and a saddle, see Figure
9.26
.

Figure 9.23.
Birth and saddle off-axis can cancel.

Figure 9.24.
Birth and saddle on-axis can cancel.

Figure 9.25.

Figure 9.26.
Collision of critical points. A birth off the axis and a saddle on the axis can be traded for a birth on the axis.

9.9.
Case
(ML-7)
. Codimension two singularity of the diagram

Suppose
s_{0},t_{0}
are such that
t_{0}
is a non-critical point of
F\circ\Phi_{s_{0}}
. Set as usual,
\mathcal{S}=\Phi_{s_{0}}(\Sigma)\cap H_{s_{0}}^{-1}(t_{0})
.
Then, by implicit function theorem, there exists a family
\widetilde{\phi}_{s,t}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
of equivariant maps such that
the image of
\mathcal{S}
parameterizes
\Phi_{s}(\Sigma)\cap H_{s}^{-1}(t)
, and
(s,t)
are close to
(s_{0},t_{0})
. We let
\phi_{s,t}
be the diagrams
obtained by the projection.

It might happen, for finitely many pairs
(s_{0},t_{0})
that
\phi_{s_{0},t_{0}}\in\mathcal{F}^{2}
, in which case the whole family is an unfolding of a codimension 2 Reidemeister singularity.
This leads to one of the 18 loop (L-1)–(L-18) displayed in Table
2
.

9.10.
Summary. Loops and movie moves

The discussion of Section
9
can be summarized in the following
list of loops.

(MM-1)

Commuting two moves occurring at different places, e.g. equivariant Reidemeister move and a Morse move, that is:

•

commuting two Reidemeister moves;

•

commuting an I-move or an S-move and a Reidemeister move;

•

commuting an I-move or an S-move with an equivariant Morse move;

•

commuting two equivariant Morse moves;

•

commuting a Reidemeister move and an equivariant Morse move;

•

inserting and removing an I-move or an S-move.

(MM-2)

A loop of Reidemeister moves of Theorem
1.3
;

(MM-3)

A loop of Reidemeister moves around the point at infinity, leading to:

•

The R-1 loop at infinity, see Figure
9.1
;

•

The R-2 loop at infinity, see Figure
9.2
;
7
7

7

Italicized items can be deduced from loops of Reidemeister
moves, but in a non-local way.

•

The M-1 loop at infinity, see Figure
9.3
;

•

The M-2 loop at infinity, see Figure
9.4
;

•

The M-3 loop at infinity, see Figure
9.5
.

(MM-4)

A loop associated to a Morse move for a critical point hitting the axis;

•

A loop of a birth or a death on the symmetry line, see Figure
9.9
;

•

A loop of a saddle on the symmetry line, see Figure
9.10
.

(MM-5)

A loop of equivariant Morse moves around the point at infinity:

•

Birth/death at infinity, see Figure
9.6
;

•

Saddle at infinity, see Figure
9.7
.

(MM-6)

A loop related to a singular equivariant Morse move:

•

Singular birth off-axis, see Figure
9.14
;

•

Singular saddle off-axis, see Figure
9.15
.

•

Singular birth on-axis, see Figure
9.17
;

•

Singular saddle on-axis, see Figure
9.18
.

(MM-7)

A loop related to an equivariant Morse move over another point in the diagram:

•

A birth off-axis over a smooth point of the diagram, see Figure
9.19
;

•

A saddle off-axis over a smooth point of the diagram, see Figure
9.20
;

•

A birth on-axis over a double point, see Figure
9.21
;

•

A saddle on-axis over a double point, see Figure
9.22
.

(MM-8)

A loop of equivariant Morse moves related to a failure of Morse condition:

•

birth+saddle off-axis, see Figure
9.23
;

•

birth+saddle on-axis, see Figure
9.24
;

•

collision of two saddles and a birth, see Figure
9.25
;

•

collision of two births and a saddle, see Figure
9.26
.

Definition 9.3
.

A loop of the above list is called an
elementary loop
.

The way an elementary loop produces an elementary movie move is the following.
Suppose a loop consists of moves
\mathscr{S}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{S}_{n}
. We choose
i,j=1,\dots,n
as parameters with
i\leq j
. We consider
movies
\mathscr{M}_{1}
consisting of
\mathscr{S}_{i+1},\mathscr{S}_{i+2},\dots,\mathscr{S}_{j}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}
consisting of
\mathscr{S}_{i},\mathscr{S}_{i-1},\mathscr{S}_{i-2}\dots,\mathscr{S}_{1},\mathscr{S}_{n},\dots,\mathscr{S}_{j+1}
.
Informally, the loop is broken into two sets of movies. We say that
i,j
are
admissible
if the traces of
\mathscr{M}_{1}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}

are equivariantly isotopic.

Definition 9.4
.

An
elementary movie move
consists of exchanging
\mathscr{M}_{1}
with
\mathscr{M}_{2}
or
-\mathscr{M}_{1}
with
-\mathscr{M}_{2}
, where

\mathscr{M}_{1},\mathscr{M}_{2}
are two sets of movies coming from admissible
i,j
and one of the loop in the list
(MM-1)
–
(MM-8)
above.
Here the sign ‘
-
’ on the movie denotes the reverse movie.

Example 9.5
.

If
\mathscr{S}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{S}_{n}
is a loop of Reidemeister moves from Table
2
, then any
i,j
are admissible.

Example 9.6
.

Consider the loop associated with a collision as in Figure
9.26
. Here
\mathscr{S}_{1}
is a birth off-the axis,
\mathscr{S}_{2}
is a saddle on-axis,
\mathscr{S}_{3}
is the death on-axis. Only the choice
i=0
,
j=2
is possible: the trace of
\mathscr{S}_{1},\mathscr{S}_{2}
is the disk with two minima, while
the trace of
-\mathscr{S}_{3}
is a disk with the minimum. All other choices lead to non-isotopic traces.

Example 9.7
.

The loop L-1 from Table
2
induces an elementary movie move replacing (IR-1) by (IR-1) and (IR-2) moves. It also induces
an elementary movie move replacing one (IR-2) move by two (IR-1) moves.

Example 9.8
.

Consider a local loop described in Figure
9.15
. It induces an elementary movie move replacing (IR-1)+Saddle by another
type (IR-1)+Saddle;
see Figure
9.16
. However,
it does not induce an elementary movie move replacing two (IR-1) moves by two saddles, because the genera of traces are not the same.

We obtain now a theorem, expanding on Theorem
1.4

from the introduction.

Theorem 9.9
.

Suppose
L_{0},L_{1}
are two equivariant links in
S^{3}\times\{0\}
and
S^{3}\times\{1\}
, respectively. Suppose
\Phi_{s}\colon\Sigma\to S^{3}\times[0,1]
,
s\in[0,1]
is a family of equivariant cobordisms connecting
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
.
Suppose for
s=0,1
the maps
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}
satisfy conditions
(CS-1)
–
(CS-10)
.
The diagrams
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
of
L_{0}
and
L_{1}

can be connected by the movies associated with
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
. The two movies can be transformed one into other
by the elementary movie moves.

Remark 9.10
.

The assumptions that
(CS-1)
–
(CS-10)
are satisfied for
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
is not
restrictive: we can guarantee it by a small equivariant perturbation of maps, see Lemma
9.1
.

Proof of Theorem
9.9
.

Suppose
\Phi_{s}
is a path of equivariant embeddings of
\Sigma
to
S^{3}\times[0,1]
. For some values of
s
, conditions
(CS-1)
–
(CS-10)
can
be violated. These singularities have been described in Subsection
9.2
.
In particular, the moves
(MM-1)
are described as consequences of
(Coi-1)
–
(Coi-5)
.

In Subsection
9.2
, we described also other types of violations of conditions
(CS-1)
–
(CS-10)
,
namely the list
(ML-1)
–
(ML-7)
. Item
(ML-1)
of that list is handled in Subsection
9.3

and leads to
(MM-3)
. Indeed, say the map
\widetilde{\phi}_{ts}
, at
t=t_{0},s=s_{0}
, has a diagram that is not regular at infinity.
By the discussion in Subsection
9.3
, for the loop
\widetilde{\phi}_{t^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
, with
t^{\prime}=t_{0}+r\cos\gamma
,
s^{\prime}=s_{0}+r\sin\gamma
,
with
r
sufficiently small, then
\gamma\in[0,2\pi]
is one of the elementary loops corresponding to
(MM-3)
. It follows
from the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
7.7
indicates that the movies associated to paths

t\mapsto\widetilde{\phi}_{ts_{1}}
and
t\mapsto\widetilde{\phi}_{ts_{2}}
for
s_{1}=s_{0}-r
,
s_{2}=s_{0}+r
are related by an elementary movie move.

Analogously, the failure of regularity described in
(ML-2)
is discussed in Subsection
9.4
. It leads
to
(MM-5)
. Next, case
(ML-4)
(Subsection
9.6
) creates loops in
(MM-6)
.
Item
(ML-5)
is expanded on in Subsection
9.7
, leading to
(MM-7)
.
Item
(ML-6)
is shown in Subsection
9.8
to create loops
(MM-8)
. Finally,
the case of Reidemeister loops is discussed in Subsection
9.9
, and in the whole Section
7
.
The list of loops is given in item
(MM-2)
.
∎

Appendix A
Equivariant Malgrange theorem

The versality Theorem
4.16
requires Malgrange preparation
theorem in its proof. As alluded to in Subsection
4.2
, the equivariant version of versality theorem relies
on an equivariant version of Malgrange preparation theorem. That result is known to the experts but not commonly stated in the literature.
For the reader’s convenience, we include a short discussion. We begin with the classical, non-equivariant statement.

Theorem A.1

(Malgrange preparation theorem, see
[
GG73
, Theorem IV.3.6]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be two smooth, finite dimensional manifolds. Let
p\in X
,
q\in Y
and
f\in C^{\infty}(X,Y)
take
p
to
q
.
Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{q}
the rings of germs at
p
(respectively: at
q
) of
C^{\infty}
smooth functions from
X
(respectively: from
Y
)
to
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{p},\mathfrak{m}_{q}
be the maximal ideals.

A finitely generated
\mathcal{O}_{p}
-module
A
is finitely generated as an
\mathcal{O}_{q}
-module if and only if
A/\mathfrak{m}_{q}A
is a finite
dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space.

Here,
\mathcal{O}_{p}
is regarded as an
\mathcal{O}_{q}
module via the map
f
. That is,
A
is regarded as a
\mathcal{O}_{q}
-module, as well.

In the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant setting, the statement is analogous.

Theorem A.2

(Equivariant Malgrange preparation theorem)
.

Let
X,Y
be two smooth, finite dimensional manifolds acted on smoothly by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
p\in X^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
,
q\in Y^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

and
f\in C^{\infty}(X,Y)
be an equivariant function taking
p
to
q
.
Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{p},\mathcal{E}_{q}
the rings of germs at
p
(respectively: at
q
) of
C^{\infty}
smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant
functions from
X
(respectively: from
Y
)
to
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathfrak{e}_{p},\mathfrak{e}_{q}
be the maximal ideals.

A finitely generated
\mathcal{E}_{p}
-module
E
is finitely generated as an
\mathcal{E}_{q}
-module if and only if
E/\mathfrak{m}_{q}E
is a finite
dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space.

Proof.

It is enough to suppose that
X,Y
are vector spaces acted linearly by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Then,
\mathcal{E}_{p}
is the ring of germs of equivariant
functions.

Let
\pi_{x}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\pi_{y}\colon Y\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be the quotient maps by the group action. Let
p^{\prime}=\pi_{x}(p)
,
q^{\prime}=\pi_{y}(q)
. The equivariant map
f

induces a (germ of) smooth map
f^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

By a result of Whitney
[
Whi43
]
, there is a canonical isomorphism between
\mathcal{E}_{p}
and
\mathcal{O}_{p^{\prime}}
. Canonical means that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{p^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}}^{*}}

commutes. Then
E
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{O}_{p^{\prime}}
-module and it is finitely generated
\mathcal{O}_{q^{\prime}}
-module
if and only if
E/\mathfrak{m}_{q^{\prime}}E
is finitely dimensional.
∎
